Minimal and intrinsic topologies on monoids of elementary embeddings
J. de la Nuez Gonzalez, Zaniar Ghadernezhad, Paolo Marimon, Michael Pinsker

Abstract
To every ω \omega -categorical structure ℳ \mathcal{M} one can associate two spaces of symmetries which determine the structure up to first-order bi-interpretability: the topological group Aut ⁡ ( M ) \Aut(M) of its automorphisms and the topological monoid EEmb ⁡ ( M ) \EEmb(M) of its elementary embeddings, both equipped with the topology of pointwise convergence τ pw \tau_{\mathrm{pw}} . We investigate the relation of τ pw \tau_{\mathrm{pw}} to other topologies on these spaces: in particular, when τ pw \tau_{\mathrm{pw}} is minimal, i.e. does not admit any strictly coarser Hausdorff semigroup topology. A common method to prove minimality of τ pw \tau_{\mathrm{pw}} on EEmb ⁡ ( M ) \EEmb(M) is to show that it coincides with the algebraically defined semigroup Zariski topology τ Z \tau_{\mathrm{Z}} . We show that τ pw \tau_{\mathrm{pw}} differs from τ Z \tau_{\mathrm{Z}} on EEmb ⁡ ( M ) \EEmb(M) whenever Aut ⁡ ( M ) \Aut(M) has non-trivial centre. We then provide general conditions on the behaviour of algebraic closure on ℳ \mathcal{M} that imply minimality of τ pw \tau_{\mathrm{pw}} . These condition cover, for example, countable vector spaces and projective spaces over finite fields. Turning to Aut ⁡ ( M ) \Aut(M) , we describe the minimal T 1 T_{1} semigroup topologies on the automorphism groups of model-theoretically simple one-based ω \omega -categorical structures with weak elimination of imaginaries. We conclude by proving that the metric pointwise topology τ mp \tau_{\mathrm{mp}} is minimal, equals τ Z \tau_{\mathrm{Z}} , and is strictly coarser than τ pw \tau_{\mathrm{pw}} , on EEmb ⁡ ( M ) \EEmb(M) for the real and the rational Urysohn space and sphere.



Keywords:
Pointwise convergence topology, Zariski topology, elementary embedding, oligomorphic permutation group, saturated model, model-theoretic stability, model-theoretic simplicity, base monotonicity, modularity, lovely pairs, Urysohn spaces.

MSC classes:
20M20, 54H15, 22A15, 03C45, 03C15.

1
Introduction

1.1
Spaces of symmetries and their topologies

Given a countable structure
M
, there are two natural spaces of symmetries preserving its structure as given by first-order logic: its automorphism group
\Aut(M)
(if we require the symmetries to be bijections), and its monoid of elementary embeddings
\EEmb(M)
.
1
1

1

Elementary (self-)embeddings are injective maps on a structure preserving the truth of all first-order formulas.
From these objects, considered respectively as a permutation group and a transformation monoid, one can draw back conclusions about the original structure
M
. For example, we can tell whether or not
M
is
\omega
-categorical
, i.e. up to isomorphism the only countable model of its first-order theory; by the theorem of Ryll-Nardzewski, Engeler, and Svenonius (see the textbook
[
60
]
), this is the case if and only if
\Aut(M)
is
oligomorphic
, that is,
\Aut(M)\curvearrowright M^{n}
has only finitely many orbits for all
n\geqslant 1
in its diagonal action. In the
\omega
-categorical case, we can even measure exactly “how well”
\Aut(M)
and
\EEmb(M)
still know
M
: they determine
M
up to first-order interdefinability, i.e. the automorphism group/monoid of elementary embeddings of any other structure acts the same way if and only if that structure has a first-order definition without parameters in
M
and vice-versa.

One can then abstract further:
\Aut(M)
and
\EEmb(M)
are, respectively, a topological group and a topological monoid when equipped with the
topology
\tau_{\mathrm{pw}}
of pointwise convergence
, for which a subbasis of open sets is given by sets of all maps which send a particular point
x\in M
to another specific point
y\in M
. Moreover,
\Aut(M)
is
\tau_{\mathrm{pw}}
-closed in the group of all permutations of
M
and
\EEmb(M)
is
\tau_{\mathrm{pw}}
-closed in the full monoid
M^{M}
. This implies that
\tau_{\mathrm{pw}}
is Polish on these spaces, i.e., separable and induced by a complete metric. From these topological algebraic structures, we can still extract considerable information about
M
: in the
\omega
-categorical case both the topological group
\Aut(M)
and the topological monoid
\EEmb(M)
determine
M
up to first-order bi-interpretations, a more generous equivalence than the above-mentioned interdefinability (Coquand; see
[
3
]
, and
[
44
]
). To provide another example, famously from
\Aut(M)
we can tell whether or not
M
has a certain combinatorial property called the Ramsey property: this is the case if and only if the topological group
\Aut(M)
is extremely amenable, i.e. any continuous action of it on a compact Hausdorff space has a fixed point
[
37
]
.

This invites a further step of abstraction where we view these spaces of symmetries only as algebraic structures by forgetting the topology, giving rise to two natural questions:

Question 1.1
.

How much information about
M
is still encoded in the pure group
\Aut(M)
or the pure monoid
\EEmb(M)
?

Question 1.2
.

What is the relation of
\tau_{\mathrm{pw}}
to the other (Hausdorff/Polish) topologies compatible with the algebraic structure of
\Aut(M)
or
\EEmb(M)
?

1.2
The case of groups

Over the past four decades, a rich literature has emerged on these questions in the context of automorphism groups.
Regarding
1.1
, in the
\omega
-categorical case, we often have that the pointwise convergence topology is the same in all possible actions of
\Aut(M)
as a closed oligomorphic permutation group; this then implies that the algebraic structure of
\Aut(M)
encodes
M
up to bi-interpretability.
As exemplary references, we point to
[
24
,
61
]
for the notion of the small index property; to
[
55
]
for forall-exists-interpretations; and to
[
35
,
38
]
for ample generics. In fact, when
\Aut(M)
has ample generics, then
\tau_{\mathrm{pw}}
is even the unique Polish group topology on it (which is a stronger statement than the pointwise topology being the same in all actions); this then provides a complete simple answer to
1.2
(in the Polish case). The survey
[
45
]
contains many further references to notable works. Counterexamples to non-uniqueness of
\tau_{\mathrm{pw}}
amongst Polish group topologies, or even to
M
not being determined up to bi-interpretability by the group
\Aut(M)
, such as that given in
[
29
]
, are hard to construct; indeed, it is consistent with
\mathrm{ZF}
that
\tau_{\mathrm{pw}}
is always the unique Polish group topology on
\Aut(M)

[
44
]
(cf.
[
59
,
56
]
). Not only the topology
\tau_{\mathrm{pw}}
, but even the action
\Aut(M)\curvearrowright M
can be reconstructed from the pure group structure in many cases under the additional assumption that
M
has no algebraicity (i.e., for each finite
A\subseteq M
every element in
M\setminus A
has an infinite orbit under the action of the stabiliser of
A
)
[
55
]
.

Looking at
1.2
, in many interesting special circumstances
\tau_{\mathrm{pw}}
is such that there are no strictly coarser Hausdorff group topologies on
\Aut(M)
. Topological groups with this property are known as
minimal
, and they are intensively studied
[
23
]
.
Examples of minimal topological groups arising from automorphism groups of countable structures are
S_{\infty}:=\Aut(\mathbb{N};=)
by
[
32
]
, and more generally the automorphism groups of
\omega
-categorical stable structures
[
7
]
. The latter is a rich class of model-theoretically “tame” structures, including, for example, infinite-dimensional vector spaces and projective spaces over finite fields. Indeed, the automorphism groups of stable
\omega
-categorical structures are not only minimal but
totally minimal
: every continuous surjective homomorphism to a Hausdorff topological group is open.
In their survey of minimal topological groups, Dikranjan and Megrelishvili explicitly ask which oligomorphic permutation group are minimal

[
23
, Question 2.3]
.

On the positive side, from
[
34
]
we have some additional results under the model-theoretic weakening of stability known as simplicity: the automorphism groups of simple transitive
\omega
-categorical one-based structures with weak elimination of imaginaries are minimal
[
34
]
. This includes, for example, the automorphism group of a vector space of infinite dimension over a finite field with a symplectic or unitary bilinear form
[
18
]
. On the negative side, we can identify three potential
sources of non-minimality: non-transitivity, the presence of intervals of some form, and the presence of a metric structure. The first source can lead to failures of minimality by “localising” the topology of pointwise convergence to an orbit of
\Aut(M)
(
Example

3.22
).
From the the second source, an interval topology
\tau_{\mathrm{int}}
can be defined, for example on
\Aut(\mathbb{Q};<)
, with the basic open sets consisting of maps sending a particular point
x\in M
into a specific interval (rather than another point); in this example,
\tau_{\mathrm{int}}
is minimal and strictly coarser than
\tau_{\mathrm{pw}}

[
16
,
34
]
. The third source happens outside of the
\omega
-categorical setting, and allows to define a “metric pointwise topology”
\tau_{\mathrm{mp}}
coarser than
\tau_{\mathrm{pw}}
; an example is the real Urysohn sphere
[
62
]
.

1.3
The case of monoids

Let us now move to the context of monoids of elementary embeddings. With the notable exception of some early works by Lascar
[
44
,
43
]
, the study of the behaviour of
\tau_{\mathrm{pw}}
on
\EEmb(M)
(or other more general spaces of symmetries) is much younger: it was only initiated about 15 years ago
[
11
]
. Since then, a considerable amount of results has emerged on the topic, as recently surveyed in
[
46
]
. The motivation for this line of research is threefold. Firstly, studying monoids of elementary embeddings sheds light also on automorphism groups: this perspective can already be found in Lascar’s work
[
44
,
43
]
. Secondly, such study can help us develop tools for the general study of topological semigroups, as shown in the seminal paper
[
27
]
; compared to groups, substantially different methods to show, say, minimality are required, and results differ: for example, every monoid of elementary embeddings of an
\omega
-categorical structure has a Polish topology strictly finer than
\tau_{\mathrm{pw}}

[
28
]
. Finally, the study of
\EEmb(M)
often serves as an initial step to understanding even richer spaces of symmetries
[
13
]
such as endomorphism monoids (maps preserving positive quantifier-free formulas) and polymorphism clones (a generalization of endomorphisms monoids to higher arity functions), with the latter having important applications even in theoretical computer science
[
12
,
31
]
.

Moving from
\Aut(M)
to
\EEmb(M)
there are surprising differences in topological behaviour that we explore in this paper. At a first glance,
\EEmb(M)
may look extremely close to
\Aut(M)
in the contexts we study. In fact, for a saturated structure
M
(which includes
\omega
-categorical ones), it coincides with the
\tau_{\mathrm{pw}}
-closure of
\Aut(M)
in
M^{M}
. Yet, there is a fascinating tension between the weaker algebraic structure of a monoid (which destroys the possibility of several techniques coming from groups) and the fact that we have a much richer space of maps (making it potentially easier to see the first-order behaviour of
M
from algebraic structure).

Arguably, as mentioned in
[
46
]
,
\EEmb(M)
often behaves better than
\Aut(M)
: already Lascar gives an extremely satisfying answer to
1.1
for
\EEmb(M)
by proving that any isomorphism between the monoids of elementary embeddings of two countable saturated
G
-finite structures is continuous when restricted to their automorphism groups. In fact, it is even a topological isomorphism between the monoids as shown recently in
[
46
]
. No result to this level of generality is known for isomorphisms between automorphism groups. This result seems to be close to optimal since the non-
G
-finite counterexamples to topological reconstruction
[
29
]
lift to monoids
[
10
]
. As another example, by
[
52
, Theorem 3.2]
, if two
\omega
-categorical structures with no algebraicity have isomorphic monoids
of elementary self-embeddings, then this isomorphism is automatically a topological one; no such result is known for groups, with various special cases proved in work following
[
55
]
.

Another context where
\EEmb(M)
seems to behave better than
\Aut(M)
is that of minimality, which is the main topic of the present paper. From
[
52
]
, we know that
\tau_{\mathrm{pw}}
is minimal on the monoid of elementary embeddings of any
\omega
-categorical structure with no algebraicity. This already shows that standard sources of failure of minimality on
\Aut(M)
do not seem to work for
\EEmb(M)
, since both
(\mathbb{Q};<)
(where one can define an interval topology on
\Aut(M)
) and the random bipartite graph (where one can define a localized topology) have no algebraicity.

1.4
The Zariski topology

Not only
\tau_{\mathrm{pw}}
seems to be more easily minimal in the context of
\EEmb(M)
, but this can often be shown by proving that
\tau_{\mathrm{pw}}
has a purely algebraic description in terms of the
semigroup Zariski topology
, a topology that is known to be contained in every Hausdorff semigroup topology on a given semigroup
[
27
]
. In this topology
\tau_{\mathrm{Z}}
, introduced in
[
27
]
(for semigroups), closed sets are solution sets of equations over the semigroup in one free variable with parameters from the semigroup. Since it is defined in purely algebraic terms, any algebraic isomorphism between monoids is a homeomorphism of their Zariski topologies.
However,
\tau_{\mathrm{Z}}
is, a priori, not particularly well-behaved or expressive: it need not be Hausdorff or a semigroup topology. Indeed, on
\Aut(M)
,
\tau_{\mathrm{Z}}
is hyperconnected (and hence not Hausdorff) whenever
M
has no algebraicity
[
6
]
, and also the more intensively studied group Zariski topology
[
14
]
(where inversion may be used in the definition of closed sets) is often not a semigroup topology
[
34
]
(with notable exceptions including
(\mathbb{N};=)
and
(\mathbb{Q};<)
).

Contrary to the group case, as demonstrated by
[
52
]
, in many cases the Zariski and the pointwise topologies coincide in
\EEmb(M)
. This was already observed in
[
28
]
, where it was then asked whether there exists an
\omega
-categorical structure
M
at all where
\tau_{\mathrm{pw}}\neq\tau_{\mathrm{Z}}
on its endomorphism monoid
\End(M)
. The question can be answered affirmatively even for
\EEmb(M)
(which is an endomorphism monoid by adequately expanding the language of
M
) by the results of
[
10
]
, and in
[
52
]
another more natural and tangible example was provided; yet these two examples remain the only known ones up to this day. In fact, showing that
\tau_{\mathrm{pw}}=\tau_{\mathrm{Z}}
is the standard path to prove minimality of
\tau_{\mathrm{pw}}
on
\End(M)
. This approach was taken, in particular, in
[
27
,
28
,
53
]
, where then other methods are used to show that
\tau_{\mathrm{pw}}
is also maximal, and hence unique, among Polish semigroup topologies on various natural endomorphism monoids.

2
Results

We now present our main results; all relevant definitions can be found in Section
3
. We present our results in terms of permutation groups
G
acting on a countable set
\Omega
. To connect this with the previous discussion, we equip
\Omega^{\Omega}
with the topology of pointwise convergence
\tau_{\mathrm{pw}}
. Note that
G\curvearrowright\Omega
is closed in the set of all permutations with respect to
\tau_{\mathrm{pw}}
if and only if it equals
\Aut(M)
for some first-order structure
M
on
\Omega
. As mentioned above, the closure
\overline{G}
of
G
in
\Omega^{\Omega}
is precisely the monoid
\EEmb(M)
of elementary self-embeddings of
M
whenever
M
is saturated; in particular, this is the case whenever
M
is
\omega
-categorical. Throughout the paper we work with the assumption that
G\curvearrowright\Omega
has
locally finite algebraic closure
, by which we mean that for any finite
A\subseteq\Omega
there are only finitely many
b\in\Omega
with finite orbit under the action of the pointwise stabiliser of
A
,
G_{A}
. If all such
b
are elements of
A
for any
A\subseteq\Omega
, then we say there is
no algebraicity
. The automorphism groups of
\omega
-categorical structures have locally finite algebraic closure.

Our first theorem provides, for the first time, general conditions that imply
\tau_{\mathrm{pw}}\neq\tau_{\mathrm{Z}}
on monoids of elementary embeddings; cf.
Section

1.4
.

Theorem A

(
Theorem

4.8
)
.

Let
G\curvearrowright\Omega
be a closed permutation group with locally finite algebraic closure. Suppose that
G
has non-trivial centre. Let
S
be either
G
or
\overline{G}
. Then, the semigroup Zariski topology
\tau_{\mathrm{Z}}
on
S
is not Hausdorff. In particular, it is properly contained in
\tau_{\mathrm{pw}}
.

Several interesting structures have automorphism groups satisfying the above assumptions on
G
: countable vector spaces over finite fields (with bilinear or quadratic forms), and infinitely many disjoint copies of the complete graph
K_{n}
(which are essentially the source of the original counterexample of
[
52
]
).

A
tells us that the standard method to prove minimality of
\tau_{\mathrm{pw}}
on
\EEmb(M)
(by showing
\tau_{\mathrm{pw}}=\tau_{\mathrm{Z}}
) cannot work in many natural
\omega
-categorical structures with algebraicity (the latter is implied by a non-trivial center). We thus develop alternative techniques to establish minimality as long as the algebraic behaviour of a structure is sufficiently tame:

Theorem B

(
Theorem

7.19
)
.

Let
G\curvearrowright\Omega
be the automorphism group of a countable saturated structure with locally finite algebraic closure such that algebraic independence satisfies base monotonicity. Then,
\tau_{\mathrm{pw}}
is minimal amongst Hausdorff semigroup topologies on
\overline{G}
.

In this theorem, algebraic independence is a natural notion of independence on subsets of a saturated structure, which, for example, in infinite vector spaces over finite fields corresponds to linear independence. Base monotonicity is a natural condition on a notion of independence which, roughly, tells us that the algebraic behaviour of a structure is not more complex than that of a vector space.
B
subsumes the results of
[
52
]
since every
\omega
-categorical structure with no algebraicity is such that algebraic independence satisfies base monotonicity. Moreover, it yields minimality of
\tau_{\mathrm{pw}}
for the automorphism group of an infinite dimensional vector space over a finite field, in spite of
\tau_{\mathrm{pw}}\neq\tau_{\mathrm{Z}}
(by Theorem
A
).

B
applies to a wide range of cases. In particular,
G=\Aut(M)
also acts on any expansion
M^{\star}
of
M
by finitely many imaginary sorts (that is, quotients of
M
by
\Aut(M)
-invariant equivalence relations) with
\tau_{\mathrm{pw}}
giving rise to the same topological group (and the same topological monoid on
\overline{G}
); and when
M
is
\omega
-categorical, algebraic closure is locally finite also on the action of
G
on
M^{\star}
, meaning that
B
still applies. Often, one needs to move to these actions for algebraic independence to satisfy base monotonicity. From this,
B
applies to all (model-theoretically) simple one-based structures with very weak elimination of imaginaries (see
Remark

7.20
). Simple one-based structures
include
all smoothly approximable structures
[
18
]
, which contain all
\omega
-categorical
\omega
-stable structures
[
17
]
, and all simple finitely homogeneous structures
[
4
]
. The condition of very weak elimination of imaginaries roughly means that the behaviour of algebraic closure on imaginary elements can be described by only looking at finitely many imaginary sorts. We do not know any examples from the aforementioned classes that do not have this property (cf.
[
18
,
19
]
). Finally, algebraic independence easily satisfies base monotonicity also outside the context of model-theoretic simplicity. For example, our theorem applies to the monoids of elementary embeddings of countably infinite densely ordered vector spaces over finite fields.

Going back to the context of groups, we improve the main result of
[
34
]
(Theorem B therein) by describing the
T_{1}
semigroup topologies on
G
coarser than
\tau_{\mathrm{pw}}
under general conditions: firstly, we consider all
T_{1}
semigroup topologies rather than Hausdorff group topologies, and moreover we remove the assumption from
[
34
, Theorem B]
that points are algebraically closed in the non-transitive setting.

Theorem C

(
Theorem

6.8
)
.

Let
G\curvearrowright\Omega
be the automorphism group of a simple one-based countable saturated structure with weak elimination of imaginaries and locally finite algebraic closure. Then, every
T_{1}
semigroup topology on
G
coarser than
\tau_{\mathrm{pw}}
is a generalized pointwise topology. Moreover, if
G
is transitive, then
\tau_{\mathrm{pw}}
is minimal amongst
T_{1}
semigroup topologies on
G
.

We define generalised pointwise topologies in
Definition

3.26
. In the non-transitive context, minimality of
\tau_{\mathrm{pw}}
can fail: this is witnessed by the random bipartite graph. On top of our theorem applying to the aforementioned examples of simple one-based
\omega
-categorical structures with weak elimination of imaginaries, Theorem
6.8
works under more general assumptions which include, for example, simple (not one-based)
\omega
-categorical Hrushovski constructions
[
65
,
34
]
. Since the original argument in
[
34
]
heavily relied on taking conjugates of pointwise stabilizers, our proof follows a substantially different route, with the initial portion of the argument for
C
running in parallel to that for
B
.

We conclude by studying topologies on the semigroup of elementary embeddings of Urysohn spaces. Recall that that in the group case, the presence of a metric is a source of failure of minimality for
\tau_{\mathrm{pw}}
.
By adapting techniques from
[
52
]
to the metric setting, we show that under general conditions, the Zariski topology is a Hausdorff semigroup topology and hence minimal amongst all such topologies on the monoid of elementary embeddings; that it agrees with the metric pointwise topology
\tau_{\mathrm{mp}}
; and that it is strictly coarser than
\tau_{\mathrm{pw}}
. In particular, we obtain the following (for the mentioned spaces the isometric embeddings are precisely the elementary embeddings):

Theorem D

(
Theorem

8.24
)
.

Let
\mathcal{U}
be any of the following Urysohn spaces:

•

the rational Urysohn space;

•

the rational Urysohn sphere;

•

the real Urysohn space;

•

the real Urysohn sphere;

•

the rational ultrametric Urysohn space.

Then, the semigroup Zariski topology
\tau_{\mathrm{Z}}
on the space
\mathrm{IE}(\mathcal{U})
of isometric embeddings of
\mathcal{U}
is strictly coarser than
\tau_{\mathrm{pw}}
and
minimal amongst Hausdorff semigroup topologies on
\mathrm{IE}(\mathcal{U})
. Moreover,
\tau_{\mathrm{Z}}
corresponds to the metric pointwise topology
\tau_{\mathrm{mp}}
.

3
Preliminaries

Throughout this paper,
\Omega
denotes a countably infinite set. Groups and monoids in this paper will usually act on
\Omega
.

3.1
Spaces of symmetries

3.1.1
Infinite permutation groups

Given a group
G\curvearrowright\Omega
and
B\subseteq\Omega
, we write
G_{B}
for the
pointwise stabiliser
of
B
and
G_{\{B\}}
for its setwise stabiliser. We write
[\Omega]^{<\omega}
for the collection of finite subsets of
\Omega
.

A group
G\curvearrowright\Omega
acting on
\Omega
is called
oligomorphic
if
G\curvearrowright\Omega^{n}
has finitely many orbits in its diagonal action for each
n\in\mathbb{N}
. Given group action
G\curvearrowright\Omega
and some finite subset
B\subseteq\Omega
, we write
\dcl(B)
for all elements in
\Omega
which are fixed by the action of

G_{B}
. This is known as the
definable closure of

B
. Meanwhile, we write
\acl(B)
for the set of points of
\Omega
with finite orbit under the action of
G_{B}
. This is known as the
algebraic closure of

B
. The above are sometimes known as group-theoretic (or Galois) definable and algebraic closure to distinguish them from algebraic closure in the model-theoretic sense. We say that
G\curvearrowright\Omega
has
locally finite
algebraic closure if for every
B\in[\Omega]^{<\omega}
,
\acl(B)
is finite. Throughout most of this paper we work with a group
G\curvearrowright\Omega
with locally finite algebraic closure and we denote by
\mathfrak{X}
the set of finite algebraically closed subsets of
\Omega
. It is well-known (and easy to prove) that oligomorphic permutation groups have locally finite algebraic closure.

We also extend the definition of algebraic closure to infinite subsets of
\Omega
: we say that
X\subseteq\Omega
is
algebraically closed
if

X=\bigcup\{\acl(B)\ |\ B\subseteq X\text{ finite}\}.

Note that this definition of algebraic closure on infinite sets does not agree in general with group-theoretic algebraic closure (cf.
[
36
, Chapter 4.1]
).

Notation 3.1
.

Usually, for a tuple
\overline{a}:=(a_{1},\dots,a_{n})
of elements from
\Omega
, we write
\acl(\overline{a}):=\acl(\{a_{1},\dots a_{n}\})
for its algebraic closure (so, concatenation of elements means taking their union, as is customary in model theory). When we have tuples of elements
\overline{b}_{1},\dots,\overline{b}_{n}
from
\Omega
, we write
\acl(\overline{b}_{1}\dots\overline{b}_{n})
for

\acl\left(\bigcup_{i\leqslant n}\{c|c\in\overline{b}_{i}\}\right)\;.

Sometimes, in Section
4
to improve readability, we will include commas between the tuples of which we are taking the algebraic closure, thus writing
\acl(\overline{b}_{1},\dots,\overline{b}_{n})
for the above.

Given two finite tuples
\overline{c},\overline{c}^{\prime}
of elements of
\Omega
of the same length, we write
\overline{c}\equiv\overline{c}^{\prime}
to denote the fact that
\overline{c}
and
\overline{c}^{\prime}
belong to the same orbit under the diagonal action of
G
. For
C,C^{\prime}\in[\Omega]^{<\omega}
, we write
C\equiv C^{\prime}
to indicate that there are enumerations
\overline{c}
and
\overline{c}^{\prime}
of
C
and
C^{\prime}
respectively such that
\overline{c}\equiv\overline{c}^{\prime}
. For
D\in[\Omega]^{<\omega}
, we write
\overline{c}\equiv_{D}\overline{c}^{\prime}
as an abbreviation for
\overline{c}\overline{d}\equiv\overline{c}^{\prime}\overline{d}
, where
\overline{d}
is some enumeration of
D
. Similarly, for
D,C,C^{\prime}\in[\Omega]^{<\omega}
, we write
C\equiv_{D}C^{\prime}
if there exits enumerations
\overline{c}
and
\overline{c}^{\prime}
of
C
and
C^{\prime}
respectively such that
\overline{c}\equiv_{D}\overline{c}^{\prime}
.

We also record the following easy observation:

Observation 3.2
.

Let
G\curvearrowright\Omega
be such that for each
G
-orbit
\Delta
and each
a\in\Delta
,
\acl(a)\cap\Delta
is finite. The relationship
R(a,b):=a\in\acl(b)
is an equivalence relation among the points in any orbit
\Delta
of the action of
\Omega
.

Proof.

The properties of the closure easily imply the relation above is reflexive and transitive, and the latter amounts to the fact that if
a\in\acl(b)
, then
\acl(a)\subseteq\acl(b)
. For symmetry, we require that
a
and
b
are in the same orbit
\Delta
and the additional local finiteness assumption. Again, since
a\in\acl(b)
,
\acl(a)\subseteq\acl(b)
. However, since the size of
\acl(a)\cap\Delta
is preserved by the action of
G
,
|\acl(a)|=|\acl(b)|
. Hence,
\acl(a)\cap\Delta=\acl(b)\cap\Delta
, so that, in particular,
b\in\acl(a)
.
∎

3.1.2
The topology of pointwise convergence

Let
\Omega
be a set and consider the
full monoid

\Omega^{\Omega}
, where multiplication is given by composition of functions.
Given
C\in[\Omega]^{<\omega}
and a submonoid
S\subseteq\Omega^{\Omega}
we write
S_{C}
for the collection of elements of
s\in S
fixing
C
pointwise. For a partial function
\phi:A\to B
,
A,B\subset\Omega
, we write
S_{\phi}
for the set of elements of
s\in S
with
s_{\restriction A}=\phi
. Note that
\Omega^{\Omega}
is naturally endowed with the
topology of pointwise convergence

\tau_{\mathrm{pw}}
, which is just the product topology when
\Omega
is endowed with the discrete topology. With this topology, when
\Omega
is countable,
\Omega^{\Omega}
is a Polish topological semigroup. That is, it is separable and completely metrizable, and multiplication is continuous. Similarly, any closed submonoid of
\Omega^{\Omega}
endowed with the subspace topology is still a Polish topological semigroup, and we also denote this as the
topology of pointwise convergence

\tau_{\mathrm{pw}}
. It is easy to see that a basis
of open subsets of
\tau_{\mathrm{pw}}
for
S
is given by sets of the form
S_{\phi}
, for
\phi\in\mathscr{P}(S)
, where
\mathscr{P}(S)
denotes the set of restrictions elements of
S
to finite subsets from
\Omega
. Given a group of permuations on
\Omega
, we denote by
\overline{G}
the closure of
G
in
\Omega^{\Omega}
with respect to
\tau_{\mathrm{pw}}
. It is easy to see this is also a monoid.

3.2
Model-theoretic background

Essentially all groups and semigroups we will study arise as the automorphism groups and monoids of elementary embeddings of countable first-order structures. We expect some familiarity with basic concepts in model theory such as that of a first-order structure, types, and elementary embeddings. We refer the reader to
[
60
, Chapters 1-4]
.

We denote structures by
\mathcal{M}
or
\mathcal{M}^{\prime}
and their domains by
\Omega
or
\Omega^{\prime}
. We denote first-order theories by
T
and first-order languages by
\mathcal{L}
(possibly with indices). With the exception of Section
8
, we always work in a countable language. We write
\Aut(\mathcal{M})
for the automorphism group of
\mathcal{M}
,
\Emb(\mathcal{M})
for its monoid of embeddings,
\EEmb(\mathcal{M})
for its monoid of elementary embeddings, and
\End(\mathcal{M})
for its endomorphism monoid.

We say that a countable structure
\mathcal{M}
is
\bm{\omega}
-categorical
if its automorphism group
\Aut(\mathcal{M})
is oligomorphic. Given a structure
\mathcal{M}
and a cardinal
\kappa\leqslant|\Omega|
, we say that
\mathcal{M}
is
\kappa
-saturated if for all subsets
B\subseteq\mathcal{M}
with
|B|<\kappa
and all
n\in\mathbb{N}
,
\mathcal{M}
realises all
n
-types over
B
. We say that
\mathcal{M}
is
saturated
if it is
\kappa
-saturated for
\kappa=|\Omega|
. It is easy to see that countable saturated structures are such that
\overline{\Aut(\mathcal{M})}=\EEmb(\mathcal{M})
. Moreover, any two saturated models of the same complete theory of the same cardinality are isomorphic
[
60
, Lemma 5.2.8]
. All
\omega
-categorical structures are saturated. It is also well-known that a complete theory (possibly in an uncountable language) has a countable saturated model if and only if it is
small
, that is, it has only countably many
n
-types for each
n\in\mathbb{N}

[
60
, Lemma 4.3.9]
. Moreover, whenever
G\curvearrowright\Omega
is the automorphism group of a countable saturated structure
\mathcal{M}
, we can choose the theory of
\mathcal{M}
to be in a countable language. An example of a countable saturated structure that is not
\omega
-categorical but has locally finite algebraic closure is the disjoint union of countably many trees where every vertex has countably infinite branching (in the language of graphs).

We shall often work under the following assumption (and refer to it as

(
♣
)

‣
3.2
):

(
\clubsuit
)

G
is the automorphism group of a countable saturated structure
\mathcal{M}
with locally finite algebraic closure and universe
\Omega
(hence,
\overline{G}=\EEmb(\mathcal{M})
).

Definition 3.3
.

We say that a structure
\mathcal{M}
is
homogeneous
if any isomorphism between finitely generated substructures of
\mathcal{M}
can be extended to an automorphism of
\mathcal{M}
. Given a structure
\mathcal{M}
, we call the class
\mathcal{K}
of finitely generated substructures the
age
of
\mathcal{M}
.

Let
\mathcal{L}
be a countable language. A
Fraïssé class

\mathcal{K}
(in the language
\mathcal{L}
) is a class of finitely generated
\mathcal{L}
-structures which is closed under isomorphism and finitely generated substructures, and satisfying the following properties:

•

(joint embedding) for any
B_{0},B_{1}\in\mathcal{K}
there exists
C\in\mathcal{K}
and embeddings
g_{i}:B_{i}\to C
(for
i\in\{0,1\}
);

•

(amalgamation property) if
A,B_{0},B_{1}\in\mathcal{K}
and there exists embeddings
f_{i}:A\to B_{i}
for
i\in\{0,1\}
, then there exist
C\in\mathcal{K}
and embeddings
g_{i}:B_{i}\to C
(for
i\in\{0,1\}
) such that
g_{0}\circ f_{0}=g_{1}\circ f_{1}
.

Note that if we are working in a relational language (with no constants), finitely generated substructures are just substructures, and the amalgamation property implies the joint embedding property.

Fraïssé’s Theorem (see
[
60
, Theorem 4.4.4]
) states that
\mathcal{K}
is the age of a countable homogeneous structure
\mathcal{M}
if and only if it is a Fraïssé class. Moreover, given a Fraïssé class
\mathcal{K}
, the countable homogeneous structure with age
\mathcal{K}
is unique up to isomorphism, and we call it the
Fraïssé limit of
\mathcal{K}
.

Except for
Section

8
, we take homogeneous structures to be countable. We will use homogeneous structures mainly for the purpose of giving examples throughout this paper. In fact, it is easy to see that homogeneous structures in a finite relational language (possibly with constants) are
\omega
-categorical. We will call these
finitely homogeneous structures
. Standard examples of finitely homogeneous structures are the random graph (the Fraïssé limit of the class of finite graphs) and
(\mathbb{Q};<)
. An example of a homogeneous (and
\omega
-categorical) structure in a functional language is an infinite dimensional vector space over a finite field (in the language with scalar functions for the field elements). In
Section

8
, we give examples of homogeneous structures in infinite languages with the real and rational Urysohn space, whose ages are finite metric spaces with, respectively real and rational distances.

3.2.1
Imaginaries

Let
G\curvearrowright\Omega
and let
(\sim_{i})_{i\in I}
be the collection of all
G
-invariant equivalence relations on finite power of
\Omega
, indexed by some set
I
. In particular, for
(n_{i})_{i\in I}
a sequence of natural numbers, we take
\sim_{i}
to be a
G
-invariant equivalence relation on
\Omega^{n_{i}}
. Then,
G
acts naturally on

\Omega^{eq}:=\bigsqcup_{i\in I}\Omega^{n_{i}}/\sim_{i}\;.

We denote
\dcl^{eq}
and
\acl^{eq}
the notions of definable and algebraic closure arising from this action. We identify
\Omega
with
\Omega^{2}/=
. We call
\Omega
the
real sort
of the action of
G
and the other
\Omega^{n_{i}}/\sim_{i}

imaginary sorts
. We say that
G\curvearrowright\Omega
has
weak elimination of imaginaries
if for every
e\in\Omega^{eq}
there is some finite tuple
b
from
\Omega
such that
e\in\dcl^{eq}(b)
and
b\in\acl^{eq}(e)
.

A well-known consequence of the Ryll-Nardzewski theorem is that if
G\curvearrowright\Omega
is a closed oligomorphic permutation group, then algebraic closure is locally finite for
G\curvearrowright\Omega^{\star}
where
\Omega^{\star}
is the restriction of
\Omega^{eq}
to finitely many imaginary sorts.
2
2

2

This may not be the case when
G
is the automorphism group of a countable saturated structure with locally finite algebraic closure.
We say that
G\curvearrowright\Omega
has
very weak elimination of imaginaries
if one of its expansions by finitely many imaginary sorts does have weak elimination of imaginaries.

3.2.2
Independence relations

In this section, we introduce some basic terminology and facts regarding notions of independence. A lot of advances in model theory since the work of Shelah
[
57
]
come from the study of independence relations generalising the notion of linear independence in vector spaces or algebraic independence in algebraically closed fields. Given the ubiquity and variety of useful notions of independence that emerged in model theory
[
39
,
20
,
22
,
47
]
, modern works favour an axiomatic approach, where one lists the relevant axioms needed of a notion of independence. This is in particular the norm after the work of Adler
[
1
,
2
]
. Whilst the notions of independence we use are related to algebraic independence (in a sense that will be made clear soon), we shall also adopt an axiomatic approach in order to make the structure of our proofs more transparent and in the hope that our work may be further generalised. We will follow the notational conventions and style of presentation of
[
26
]
.

Definition 3.4
.

Let
G\curvearrowright\Omega
be a group acting on an infinite
set with locally finite algebraic closure.
An
independence relation

\mathop{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\textstyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\scriptstyle\kern 2.80048pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 1.89871pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 2.80048pt}{\scriptscriptstyle\kern 1.42882pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 0.96873pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 1.42882pt}}
is a ternary
G
-invariant relation on finite subsets of
\Omega
satisfying the axioms below. For
A,B,C\in[\Omega]^{<\omega}
, we write
A\mathop{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\textstyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\scriptstyle\kern 2.80048pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 1.89871pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 2.80048pt}{\scriptscriptstyle\kern 1.42882pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 0.96873pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 1.42882pt}}_{C}B
and read “
A
is independent from
B
over
C
”. We ask the following properties from an independence relation, for
A,B,C,D\in[\Omega]^{<\omega}
:

•

(symmetry) if
A\mathop{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\textstyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\scriptstyle\kern 2.80048pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 1.89871pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 2.80048pt}{\scriptscriptstyle\kern 1.42882pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 0.96873pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 1.42882pt}}_{C}B
, then
B\mathop{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\textstyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\scriptstyle\kern 2.80048pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 1.89871pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 2.80048pt}{\scriptscriptstyle\kern 1.42882pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 0.96873pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 1.42882pt}}_{C}A
;

•

(normality) if
A\mathop{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\textstyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\scriptstyle\kern 2.80048pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 1.89871pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 2.80048pt}{\scriptscriptstyle\kern 1.42882pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 0.96873pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 1.42882pt}}_{C}B
, then
A\mathop{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\textstyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\scriptstyle\kern 2.80048pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 1.89871pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 2.80048pt}{\scriptscriptstyle\kern 1.42882pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 0.96873pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 1.42882pt}}_{C}BC
;

•

(monotonicity) if
A\mathop{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\textstyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\scriptstyle\kern 2.80048pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 1.89871pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 2.80048pt}{\scriptscriptstyle\kern 1.42882pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 0.96873pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 1.42882pt}}_{C}BD
, then
A\mathop{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\textstyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\scriptstyle\kern 2.80048pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 1.89871pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 2.80048pt}{\scriptscriptstyle\kern 1.42882pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 0.96873pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 1.42882pt}}_{C}B
;

•

(transitivity) if
A\mathop{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\textstyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\scriptstyle\kern 2.80048pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 1.89871pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 2.80048pt}{\scriptscriptstyle\kern 1.42882pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 0.96873pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 1.42882pt}}_{C}B
and
A\mathop{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\textstyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\scriptstyle\kern 2.80048pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 1.89871pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 2.80048pt}{\scriptscriptstyle\kern 1.42882pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 0.96873pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 1.42882pt}}_{BC}D
, then
A\mathop{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\textstyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\scriptstyle\kern 2.80048pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 1.89871pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 2.80048pt}{\scriptscriptstyle\kern 1.42882pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 0.96873pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 1.42882pt}}_{C}BD
;

•

(full existence) there is
A^{\prime}\equiv_{C}A

such that
A^{\prime}\mathop{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\textstyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\scriptstyle\kern 2.80048pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 1.89871pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 2.80048pt}{\scriptscriptstyle\kern 1.42882pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 0.96873pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 1.42882pt}}_{C}B
;

•

(anti-reflexivity) for
a\in\Omega
, if
a\mathop{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\textstyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\scriptstyle\kern 2.80048pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 1.89871pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 2.80048pt}{\scriptscriptstyle\kern 1.42882pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 0.96873pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 1.42882pt}}_{C}a
, then
a\in\acl(C)
.

We write
A\mathop{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\textstyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\scriptstyle\kern 2.80048pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 1.89871pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 2.80048pt}{\scriptscriptstyle\kern 1.42882pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 0.96873pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 1.42882pt}}B
to abbreviate
A\mathop{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\textstyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\scriptstyle\kern 2.80048pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 1.89871pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 2.80048pt}{\scriptscriptstyle\kern 1.42882pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 0.96873pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 1.42882pt}}_{\emptyset}B
.

Unlike standard works in model theory, we consider independence relations restricted to finite subsets of a countable set. Usually, in model theory, independence relations are considered on sufficiently small subsets of a large and (sufficiently) saturated model

[
60
]
.

Fact 3.5

(
[
26
, Proposition 3.1.3]
)
.

Let
G\curvearrowright\Omega
be a group acting on a set with locally finite algebraic closure. Let
\mathop{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\textstyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\scriptstyle\kern 2.80048pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 1.89871pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 2.80048pt}{\scriptscriptstyle\kern 1.42882pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 0.96873pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 1.42882pt}}
be an independence relation. Then,
\mathop{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\textstyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\scriptstyle\kern 2.80048pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 1.89871pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 2.80048pt}{\scriptscriptstyle\kern 1.42882pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 0.96873pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 1.42882pt}}
satisfies the following additional properties for all
A,B,C\in[\Omega]^{<\omega}
:

•

(existence) we have
B\mathop{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\textstyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\scriptstyle\kern 2.80048pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 1.89871pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 2.80048pt}{\scriptscriptstyle\kern 1.42882pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 0.96873pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 1.42882pt}}_{B}A
and
A\mathop{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\textstyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\scriptstyle\kern 2.80048pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 1.89871pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 2.80048pt}{\scriptscriptstyle\kern 1.42882pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 0.96873pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 1.42882pt}}_{B}B
;

•

(strong closure)
A\mathop{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\textstyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\scriptstyle\kern 2.80048pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 1.89871pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 2.80048pt}{\scriptscriptstyle\kern 1.42882pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 0.96873pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 1.42882pt}}_{C}B
if and only if
\acl(A)\mathop{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\textstyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\scriptstyle\kern 2.80048pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 1.89871pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 2.80048pt}{\scriptscriptstyle\kern 1.42882pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 0.96873pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 1.42882pt}}_{\acl(C)}\acl(B)
.

Notation 3.6
.

Often, it will be convenient to apply our independence relation to finite tuples from
\Omega
, thus writing
\overline{a}\mathop{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\textstyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\scriptstyle\kern 2.80048pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 1.89871pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 2.80048pt}{\scriptscriptstyle\kern 1.42882pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 0.96873pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 1.42882pt}}_{\overline{c}}\overline{b}
to indicate that
A\mathop{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\textstyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\scriptstyle\kern 2.80048pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 1.89871pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 2.80048pt}{\scriptscriptstyle\kern 1.42882pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 0.96873pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 1.42882pt}}_{C}B
, where
A,B
and
C
are, respectively, the sets of elements enumerated by
\overline{a},\overline{b},\overline{c}
. We also note that for
\overline{a}
and
\overline{b}
finite tuples from
\Omega
, we write
\overline{a}\cap\overline{b}
for the set of elements of
\Omega
that belong to both
\overline{a}
and
\overline{b}
. We define similarly
\overline{a}\cup\overline{b}
and
\overline{a}\subseteq\overline{b}
.

A large class of structures to which our results apply are (model-theoretically) simple one-based countable saturated structures with locally finite algebraic closure and weak elimination of imaginaries. Simplicity is a combinatorial property of a theory which can be characterised as the existence of an independence relation satisfying a certain set of axioms (generalising the axioms of linear independence in vector spaces) on a sufficiently saturated model. A stronger property of a theory than simplicity is known as stability. Both stability and simplicity are extensively studied in model theory
[
51
,
5
,
40
]
. We refer the reader to
[
60
]
for the formal definitions of
stability
,
simplicity
, and
non-forking independence
, which gives a well-behaved notion of independence on arbitrary simple theories. Our proofs of Theorems
C
and
B
only rely on the existence of a notion of independence satisfying certain axioms, and such notions of independence exist for countable saturated models of simple theories with locally finite algebraic closure for which non-forking independence agrees with the following notion of algebraic independence:

Example 3.7
.

Let
G\curvearrowright\Omega
have locally finite algebraic closure. We define
algebraic independence

\mathop{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\textstyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\scriptstyle\kern 2.80048pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 1.89871pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 2.80048pt}{\scriptscriptstyle\kern 1.42882pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 0.96873pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 1.42882pt}}^{a}
as follows: for
A,B,C\in[\Omega]^{<\omega}

A\mathop{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\textstyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\scriptstyle\kern 2.80048pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 1.89871pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 2.80048pt}{\scriptscriptstyle\kern 1.42882pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 0.96873pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 1.42882pt}}^{a}_{C}B\text{ if and only if }\acl(AC)\cap\acl(BC)=\acl(C)\;.

It is well-known that
\mathop{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\textstyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\scriptstyle\kern 2.80048pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 1.89871pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 2.80048pt}{\scriptscriptstyle\kern 1.42882pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 0.96873pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 1.42882pt}}^{a}
is an independence relation
[
26
, Proposition 3.16]
. Moreover, for any independence relation,
\mathop{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\textstyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\scriptstyle\kern 2.80048pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 1.89871pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 2.80048pt}{\scriptscriptstyle\kern 1.42882pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 0.96873pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 1.42882pt}}
, we have that if
A\mathop{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\textstyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\scriptstyle\kern 2.80048pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 1.89871pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 2.80048pt}{\scriptscriptstyle\kern 1.42882pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 0.96873pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 1.42882pt}}_{C}B
, then
A\mathop{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\textstyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\scriptstyle\kern 2.80048pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 1.89871pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 2.80048pt}{\scriptscriptstyle\kern 1.42882pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 0.96873pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 1.42882pt}}^{a}_{C}B

[
26
, Remark 1.2.11]
.

Remark 3.8
.

Full existence for algebraic independence is often known as Neumann’s lemma
[
26
, Fact 3.1.5]
(cf.
[
48
]
).

Remark 3.9
.

Often, in the presence of non-trivial
G
-invariant equivalence relations it is more natural to consider the relation of
imaginary algebraic independence

\mathop{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\textstyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\scriptstyle\kern 2.80048pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 1.89871pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 2.80048pt}{\scriptscriptstyle\kern 1.42882pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 0.96873pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 1.42882pt}}^{ia}
, where

A\mathop{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\textstyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\scriptstyle\kern 2.80048pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 1.89871pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 2.80048pt}{\scriptscriptstyle\kern 1.42882pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 0.96873pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 1.42882pt}}^{ia}_{C}B\text{ if and only if }\acl^{eq}(AC)\cap\acl^{eq}(BC)=\acl^{eq}(C)\;.

We note that if
G\curvearrowright\Omega
has weak elimination of imaginaries, then algebraic independence and imaginary algebraic independence agree (cf.
[
15
, Propositions 3.2 and 3.4]
). In particular, several of our results apply to structures for which imaginary algebraic independence is well-behaved and which admit very weak elimination of imaginaries.

Definition 3.10
.

Let
G\curvearrowright\Omega
have locally finite algebraic closure. Let
\mathop{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\textstyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\scriptstyle\kern 2.80048pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 1.89871pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 2.80048pt}{\scriptscriptstyle\kern 1.42882pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 0.96873pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 1.42882pt}}
be a notion of independence. We define the following additional property that a notion of independence may have:

•

(base monotonicity) for all
B\subseteq C\subseteq D\in[\Omega]^{<\omega}
, if
A\mathop{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\textstyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\scriptstyle\kern 2.80048pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 1.89871pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 2.80048pt}{\scriptscriptstyle\kern 1.42882pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 0.96873pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 1.42882pt}}_{B}D
, then
A\mathop{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\textstyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\scriptstyle\kern 2.80048pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 1.89871pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 2.80048pt}{\scriptscriptstyle\kern 1.42882pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 0.96873pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 1.42882pt}}_{C}D
.

Definition 3.11
.

Let
G\curvearrowright\Omega
. We say that algebraic closure is
modular
if its lattice of (possibly infinite) algebraically closed sets satisfies the modular law:

•

(modularity) for all algebraically closed sets
A\subseteq B
and
C
,

\acl(A\cup(C\cap B))=\acl(A\cup C)\cap B\;.

We say that algebraic closure forms a
pregeometry
if it satisfies exchange:

•

(exchange) for all algebraically closed
A
, if
a\in\acl(Ab)\setminus A
, then
b\in\acl(Aa)
.

The following is well-known
[
26
, Proposition 1.2.17]
.

Fact 3.12
.

If algebraic closure forms a pregeometry, then
\mathop{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\textstyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\scriptstyle\kern 2.80048pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 1.89871pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 2.80048pt}{\scriptscriptstyle\kern 1.42882pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 0.96873pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 1.42882pt}}^{a}
satisfies base monotonicity if and only if algebraic closure is modular.

Definition 3.13
.

Let
G\curvearrowright\Omega
have locally finite algebraic closure. We say that an independence relation is
strongly one-based
if the following holds:

•

(strong one-basedness) for any
A,B\in[\Omega]^{<\omega}
,
A\mathop{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\textstyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\scriptstyle\kern 2.80048pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 1.89871pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 2.80048pt}{\scriptscriptstyle\kern 1.42882pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 0.96873pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 1.42882pt}}_{\acl(A)\cap\acl(B)}B
.

We reserve the name
one-based
for an independence relation in the following context:
G\curvearrowright\Omega
is the automorphism group of a countable saturated structure, and there is a ternary relation
\mathop{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\textstyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\scriptstyle\kern 2.80048pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 1.89871pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 2.80048pt}{\scriptscriptstyle\kern 1.42882pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 0.96873pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 1.42882pt}}
on
\Omega^{eq}
satisfying the axioms for an independence relation on finite subsets of
\Omega^{eq}
and their imaginary algebraic closures, and also satisfying the condition for strong one-basedness with respect to
\acl^{eq}
. This aligns with what model-theorists usually refer to as one-basedness (in the context of elimination of hyperimaginaries, cf.
[
40
, Chapter 5]
). Under the assumption of weak elimination of imaginaries, one-basedness in the sense above implies strong one-basedness.

Remark 3.14
.

From the observation at the end of
Example

3.7
, it is easy to deduce that if an independence relation is strongly one-based then it actually is
\mathop{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\textstyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\scriptstyle\kern 2.80048pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 1.89871pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 2.80048pt}{\scriptscriptstyle\kern 1.42882pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 0.96873pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 1.42882pt}}^{a}
. In
C
, when we say that a simple countable saturated structure with weak elimination of imaginaries is one-based, we mean that non-forking independence agrees with algebraic independence. Really, our proof of
Theorem

6.8
requires only the existence of an independence relation satisfying two additional conditions, namely, independent
3
-amalgamation (see
Definition

3.18
below) and a weakening of strong one-basedness that we call
k
-narrowness (
Definition

6.1
).

Example 3.15
.

Smoothly approximable structures
[
18
]
, which include all
\omega
-categorical
\omega
-stable structures
[
17
]
, are such that
\mathop{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\textstyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\scriptstyle\kern 2.80048pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 1.89871pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 2.80048pt}{\scriptscriptstyle\kern 1.42882pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 0.96873pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 1.42882pt}}^{ia}
satisfies base monotonicity
[
18
, Theorem 7]
.
They are one-based,
and
\acl^{eq}
satisfies modularity for them (indeed, the two conditions are equivalent for structures of finite thorn-rank
[
18
, Proposition 5.5.2]
, see
[
58
]
). These structures are known to include various linear, projective, semiprojective, and affine geometries over finite fields (arising from vector spaces with forms)
[
18
, Definitions 2.1.4, 2.1.7., and 2.1.8]
, and indeed can be “coordinatised” by such Lie geometries
[
18
, Theorem 2]
. Some interesting structures in this class have weak elimination of imaginaries, such as basic linear and semiprojective geometries
[
18
, Lemmas 2.3.5 and 2.3.6]
, and so
\mathop{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\textstyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\scriptstyle\kern 2.80048pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 1.89871pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 2.80048pt}{\scriptscriptstyle\kern 1.42882pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 0.96873pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 1.42882pt}}^{a}
satisfies base monotonicity for them. This, in particular, includes the infinite dimensional versions of the geometries giving rise to the classical groups, that is, infinite dimensional vector, symplectic, unitary, and orthogonal spaces over finite fields. Indeed, these are known to weakly eliminate imaginaries from
[
30
]
. Other smoothly approximable structures, such as basic affine geometries (not of quadratic type) have very weak elimination of imaginaries
[
18
, Lemma 2.3.12]
. So, for example, whilst it is well-known that
\mathop{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\textstyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\scriptstyle\kern 2.80048pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 1.89871pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 2.80048pt}{\scriptscriptstyle\kern 1.42882pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 0.96873pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 1.42882pt}}^{a}
does not satisfy base monotonicity in an affine space over a finite field
[
51
, Ch.2, Example 1.7]
, this structure has an expansion by finitely many imaginary sorts in which
\mathop{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\textstyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\scriptstyle\kern 2.80048pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 1.89871pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 2.80048pt}{\scriptscriptstyle\kern 1.42882pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 0.96873pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 1.42882pt}}^{a}
does satisfy base monotonicity. Our understanding is that it is an open question whether any smoothly approximable structure does not have very weak elimination of imaginaries.

Example 3.16
.

From the recent answer to Koponen’s conjecture
[
41
]
in
[
4
]
, we know that all simple finitely homogeneous structures are one-based,
and so our results apply to all structures in this class with very weak elimination of imaginaries. Indeed, it is an open problem whether all (finitely bounded) finitely homogeneous structures have very weak elimination of imaginaries
[
19
, Problem 18]
. Standard examples of simple finitely homogeneous structures are the random graph, the random bipartite graph, and the generic tetrahedron-free
3
-hypergraph (the Fraïssé limit of all finite uniform
3
-hypergraphs omitting a clique on four vertices). Examples of stable finitely homogeneous structures are
(\mathbb{N};=)
, all countable structures in a finite unary language and countably infinitely many disjoint copies of
K_{n}
.

Examples 3.17
.

Algebraic closure forms a pregeometry and is modular in several interesting cases:

•

whenever algebraic closure is trivial (i.e.,
\acl(A)=A
for all
A\in[\Omega]^{<\omega}
). This is the case, for example for
(\mathbb{Q};<)
or
(\mathbb{N};=)
;

•

whenever algebraic closure is degenerate in the sense that
\acl(A)=\bigcup_{a\in A}\acl(a)
. This is the case, for example for infinitely many disjoint copies of the complete graph
K_{n}
;

•

when
G
is
\mathrm{GL}(\aleph_{0},q)
or
\mathrm{PGL}(\aleph_{0},q)
for
q=p^{n}
for some
n\in\mathbb{N}
in its action on its corresponding vector space (or projective space).

The definitions in the rest of this subsection are relevant to
Section

6
.

Definition 3.18
.

We define some additional properties an independence relation might have:

•

(independent
3
-amalgamation) Suppose we are given
A,B_{1},B_{2},C_{1},C_{2}\in[\Omega]^{<\omega}
finite and algebraically closed such that
B_{1}\mathop{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\textstyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\scriptstyle\kern 2.80048pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 1.89871pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 2.80048pt}{\scriptscriptstyle\kern 1.42882pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 0.96873pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 1.42882pt}}_{A}B_{2}
,
A\subseteq B_{i}
and
C_{i}\mathop{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\textstyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\scriptstyle\kern 2.80048pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 1.89871pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 2.80048pt}{\scriptscriptstyle\kern 1.42882pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 0.96873pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 1.42882pt}}_{A}B_{i}
for
i=1,2
and
C_{1}\equiv_{A}C_{2}
. Then there exists
D\in[\Omega]^{<\omega}
such that

D\equiv_{B_{i}}C_{i}
for
i=1,2
and
D\mathop{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\textstyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\scriptstyle\kern 2.80048pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 1.89871pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 2.80048pt}{\scriptscriptstyle\kern 1.42882pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 0.96873pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 1.42882pt}}_{A}B_{1}B_{2}
.

•

(stationarity) If
A,B_{1},B_{2},C\in[\Omega]^{<\omega}
be finite and algebraically closed. Suppose that
B_{i}\mathop{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\textstyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\scriptstyle\kern 2.80048pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 1.89871pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 2.80048pt}{\scriptscriptstyle\kern 1.42882pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 0.96873pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 1.42882pt}}_{A}C
for
i=1,2
. Then
B_{1}\equiv_{A}B_{2}
implies
B_{1}\equiv_{AC}B_{2}
.

Fact 3.19
.

Let
G\curvearrowright\Omega
be the automorphism group of a countable saturated structure
\mathcal{M}
with locally finite algebraic closure and weak elimination of imaginaries. Then,

•

if the theory of
\mathcal{M}
is stable, there is an independence relation on
\Omega
(known as non-forking independence) satisfying base monotonicity and stationarity;

•

if the theory of
\mathcal{M}
is simple, then there is an independence relation on
\Omega
(known as non-forking independence) satisfying base monotonicity and independent
3
-amalgamation.

The second point in the fact above requires
[
40
, Theorem 3.2.8]
and the observation that in simple and small theories Lascar types agree with strong types
[
40
, Proposition 2.1.19(2) and Corollary 5.3.5]
.

Remark 3.20
.

Stable and simple theories can actually be characterised in terms of the existence of an abstract independence relation on sufficiently saturated models of its theory satisfying a certain set of axioms (cf.
[
39
]
for simple theories and
[
60
, Theorem 8.5.10]
for stable theories).

3.3
More topologies

In this subsection we introduce some additional topologies we encounter at various points of this paper. Firstly, the (semigroup) Zariski topology, which is central to
Section

4
and
Section

8
. Then, after a small result on localised pointwise topologies and how they cannot give us Hausdorff semigroup topologies on
\overline{G}
coarser than
\tau_{\mathrm{pw}}
in our context, we introduce generalized pointwise topologies. These are a generalization of localised topologies which feature as potential minimal
T_{1}
semigroup topologies on
G
in the context of
C
. These only feature in the proof of Theorem
6.8
.

3.3.1
Zariski topology

Let
S\subseteq\Omega^{\Omega}
be a monoid. The
Zariski topology on
S
is the topology generated by solution sets of inequalities of the form

\lambda_{n}s\lambda_{n-1}s\cdots\lambda_{1}s\lambda_{0}\neq\eta_{m}s\eta_{m-1}s\cdots\eta_{1}s\eta_{0}.

(1)

where
\lambda_{0},\dots,\lambda_{n},\eta_{0},\dots,\eta_{m}\in S
. It need not be Hausdorff or a semigroup topology in general. However, for
S
the endomorphism monoid of an
\omega
-categorical structure, it often coincides with the pointwise convergence topology on
S
, with the only counterexample so far provided in
[
52
]
. In general, it is contained in any
T_{1}
semigroup topology on
S

[
27
, Proposition 2.1]
. We say that a topology on a semigroup
S
is
semitopological
(or shift-continuous) if for each
s\in S
, the shift-maps
\lambda_{s},\rho_{s}:S\to S
given by
u\mapsto us
and
u\mapsto su
respectively are continuous. The Zariski topology is semitopological on any semigroup
[
27
, Proposition 2.2]
.

3.3.2
Localised pointwise topologies are not
T_{1}
on
\overline{G}

Localised pointwise topologies are a natural source of Hausdorff group topologies coarser than
\tau_{\mathrm{pw}}
in automorphism groups. In this section we give a quick proof that on monoids of elementary embeddings of countable saturated structures they are never
T_{1}
. The proof uses some basic saturation arguments from model theory. We hope it may help the reader appreciate what is to come in terms of the topological behaviour of
\overline{G}
being very different from that of
G
.

Definition 3.21
.

Let
G\curvearrowright\Omega
. Let
S\in\{G,\overline{G}\}
. Let
X\subseteq\Omega
be a
G
-invariant set. Then, by the
localised pointwise convergence topology
at
X
,
\tau_{\mathrm{pw}}^{X}
, we mean the topology on
S
whose subbasis of open sets is given by the sets
G_{\phi}
for
\phi\in\mathscr{P}(G)
with
\phi:A\to B
with
A,B\subseteq X
.

Example 3.22
.

Consider the random bipartite graph
\mathcal{B}
with orbits
X
and
Y
. This is just the Fraïssé limit of the class of bipartite graphs where each side of the partition is named by a unary predicate. Let
G:=\Aut(\mathcal{B})
and consider the localised pointwise convergence topology
\tau_{\mathrm{pw}}^{X}
. It is easy to observe that the identity is closed in
\tau_{\mathrm{pw}}^{X}
(on either
G
or
\overline{G}
)
[
34
, Example 3D]
. For
G
this is equivalent to
\tau_{\mathrm{pw}}^{X}
being Hausdorff, and indeed
\tau_{\mathrm{pw}}^{X}
is a Hausdorff group topology on
G
strictly coarser than
\tau_{\mathrm{pw}}
. However, as we will see in
Theorem

3.24
,
