\tau_{\mathrm{pw}}^{X}
on
\overline{G}
is not even
T_{1}
.

Lemma 3.23
.

Let
G\curvearrowright\Omega
be the automorphism group of a countable saturated structure. Let
X,Y\subseteq\Omega
be disjoint. Suppose that for all
B\in[X]^{<\omega}
, and
\dcl(B)\cap Y=\emptyset
. Then, for each
a\in Y
, there are
\alpha,\beta\in\overline{G}
such that
\alpha\upharpoonright_{X}=\beta\upharpoonright_{X}
and
\alpha(a)\neq\beta(a)
.

Proof.

We consider the countable saturated structure
\mathcal{M}
with automorphism group
G\curvearrowright\Omega
. Let
\overline{b}:=(b_{0},b_{1},b_{2},\dots)
be a tuple of length
\omega
enumerating
\mathcal{M}
with
a=b_{0}
. Consider the infinitary type
p(\overline{x}):=\mathrm{tp}(\overline{b})
. For the tuples of variables of length
\omega
,
\overline{x}
and
\overline{y}
, consider the partial type
\Gamma(\overline{x},\overline{y})
given by

p(\overline{x})\cup p(\overline{y})\cup\{x_{0}\neq y_{0}\}\cup\{x_{i}=y_{i}|\ b_{i}\in X\}.

Note that
\Gamma(\overline{x},\overline{y})
is finitely consistent. To see this, for some finite indexing set
J\subseteq\omega
, consider the restriction of
\Gamma
to variables
x_{i}
and
y_{i}
from
J
, which we call
\Gamma_{J}(\overline{x}_{J},\overline{y}_{J})
. Since
\dcl((b_{i}|i\in J)\cap X)\cap Y=\emptyset
, there is
g\in G
such that
g\upharpoonright_{(b_{i}|i\in J)\cap X}=\mathrm{Id}_{(b_{i}|i\in J)\cap X}
and
g(a)\neq a
, meaning that
\Gamma_{J}(\overline{x}_{J},\overline{y}_{J})
is satisfied by the tuple
((b_{i}|i\in J),(g(b_{i})|i\in J))
. Since
\Gamma(\overline{x},\overline{y})
is finitely satisfiable, by compactness and the Lowenheim-Skolem Theorem it is also satisfiable in some model
\mathcal{M}^{\prime}
, which can be chosen to be countable and saturated. In particular,
\mathcal{M}^{\prime}
is isomorphic to
\mathcal{M}
and so, we know that
\Gamma(\overline{x},\overline{y})
is satisfied in
\mathcal{M}
by a tuple
(\overline{c},\overline{d})
. Consider the maps
\alpha
and
\beta
given by
\alpha:b_{i}\mapsto c_{i}
and
\beta:b_{i}\mapsto d_{i}
for
i<\omega
. Since
\alpha
and
\beta
preserve the types of arbitrary finite tuples, they are elementary embeddings of
\mathcal{M}
by construction. Moreover, by construction,
\alpha(a)=c_{0}\neq d_{0}=\beta(a)
and for each
b_{i}\in X
,
\alpha(b_{i})=\beta(b_{i})
, meaning that
\alpha\upharpoonright_{X}=\beta\upharpoonright_{X}
, as desired.
∎

Theorem 3.24
.

Let
G\curvearrowright\Omega
be the automorphism group of a countable saturated structure. Let
X\subseteq\Omega
be a
G
-invariant set. Then,
\tau_{\mathrm{pw}}^{X}
is either equal to
\tau_{\mathrm{pw}}
or not
T_{1}
on
\overline{G}
.

Proof.

Firstly, note that if
Z\subseteq\Omega\setminus X
is a
G
-orbit and for some
B\in[X]^{<\omega}
,
\dcl(B)\cap Z\neq\emptyset
, then
\tau_{\mathrm{pw}}^{X}=\tau_{\mathrm{pw}}^{X\cup Z}
. Hence, we may assume without loss of generality that
X\subsetneq\Omega
and for all
G
-orbits
Z\subseteq\Omega\setminus X
and all
B\in[X]^{<\omega}
,
\dcl(B)\cap Z=\emptyset
. But then, we are in the setting of Lemma
3.23
and we can build
\alpha,\beta\in\overline{G}
agreeing on
X
but disagreeing on some point
a\in\Omega\setminus X
. In particular,
\beta
belongs to any open neighbourhood of
\alpha
in
\tau_{\mathrm{pw}}^{X}
, meaning that
\tau_{\mathrm{pw}}^{X}
is not
T_{1}
.
∎

3.3.3
Generalized pointwise topologies

In this section we introduce a class of topologies generalizing localised pointwise topologies which are needed for a precise statement of
Theorem

6.8
(
C
in the introduction).
Throughout this section
\Omega
is a countable set and
G\curvearrowright\Omega
is a group acting on
\Omega
with locally finite algebraic closure. Note that the family of topologies introduced in this subsection is only used in
Section

6
for the statement and proof of
Theorem

6.8
. In particular,
Lemma

5.15
and
Lemma

5.16
, mention these topologies only insofar they are needed for the proof of
Theorem

6.8
, and the parts of these lemmas referring to generalized pointwise topologies are not needed in their uses for
Theorem

7.19
.

Given
a\in\Omega
, we write
[a]:=\mathrm{acl}(a)\cap G\cdot a
. Given
A\in\mathfrak{X}
, we use the notation
G_{A/\sim}
to denote the set of elements in
G
which stabilize every class
[a]
for
a\in A
. Note that all those elements must preserve
A
setwise. It follows from
3.2
that
[x]
is the equivalence class of an equivalence relation
\sim
with finite classes.

Given any closed
G
-invariant
\Delta\subseteq\Omega
, write
G(\Delta)
for the image of the restriction homomorphism
r_{\Delta}:G\to{\Sym(\Delta)}
. Let also

[\Delta]=\{[x]\,|\,x\in\Delta\},\quad\quad K(\Delta)=\{h\in G(\Delta)\,|\,\forall x\in\Delta\,\,\,h(x)\in[x]\}.

Observation 3.25
.

The set
K(\Delta)
is a normal subgroup of
G(\Delta)
and compact with respect to the pointwise convergence topology on
G(\Delta)
.

Proof.

We can prove that
K(\Delta)
is normal from the
G
-invariance of
\Delta
and
\sim
.
Indeed, given elements
g,h\in G
and
a\in\Delta
, we have that
g^{h}([a])=[a]

if and only if
g([h(a)])=[h(a)]=h([a])
. So,
g
preserves
[a]
for all
a\in\Delta
if and only if
g^{h}
does.
The various restriction maps of the action of
K(\Delta)
to each of the classes
[a]
for
a\in\Delta

jointly determine a map
\iota:K(\Delta)\hookrightarrow\prod_{[a]\in\Delta/\sim}G([a])
.
It is not hard to check that this map is a homeomorphic embedding with respect to the pointwise convergence topology on the left and the product topology of the discrete topologies on the right. On the other hand, it is not hard to see that
K(\Delta)
is closed in
G(\Delta)
.
Thus,
K(\Delta)
and its image by
\iota
are sequentially complete, which implies that
\im(\iota)
is closed in the compact topological group
\prod_{[a]\in\Delta/\sim}G([a])
. It follows that
K(\Delta)
is compact as well.
∎

Write
\mathfrak{X}_{\Delta}
for the collection of all intersections with
\Delta
of sets in
\mathfrak{X}
. Note that if
\Delta
is algebraically closed, the latter agrees with the family of sets in
\mathfrak{X}
that are contained in
\Delta
. Given closed
G
-invariant subsets
\Delta\subseteq\Sigma
we write
\pi^{\Sigma}_{\Delta}:K(\Sigma)\to K(\Delta)
for the restriction map, which is clearly a continuous surjection.

Definition 3.26
.

Let
S\in\{G,\overline{G}\}
. A
generalized pointwise
topology on
S

is given by a
G
-invariant subset
\Delta\subseteq\Omega
, as well as some normal subgroup
K\unlhd G(\Delta)
,
K\leqslant K(\Delta)
, closed in the pointwise convergence topology, and is the topology, denoted by

\tau_{\mathrm{pw}}^{\Delta,K}
, determined by the collection basic open sets of the form

S_{\phi}^{K}:=\{s\in S\,|\,\exists h\in K\,\,s_{\restriction A}=(\phi h)_{\restriction A}\},

(2)

where
\phi\in\mathscr{P}(G)
with
\phi:A\cong B
for some
A,B\in\mathfrak{X}_{\Delta}
. We will also write
G_{A}^{K}
for
G_{Id_{A}}^{K}
.

Note that in the current setting localised topologies are just a special case of generalized pointwise topologies. However, since the latter can only be coarser, in the context of
\overline{G}
, generalised pointwise topologies are never
T_{1}
when they disagree with
\tau_{\mathrm{pw}}
by
Theorem

3.24
.

4
On the centre and the Zariski topology

In this section, we prove that that the semigroup Zariski topology
\tau_{Z}
is not Hausdorff on
G
or
\overline{G}
whenever
G\curvearrowright\Omega
has locally finite algebraic closure and non-trivial centre. These assumptions are easily satisfied by various natural infinite permutation groups such as the automorphism group of a countably infinite vector space over a finite field
\mathrm{GL}(\aleph_{0},q)
, or the automorphism group of infinitely many disjoint copies of the complete graph
K_{l}
. Indeed, the latter case was at the core of the original example of
[
52
]
of the endomorphism monoid of a homogeneous structure for which
\tau_{\mathrm{Z}}
is not Hausdorff (previously no examples were known).

Definition 4.1
.

Let
\lambda_{0},\dots,\lambda_{n},\eta_{0},\dots,\eta_{m}\in\overline{G}
for
n\geqslant m
. Consider the inequality

\lambda_{n}s\lambda_{n-1}s\cdots\lambda_{1}s\lambda_{0}\neq\eta_{m}s\eta_{m-1}s\cdots\eta_{1}s\eta_{0}.

(3)

We say that this inequality is of
type (I)
if
n=m
. Otherwise, if
n>m
, we say this inequality is of
type (II)
.

Notation 4.2
.

Below, we will be considering elements
f\in\overline{G}^{2}
. When we write
fa
for
a\in\Omega^{2}
, we consider the natural action, where, writing
(f^{\prime},f^{\prime\prime})
for
f
and
(a^{\prime},a^{\prime\prime})
for
a
,
fa=(f^{\prime}a^{\prime},f^{\prime\prime}a^{\prime\prime})
. If
a\in\Omega^{2}
and
\delta\in\overline{G}
, we write
\delta a
for
(\delta a^{\prime},\delta a^{\prime\prime})
, the diagonal action of
\delta
on
\Omega
.

Definition 4.3
.

Consider sequences
\overline{f}:=(f_{n},\dots,f_{0})
where for each
i\leqslant n
,
f_{i}\in\overline{G}^{2}
and
\overline{a}:=(a_{n},\dots,a_{0})
such that for each
i\leqslant n
,
a_{i}\in\Omega^{2}
. Further choose finite tuples
\overline{b}
and
\overline{b}^{\prime}
from
\Omega
such that

\overline{b}\equiv\overline{b}^{\prime}
. We say that
\overline{a}
is a
free
sequence with respect to
\overline{f}
over
(\overline{b},\overline{b}^{\prime})
if the following two conditions hold

•

There exists some
\delta\in G
such that
\delta(\overline{b})=\overline{b}^{\prime}
and for each
i<n

a_{i+1}=\delta f_{i}a_{i}
. That is,

(f_{n}a_{n})(f_{n-1}a_{n-1})\dots(f_{0}a_{0})\overline{b}\equiv a_{n+1}a_{n}\dots a_{1}\overline{b}^{\prime}\;;

•

For all
i<n
,

f_{i+1}a_{i+1}\not\in\acl((f_{i}a_{i}),\dots,(f_{0}a_{0}),\overline{b})\;.

We say that
\overline{a}
has length
n
.

Lemma 4.4
.

Let
G\curvearrowright\Omega
have locally finite algebraic closure. Let
\overline{a}
be a free sequence with respect to
\overline{f}
over
(\overline{b},\overline{b}^{\prime})
of length
n
. Consider
f_{n+1}\in\overline{G}^{2}
. Then, there is
a_{n+1}\in\Omega^{2}
such that
(a_{n+1},\overline{a})
is free with respect to
(f_{n+1},\overline{f})
over
(\overline{b},\overline{b}^{\prime})
.

Proof.

Let
\overline{b}_{n}:=\acl((f_{n-1}a_{n-1}),\dots,(f_{0}a_{0}),\overline{b})
and
\overline{b}_{n}^{\prime}:=\acl(a_{n}\dots a_{1}\overline{b}^{\prime})
. By freeness, there is an element
\delta\in G
that yields
\overline{b}_{n}\equiv\overline{b}_{n}^{\prime}
. Now, take
a^{\prime}_{n+1}=\delta(f_{n}a_{n})
. We have
(f_{n}a_{n})\overline{b}_{n}\equiv a_{n+1}^{\prime}\overline{b}_{n}^{\prime}
. Finally, recall that
f_{n}a_{n}\not\in\overline{b}_{n}
by Definition
4.3
, and note that
f_{n+1}^{-1}(\acl((f_{n}a_{n}),\overline{b}_{n}))
is finite since algebraic closure is locally finite and each of
f^{\prime}_{n+1}
and
f^{\prime\prime}_{n+1}
is injective, where
f_{n+1}:=(f^{\prime}_{n+1},f^{\prime\prime}_{n+1})
. Thus, by Neumann’s Lemma, there is
a_{n+1}\equiv_{\overline{b}_{n}^{\prime}}a_{n+1}^{\prime}
such that
a_{n+1}\not\in f_{n+1}^{-1}(\acl((f_{n}a_{n})\overline{b}_{n}))
. In particular,

f_{n+1}a_{n+1}\not\in\acl((f_{n}a_{n})\overline{b}_{n}))=\acl((f_{i}a_{i}),\dots,(f_{0}a_{0}),\overline{b}),

as required in the second condition of the definition of freeness.
Since
a_{n+1}\equiv_{\overline{b}_{n}^{\prime}}a_{n+1}^{\prime}
, we have
a_{n+1}\overline{b}_{n}^{\prime}\equiv(f_{n}a_{n})\overline{b}_{n}
.
∎

Lemma 4.5
.

Let
G\curvearrowright\Omega
have locally finite algebraic closure. Then, the set of solutions of any semigroup inequality of type (II) in
\overline{G}
is comeagre (with respect to
\tau_{\mathrm{pw}}
).

Proof.

Consider an inequality of type (II) of the form of (
3
) and write
\mathcal{O}
for its set of solutions. Let
\gamma\in\overline{G}
, let
\overline{b}
be a finite tuple from
\Omega
, and set
\overline{b}^{\prime}=\gamma\overline{b}
. Consider the
\tau_{\mathrm{pw}}
-open neighbourhood
S_{(\overline{b},\overline{b}^{\prime})}
of
\gamma
. Let

\overline{f}:=((\lambda_{n},1),\dots,(\lambda_{m+1},1),(\lambda_{m},\eta_{m})(\lambda_{m-1},\eta_{m-1}),\dots,(\lambda_{0},\eta_{0})).

Select
e_{0}\not\in f_{0}^{-1}(\acl(\overline{b}))

Let
a_{0}:=(e_{0},e_{0})
. It is easy to see that
a_{0}
is free with respect to
f_{0}
over
(\bar{b},\bar{b}^{\prime})
. We can use Lemma
4.4
to build a free sequence
\overline{a}
with respect to
\overline{f}
over
(\overline{b},\overline{b}^{\prime})
. Since
n>m
, at the end of this process,

f_{n}a_{n}\not\in\acl((f_{m}a_{m}),\dots,(f_{0}a_{0}),\overline{b}).

Thus, writing for each
i<n
,
(a_{i}^{1},a_{i}^{2})
for
a_{i}
, we have that
\lambda_{n}a^{1}_{n}\neq\eta_{m}a_{m}^{2}
. Hence, there is
\delta\in G
such that
\delta\overline{b}=\overline{b}^{\prime}
and
a_{i+1}=\delta\lambda_{i}a_{i}
for each
i<n
. Since
f_{n}a^{1}_{n}\neq a_{m}^{2}
,
\delta
satisfies the given inequality.

We have thus proved that for any
\tau_{\mathrm{pw}}
-open neighbourhood
S_{(\overline{b},\overline{b}^{\prime})}
of any element
\overline{G}
, there is
\delta\in S_{(\overline{b},\overline{b}^{\prime})}
and a neighbourhood of
\delta
,
S^{\prime}:=S_{\delta\upharpoonright{\overline{b}(f_{i}a_{i}|i\leqslant n)}}
such that
S^{\prime}\cap(\overline{G}\setminus\mathcal{O})=\emptyset
. That is,
\overline{G}\setminus\mathcal{O}
is nowhere dense, and so meagre in
\overline{G}
. Thus
\mathcal{O}
is comeagre.
∎

Corollary 4.6
.

Let
G\curvearrowright\Omega
have locally finite algebraic closure. Let
\mathcal{O}\in\tau_{\mathrm{Z}}
be the intersection of finitely many basic Zariski-open sets where each of these is defined by an inequality of type (II). Then,
\mathcal{O}
is comeagre in
\overline{G}
.

Proof.

This just follows from
Lemma

4.5
since the finite (or even countable) intersection of comeagre sets is comeagre.
∎

Lemma 4.7
.

Let
G\curvearrowright\Omega
. Suppose that
\gamma\in Z(G)
. Then,
\gamma\in Z(\overline{G})
.

Proof.

Let
\alpha\in\overline{G}
. Take
a\in\Omega
. There is some
\alpha^{\prime}\in G
such that
\alpha^{\prime}_{\upharpoonright\{a\gamma(a)\}}=\alpha_{\upharpoonright\{a\gamma(a)\}}
. Hence,

\gamma\alpha(a)=\gamma\alpha^{\prime}(a)=\alpha^{\prime}\gamma(a)=\alpha\gamma(a),

where the first and last equality follow from the choice of
\alpha^{\prime}
and the second equality from
\gamma\in Z(G)
. Since the equation above holds for all
a\in\Omega
, it follows that
\gamma\alpha=\alpha\gamma
, and so that
\gamma\in Z(\overline{G})
.
∎

Theorem 4.8
.

Let
G\curvearrowright\Omega
have locally finite algebraic closure and non-trivial centre. Then, the semigroup Zariski topology
\tau_{\mathrm{Z}}
on
\overline{G}
is not Hausdorff. This is also the case for the semigroup Zariski topology on
G
.

Proof.

We write the argument for
\tau_{\mathrm{Z}}
on
\overline{G}
(the argument for
G
is identical).
Let
\gamma\in Z(G)
be a non-trivial central element in
G
. Let
\mathcal{O}
and
\mathcal{U}
be Zariski-open neighbourhoods of
1
and
\gamma
respectively; we prove
\mathcal{O}\cap\mathcal{U}\neq\emptyset
. We can consider
\mathcal{O}
as
\mathcal{O}_{(I)}\cap\mathcal{O}_{(II)}
, where
\mathcal{O}_{(I)}
is the intersection of (finitely many) Zariski-open sets defined by inequalities of type (I) and
\mathcal{O}_{(II)}
is defined by the intersection of (finitely many) Zariski-open sets defined by inequalities of type (II). Similarly, we can consider
\mathcal{U}
as
\mathcal{U}_{(I)}\cap\mathcal{U}_{(II)}
.

Firstly, note that
\gamma\in\mathcal{O}_{(I)}\cap\mathcal{U}_{(I)}
. In fact,
\gamma
satisfies all inequalities of type (I) satisfied by
1
. To see this, suppose that, for
a_{1},\dots,a_{n},b_{1},\dots,b_{n}\in\overline{G}
we have that

a_{n}\cdots a_{1}\neq b_{n}\cdots b_{1}\;.

Then, we can deduce that

\displaystyle a_{n}\gamma\dots a_{1}\gamma

\displaystyle=\gamma^{n}a_{n}\cdots a_{1}

\displaystyle\neq\gamma^{n}b_{n}\cdots b_{1}

\displaystyle=b_{n}\gamma\dots b_{1}\gamma\;,

where the first and last equalities follow from
\gamma\in Z(\overline{G})
(Lemma
4.7
) and the intermediate inequality follows from
\gamma
, and so
\gamma^{n}
being injective (since all elements of
\overline{G}
are injective).

Now, since
\tau_{\mathrm{Z}}
is coarser than
\tau_{\mathrm{pw}}
and
\gamma\in\mathcal{U}\cap\mathcal{O}_{(I)}
, there is some finite
A\subseteq\Omega
such that
S_{\gamma_{\upharpoonright A}}\subseteq\mathcal{U}\cap\mathcal{O}_{(I)}
. By Corollary
4.6
, there is some
\delta\in S_{\gamma_{\upharpoonright A}}\cap\mathcal{O}_{(II)}
. In particular, by construction,

\delta\in\mathcal{O}\cap\mathcal{U},

and hence
\tau_{\mathrm{Z}}
is not Hausdorff.
∎

5
Chains, rigidity, and dispersivity

5.1
Chains

In this section, we work again with a group
G\curvearrowright\Omega
with locally finite algebraic closure, and the semigroup (under composition) given by its topological closure
\overline{G}
in the space
\Omega^{\Omega}
with the
topology of pointwise convergence.
We let
S\in\{G,\overline{G}\}
.

For
k\geqslant 0
, we refer to a finite tuple of finite tuples

\mathcal{C}=(\overline{c}_{i})_{i=0}^{k}
as a chain of length
k
. When
k
is known or implicit, we just call
\mathcal{C}
a chain. If each
\overline{c}_{i}
from the chain
\mathcal{C}
is such that
\acl(\overline{c}_{i})=\overline{c}_{i}
, we call
\mathcal{C}
a chain of algebraically closed sets.
Given a chain
\mathcal{C}:=(\overline{c}_{i})_{i=0}^{k}
of length
k
, we write
S_{(\overline{c}_{i})_{i=0}^{k}}
(or
S_{\mathcal{C}}
) for

S_{{\overline{c}_{0}}}S_{\overline{c}_{1}}\cdots S_{\overline{c}_{k}},

where, for each
i\leqslant k
,
S_{\overline{c}_{i}}
is the stabiliser of
\overline{c}_{i}
in
S
(which is a basic open set in
\tau_{\mathrm{pw}}
).

Lemma 5.1
.

For any

C\in[\Omega]^{<\omega}

and any
g\in G
, we have
S_{g(C)}g=gS_{C}
.

Proof.

For the inclusion
S_{g(C)}g\subseteq gS_{C}
consider
h\in S_{g(C)}
. Then,
s:=g^{-1}hg\in S_{C}
yields that
hg=gs
. The other inclusion is analogous: for
s\in S_{C}
, choose
h:=gsg^{-1}\in S_{g(C)}
, yielding
S_{g(C)}g\supseteq gS_{C}
. This concludes the proof.
∎

Observation 5.2
.

Assume that we are given a chain
\mathcal{C}=(\overline{c}_{i})_{i=0}^{k}
, where each
\overline{c}_{i}\in[\Omega]^{<\omega}
. Then the set
S_{\mathcal{C}}
coincides with the collection of elements
s\in S
such that there is a chain
\overline{c}^{\prime}:=(\overline{c}_{i}^{\prime})_{i=0}^{k}
with
\overline{c}_{0}^{\prime}=\overline{c}_{0}
,
\overline{c}^{\prime}_{k}=s(\overline{c}_{k})
, and
\overline{c}^{\prime}_{i}\overline{c}^{\prime}_{i+1}\equiv\overline{c}_{i}\overline{c}_{i+1}
.

Proof.

To see this, assume first that
s\in S_{\mathcal{C}}
, so
s=g_{0}\cdots g_{k}
for some
g_{i}\in S_{\overline{c}_{i}}
. Then one can set
\overline{c}_{i}^{\prime}:=g_{0}\cdots g_{i}(\overline{c}_{i})
for all
i
, with
\overline{c}^{\prime}_{i}\overline{c}^{\prime}_{i+1}\equiv\overline{c}_{i}\overline{c}_{i+1}
witnessed by
g_{0}\cdots g_{i}
. Conversely, picking
g_{i}
with
g_{i}(\overline{c}_{i}\overline{c}_{i+1})=\overline{c}_{i}^{\prime}\overline{c}_{i+1}^{\prime}
, one can write
s=sg_{k}^{-1}g_{k}\cdots g_{0}^{-1}g_{0}
and obtains
s\in S_{\mathcal{C}}
by virtue of
g_{i+1}g_{i}^{-1}\in S_{\overline{c}_{i}}
for all
i
and
sg_{k}^{-1}\in S_{\overline{c}_{k}}
.
∎

Observation 5.3
.

Let
\mathcal{C}=(\overline{c}_{i})_{i=0}^{k}
be a chain, let
J\subseteq\{0,\dots,k\}
and consider the subchain
\mathcal{D}:=(\overline{c}_{i})_{i\in J}
. Then,
S_{\mathcal{D}}\subseteq S_{\mathcal{C}}
.

Proof.

Let
s\in S_{\mathcal{D}}
. By definition,
s=\prod_{j\in J}g_{j}
, where
g_{j}\in S_{\overline{c}_{j}}
. For
i\leqslant k
, let

g^{\prime}_{i}:=\begin{cases}g_{i}&\text{ if }i\in J;\\
1&\text{ otherwise.}\end{cases}

Then,

s=\prod_{j\in J}g_{j}=g^{\prime}_{0}\cdot\dots\cdot g_{k}^{\prime}\in S_{\mathcal{C}},

concluding the proof.
∎

The following is a consequence of either of the two results above.

Corollary 5.4
.

Assume that
A\in[\Omega]^{<\omega}
and
k\geqslant 0
. For any two chains
\mathcal{C}=(\overline{c}_{i})_{i=0}^{k}
and
\mathcal{C}^{\prime}=(\overline{c}_{i}^{\prime})_{i=0}^{k}
such that:

•

\overline{c}_{k}=\overline{c}^{\prime}_{k}
, and

•

\overline{c}_{i}\overline{c}_{i+1}\equiv_{A}\overline{c}^{\prime}_{i}\overline{c}^{\prime}_{i+1}
for all
0\leqslant i\leqslant k-1
,

there is
g\in G_{A}
such that

gS_{\mathcal{C}}=S_{\mathcal{C}^{\prime}},\quad\quad g(\overline{c}_{0})=\overline{c_{0}}^{\prime}.

Proof.

The result can be proved by induction on the length of the chain. If
k=0
, i.e., the chains have only one entry, then the conclusion agrees with the assumptions and the result is trivial.
Assume now the result holds for
k\geqslant 0
and take two chains
\mathcal{C}=(\overline{c}_{i})_{i=0}^{k+1}
and
\mathcal{C}^{\prime}=(\overline{c}^{\prime}_{i})_{i=0}^{k+1}
with
\overline{c}^{\prime}_{k+1}=\overline{c}_{k+1}
and
\overline{c}_{i}\overline{c}_{i+1}\equiv_{A}\overline{c}_{i}^{\prime}\overline{c}^{\prime}_{i+1}
for all
0\leqslant i\leqslant k
. Denote by
\tilde{\mathcal{C}}
and
\tilde{\mathcal{C}^{\prime}}
the chains obtained from
\mathcal{C}
and
\mathcal{C}^{\prime}
respectively by removing the first term.

The induction assumption provides some
h\in S_{A}\cap G
with the property that
hS_{\widetilde{\mathcal{C}}}=S_{\widetilde{\mathcal{C}^{\prime}}}
and
\overline{c_{1}}^{\prime}=h(\overline{c}_{1})
. Using
Lemma

5.1
for the last equation below we obtain

S_{\overline{c}^{\prime}}=S_{\overline{c_{0}}^{\prime}}S_{\widetilde{\mathcal{C}^{\prime}}}=S_{\overline{c_{0}}^{\prime}}hS_{\widetilde{\mathcal{C}}}=hS_{h^{-1}(\overline{c_{0}}^{\prime})}S_{\widetilde{\mathcal{C}}}.

(4)

Notice that

h^{-1}(\overline{c_{0}}^{\prime})\overline{c}_{1}\equiv_{A}\overline{c_{0}}^{\prime}h(\overline{c}_{1})=\overline{c_{0}}^{\prime}\overline{c_{1}}^{\prime}\equiv_{A}\overline{c}_{0}\overline{c}_{1},

(5)

which means there is
h^{\prime}\in S_{A\overline{c}_{1}}\cap G
with the property that
(h^{\prime})^{-1}h^{-1}(\overline{c_{0}}^{\prime})=\overline{c}_{0}
. So using
Lemma

5.1
again the right hand side of (
4
) is also equal to the left hand side of

hh^{\prime}S_{(hh^{\prime})^{-1}(\overline{c}_{0}^{\prime})}(h^{\prime})^{-1}S_{\tilde{\mathcal{C}}}=hh^{\prime}S_{\mathcal{C}},

(6)

where we have used
(h^{\prime})^{-1}S_{\widetilde{\mathcal{C}}}=S_{\widetilde{\mathcal{C}}}
, and so we are done by taking
g=hh^{\prime}
.
∎

The following is a standard consequence of Neumann’s lemma:

Fact 5.5
.

Let
C\in[\Omega]^{<\omega}
be algebraically closed. Then, for all
A,B\in[\Omega]^{<\omega}
there is
A^{\prime}\equiv_{C}A

A^{\prime}\cap B=A\cap B\cap C
.

An easy but useful consequence of this is the following:

Lemma 5.6
.

Let
G\curvearrowright\Omega
have locally finite algebraic closure.
Then for any algebraically closed
A,C,D\in[\Omega]^{<\omega}
there is some algebraically closed
E\in[\Omega]^{<\omega}
and
g\in G
such that
E\cap A=C\cap D\cap A
and the following inclusion holds:

S_{E}g\subseteq S_{C}S_{D}.

Proof.

By
5.5
, we can find some element
g\in S_{C}\cap G
such that
g(D)\cap A=C\cap D\cap A
. By applying
Lemma

5.1
, we have
S_{g(D)}g=gS_{D}\subseteq S_{C}S_{D}
, so the desired conclusion holds with
E=g(D)
.
∎

Lemma 5.7
.

Let
\mathcal{C}=(\overline{c}_{i})_{i=0}^{2k}
be a chain of algebraically closed sets of length
2k
for some
k\in\mathbb{N}
. Assume that we are in one the following two cases:

(I)

There is some algebraically closed
A\in[\Omega]^{<\omega}
such that
\overline{c}_{2i}\cap\overline{c}_{2i+1}\cap\overline{c}_{2i+2}=A
for all
0\leqslant i\leqslant k-1
.

(II)

There is some
G
-invariant
\Delta\subseteq\Omega
such that
\overline{c}_{2i}\cap\overline{c}_{2i+1}\subseteq\Delta
for all
0\leqslant i\leqslant k-1
.

Then there is a chain of algebraically closed sets
\mathcal{D}=(\overline{d}_{i})_{i=0}^{k}
of length
k
such that for some
g\in G
,

S_{\mathcal{D}}\subseteq gS_{\mathcal{C}}.

Moreover:

•

In case
(I)
we can ensure
\overline{d}_{i}\cap\overline{d}_{i+1}=A
for all
0\leqslant i\leqslant k-1
, and
g\in G_{A}
;

•

In case
(II)
we can ensure
\overline{d}_{i}\cap\overline{d}_{i+1}\subseteq\Delta
for all
0\leqslant i\leqslant k-1
.

Proof.

First consider case
(I)
.
Our aim is to construct a chain of algebraically closed sets
\mathcal{C}^{\prime}=(\overline{c}^{\prime}_{i})_{i=0}^{2k}
such that for all
0\leqslant i\leqslant k-1
,
\overline{c}_{i}\overline{c}_{i+1}\equiv_{A}\overline{c}^{\prime}_{i}\overline{c}^{\prime}_{i+1}
,
\overline{c}^{\prime}_{2i}\cap\overline{c}^{\prime}_{2i+2}=A
, and with
\overline{c}^{\prime}_{2k}=\overline{c}_{2k}
. Given such a chain of algebraically closed sets
\overline{c}^{\prime}
, using
Corollary

5.4
we know that for some
g\in G_{A}
we have
S_{\overline{c}^{\prime}}=gS_{\mathcal{C}}
. In particular, letting
\mathcal{D}=(\overline{c}^{\prime}_{2i})_{i=0}^{k}
, by Observation
5.3
, we get that
S_{\mathcal{D}}\subseteq S_{\overline{c}^{\prime}}=gS_{\mathcal{C}}
, thus completing the proof.

We now construct
\mathcal{C}^{\prime}
backwards by induction. The base case is trivial, setting
\overline{c}_{2k}^{\prime}=\overline{c}_{2k}
. Suppose that we have built
(\overline{c}^{\prime}_{j})_{j=2l}^{2k}
have already been constructed for some
l>0
, with all the desired properties. We need to build
\overline{c}^{\prime}_{2l-1}
and
\overline{c}^{\prime}_{2l-2}
. First we choose any
\overline{c}^{\prime}_{2l-1}
with
\overline{c}^{\prime}_{2l-1}\overline{c}^{\prime}_{2l}\cong_{A}\overline{c}_{2l-1}\overline{c}_{2l}
. Then, we claim we can find
\overline{c}^{\prime}_{2l-2}
with
\overline{c}^{\prime}_{2l-2}\overline{c}^{\prime}_{2l-1}\cong_{A}\overline{c}_{2l-2}\overline{c}_{2l-1}
and
\overline{c}^{\prime}_{2l}\cap\overline{c}^{\prime}_{2l-2}=A
. For this purpose, first take
\overline{c}_{2l-2}^{\prime\prime}
such that
\overline{c}_{2l-2}^{\prime\prime}\overline{c}_{2l-1}^{\prime}\overline{c}_{2l}^{\prime}\equiv_{A}\overline{c}_{2l-2}\overline{c}_{2l-1}\overline{c}_{2l}
. From this, and our assumption on
\mathcal{C}
we get
\overline{c}_{2l-2}^{\prime\prime}\cap\overline{c}_{2l-1}^{\prime}\cap\overline{c}_{2l}^{\prime}=A
. Now, by
5.5
(replacing in its statement
D
with
\overline{c}^{\prime\prime}_{2l-2}
,
C
with
\overline{c}^{\prime}_{2l-1}
and
D
with
\overline{c}_{2l}
), we can pick
\overline{c}^{\prime}_{2l-2}\equiv_{\overline{c}^{\prime}_{2l-1}}\overline{c}^{\prime\prime}_{2l-2}
such that
\overline{c}^{\prime}_{2l-2}\cap\overline{c}_{2l}^{\prime}=\overline{c}^{\prime\prime}_{2l-2}\cap\overline{c}^{\prime}_{2l-1}\cap\overline{c}_{2l}^{\prime}=A
, as desired.

In case
(II)
, let

B:=\acl\left(\bigcup_{i=0}^{k-1}\overline{c}_{2i}\cap\overline{c}_{2i+1}\right)\;.

We then construct
\overline{c}^{\prime}
similarly to case
(I)
, but only requiring that
\overline{c}_{i}\overline{c}_{i+1}\equiv\overline{c}_{i}^{\prime}\overline{c}_{i+1}^{\prime}
; in particular, we can only ensure that
g\in G
, and we cannot ensure that it stabilises
B
and
\overline{c}^{\prime}_{2i}\cap\overline{c}^{\prime}_{2i+2}\subseteq B\subseteq\Delta
for all
0\leqslant i\leqslant k-1
. This can be ensured again in the relevant step of the construction of
\overline{c}^{\prime}
by Fact
5.5
.

∎

5.2
Rigidity and dispersivity

For the entirety of this subsection, we work with a closed permutation group
G\curvearrowright\Omega
with locally finite algebraic closure and we consider the semigroup
S
to be be either
G
or
\overline{G}
. Moreover,
\tau
will denote a semigroup topology on
S
coarser than
\tau_{\mathrm{pw}}
. Throughout this subsection, we carry out in parallel arguments for when
S=G
(needed for
Theorem

6.8
) and when
S=\overline{G}
with

(
♣
)

‣
3.2
holding (needed for
Theorem

7.19
). The reader interested in only one of the two results may consequently only care about the parts of the statements and proofs of the lemmas below involving their preferred choice between
G
or
\overline{G}
.

In this subsection we introduce two potential behaviours that, given a semigroup topology
\tau
on
S
strictly coarser than
\tau_{\mathrm{pw}}
, we may expect some
t\in S
to have with respect to some
X\subseteq\Omega
:
\tau
-rigidity and
\tau
-dispersivity. As we clarify in Observation
5.9
.
6
and Lemma
5.13
, these two behaviours are mutually complementary in a weak sense. We prove that if every
s\in S
is
\tau
-rigid on all of
\Omega
then
\tau
is not
T_{1}
(Lemma
5.11
). However, the existence of an element which is not
\tau
-rigid on some
a^{\prime}\in\Omega
implies that of an element which is
\tau
-dispersive on some
a\in\Omega
(Lemma
5.13
), and so the existence of an element which is
\tau
-dispersive on the entire
G
-orbit of
a
(Lemma
5.14
). In both the contexts of
Theorem

6.8
and
Theorem

7.19
, we are able to deduce from the existence of some
s\in S
which is
\tau
-dispersive on a suitable orbit
\Sigma
, the existence of some
t\in S
satisfying a stronger version of
\tau
-dispersivity, which we call deep
\tau
-dispersiveness. The existence of such deeply
\tau
-dispersive elements on
\Sigma
will allow to show that any suitable non-empty
\tau
-open set
\mathcal{U}
contains all elementary embedding which map a certain tuple to the end of some chain of finite closed sets of a certain fixed type, namely, where the initial term and the joint type of consecutive links is predetermined.
The richness of
\tau
-open sets in this sense is used, in combination with other arguments, in the proof of the main theorems of two next sections. First in the proof of
Theorem

6.8
and later in that of
Theorem

7.19
, where it serves to guarantee the existence in
\mathcal{U}
of embeddings with suitably small image (the core argument takes place in
7.5
).

Definition 5.8
.

Let
s_{0}\in S
. We say that
s_{0}
is
\bm{\tau}
-rigid
on a point
a\in\Omega
if there exists some

\mathcal{V}\in\mathcal{N}_{\tau}(s_{0})
such that
s(a)\in\mathrm{acl}(s_{0}(a))
for all
s\in\mathcal{V}
.

We say that
s_{0}
is
\bm{\tau}
-dispersive
on
a
if for every finite
B\subseteq\Omega
and any
\mathcal{V}\in\mathcal{N}_{\tau}({s_{0}})
there is
s\in\mathcal{V}
such that
a\notin s(B)
.

We say that
s_{0}
is
\tau
-dispersive (resp.
\tau
-rigid) on
X\subseteq\Omega
if it is
\tau
-dispersive (
\tau
-rigid) on all
a\in X
.
We say that
s
is
deeply
\bm{\tau}
-dispersive
on some
X\subseteq\Omega
if for every
n>1
there are
s_{1},\dots,s_{n}\in S
which are
\tau
-dispersive on
X
and such that
s_{1}\cdots s_{n}=s
.

Note that any
s_{0}\in S
is always
deeply
\tau
-dispersive on any point outside of
\im(s_{0})
. If we consider the above notions with respect to the topology of pointwise convergence, it is easy to see that any
s\in S
is
\tau_{\mathrm{pw}}
-rigid on any point of
\Omega
and not
\tau_{\mathrm{pw}}
-dispersive on any point in
\im(s_{0})
.

We collect a few easy facts about these notions.

Observation 5.9
.

Let
S\in\{G,\overline{G}\}
, and let
\tau
be a semigroup topology on
S
coarser than
\tau_{\mathrm{pw}}
.
Let
t,s\in S
. Then, for
a,a^{\prime}\in\Omega
,

1.

if
ts
is
\tau
-rigid on
a
, then
s
is also
\tau
-rigid on
a
;

2.

if
a^{\prime}=s(a)
and
ts
is
\tau
-rigid on
a
, then
t
is
\tau
-rigid on
a^{\prime}
;

3.

if
t
is
\tau
-dispersive on
a
, then
ts
is also
\tau
-dispersive on
a
;

4.

if
a=t(a^{\prime})
and
s
is
\tau
-dispersive on
a^{\prime}
, then
ts
is
\tau
-dispersive on
a
;

5.

if
s_{0}
is
\tau
-dispersive on
a
then, given
\mathcal{V}\in\mathcal{N}_{\tau}(s_{0})
and
B
as in the definition, the element in
s\in\mathcal{V}
witnessing
a\notin s(B)
can be assumed to be in
G
;

6.

if
s
is
\tau
-rigid on some
G
-invariant
\Delta\subseteq\Omega
and
a\in\mathrm{acl}(\Delta)\cap\im(s)
, then
s
is not
\tau
-dispersive on
a
.

Proof.

We begin with (
1
). Since
ts
is
\tau
-rigid on
a
, there is
\mathcal{V}\in\mathcal{N}_{\tau}(ts)
such that for all
r\in\mathcal{V}
,
r(a)\in\mathrm{acl}(ts(a))
. Since
\tau
is a semigroup topology, the function
\rho_{t}:S\to S
given by
u\mapsto tu
is continuous. In particular,
\mathcal{U}:=\rho_{r}^{-1}(\mathcal{V})
is an open neighbourhood of
s
. Note that for every
u\in\mathcal{U}
,
tu(a)\in\mathrm{acl}(ts(a))
, and since elements of
S
preserve algebraic closures,
u(a)\in\mathrm{acl}(s(a))
, proving that
s
is
\tau
-rigid on
a
, as desired. The proof of (
2
) is obtained analogously to that of (
1
) by considering the function
\lambda_{s}:S\to S
given by
u\mapsto us
.

Now, for (
3
) suppose that
t
is
\tau
-dispersive on
a
. Take
B
finite and
\mathcal{V}\in\mathcal{N}_{\tau}(ts)
. Then
\mathcal{U}:=\lambda_{s}^{-1}(\mathcal{V})
is a
\tau
-neighbourhood of
t
. Since
t
is
\tau
-dispersive, there is
u\in\mathcal{U}
such that
a\not\in u(s(B))
. But
us\in\mathcal{V}
by construction, yielding that
ts
is
\tau
-dispersive on
a
. Again, (
4
) is obtained analogously to (
3
) by considering
\rho_{t}
.

For (
5
), take
s\in\mathcal{V}
witnessing
\tau
-dispersivity on
a
with respect to
B
. Then, since
\tau
is coarser than
\tau_{\mathrm{pw}}
, there is
S_{\phi}
such that
s\in S_{\phi}\subseteq\mathcal{V}
, where
\phi\in\mathscr{P}(G)
. Now, take
\psi
to be the extension of
\phi
by
s_{\upharpoonright B}
. We still have that
s\in S_{\psi}\subseteq\mathcal{V}
. Moreover, by definition of the topology of pointwise convergence (and since every element of
S
agrees on each finite set with some element of
G
), we have that
S_{\psi}\cap G\neq\emptyset
. Hence, take
r\in S_{\psi}\cap G
as the witness of
\tau
-dispersivity on
a
. By construction,
a\not\in r(B)=s(B)
.

We conclude by proving (
6
). Let
a\in\mathrm{acl}(\Delta)\cap\im(s)
. Let
a=s(a^{\prime})
. Assume first that
a^{\prime}\in\Delta
(and hence
a\in\Delta
). Since
S
is locally approximated by
G
,
a=g(a^{\prime})
for some
g\in G
, and so, by
G
-invariance of
\Delta
,
s
is also
\tau
-rigid on
a^{\prime}
. Now, by
\tau
-rigidity on
a^{\prime}
, there is
\mathcal{V}\in\mathcal{N}_{\tau}(s)
such that for all
r\in\mathcal{V}
,
r(a^{\prime})\in\mathrm{acl}(s(a^{\prime}))=\mathrm{acl}(a)
. But then, letting
B=\{a^{\prime}\}
,
B
and
\mathcal{V}
witness that
s
is not
\tau
-dispersive on
a
. Now, consider the more general case where
a^{\prime}\in\mathrm{acl}(\Delta)
. Take
A\in[\Delta]^{<\omega}
such that
a^{\prime}\in\acl(A)
. For each
c\in A
, since
s
is
\tau
-rigid on
c
, there is some
\mathcal{V}_{c}\in\mathcal{N}_{\tau}(s)
such that
r(\overline{c})\in\mathrm{acl}(s(\overline{c}))
for each
r\in\mathcal{V}_{c}
. In particular, taking

\mathcal{V}_{A}:=\bigcap_{c\in A}\mathcal{V}_{c},

we have that for all
r\in\mathcal{V}_{A}
,
r(A)\subseteq\mathrm{acl}(s(A))
, and also
s\in\mathcal{V}_{A}
.
In particular, by local finiteness of algebraic closure, for all
r\in\mathcal{V}_{A}
,
r(\mathrm{acl}(A))=\mathrm{acl}(s(A))=s(\mathrm{acl}(A))
. Letting
B:=\mathrm{acl}(A)
, we have that for all
r\in\mathcal{V}_{A}
,
a\in r(B)
, yielding that
s
is
\tau
-dispersive on
a
.

∎

Remark 5.10
.

A consequence of (
3
) of Observation
5.9
is that if an element
g
of
G
is
\tau
-dispersive on some
X\subseteq\Omega
, then it is deeply
\tau
-dispersive on
X
. This is because if
g
is
\tau
-dispersive on a given
X
, by (
3
)
gg^{-1}=1
is
\tau
-dispersive on
X
. In particular,
1\cdots 1g=g
gives a decomposition of
g
as a product of elements of
S
which are
\tau
-dispersive on
X
, so that
g
is deeply
\tau
-dispersive.

The following lemma generalises the argument of
[
34
, Lemma 2.7]
from groups to semigroups. We include proofs for both groups and semigroups since even in the former case we remove some unnecessary and hidden assumptions of
[
34
, Lemma 2.7]
.

Lemma 5.11
.

Let
G\curvearrowright\Omega
be a closed permutation group with locally finite algebraic closure and let
S
be either
G
or
\overline{G}
. Then any semigroup topology
\tau
on
S
that is strictly coarser than
\tau_{\mathrm{pw}}
and for which every
s\in S
is
\tau
-rigid on
\Omega
is not a
T_{1}
topology.

Proof.

We give the proof for
\overline{G}
. The proof for
G
is essentially identical, and we explain at the end of the proof how to adapt the argument. Since
\tau_{\mathrm{pw}}\subsetneq\tau
, there is some
r\in S
and
\mathcal{V}\in\mathcal{N}_{\tau_{\mathrm{pw}}}(r)
such that for any
\mathcal{U}\in\mathcal{N}_{\tau}(l)
,
\mathcal{U}
is not a subset of
\mathcal{V}
. We may take without loss of generality
\mathcal{V}
to be of the form
S_{r_{\upharpoonright A_{0}}}
where
A_{0}
is some finite algebraically closed set. Take
A\supseteq A_{0}
finite and algebraically closed.
From our rigidity assumption, for every
a\in A
there is some
\mathcal{V}_{a}\in N_{\tau}(r)
such that
s(a)\in\mathrm{acl}(r(a))
for all
s\in\mathcal{V}_{a}
. In particular, we may take

\mathcal{V}_{A}:=\bigcap_{a\in A}\mathcal{V}_{a}\;.

Since
S=\overline{G}
, the elements of the semigroup
\overline{G}
send algebraically closed sets to algebraically closed sets,
r\in\mathcal{V}_{A}
and any
s\in\mathcal{V}_{A}
is such that
s_{\upharpoonright A}\in\mathrm{Bij}(A;r(A))
, where
\mathrm{Bij}(A;r(A))
is the set of bijections between
A
and
r(A)
. We define
\mathcal{B}_{A}
to be the minimal subset of
\mathrm{Bij}(A;r(A))
such that there is some
\mathcal{U}\in\mathcal{N}_{\tau}(r)
such that for all
t\in\mathcal{U}
,
t_{\upharpoonright A}\in\mathcal{B}_{A}
. Given the existence of
\mathcal{V}_{A}\in\mathcal{N}_{\tau}(r)
and finiteness of
\mathrm{Bij}(A;r(A))
,
\mathcal{B}_{A}
exists. Clearly,
|\mathcal{B}_{A}|\geqslant 1
because
r_{\upharpoonright A}\in\mathcal{B}_{A}
. Indeed,
|\mathcal{B}_{A}|>1
: otherwise, we would have that some
\mathcal{U}\in\mathcal{N}_{\tau}(r)
is such that
\mathcal{U}\subseteq S_{r_{\upharpoonright A}}\subseteq\mathcal{V}
, contradicting our original assumption. Thus, we define
\mathcal{B}_{A}^{\prime}:=\mathcal{B}_{A}\setminus\{r_{\upharpoonright A}\}
. Take a sequence
(A_{i})_{i\in\mathbb{N}}
of nested finite algebraically closed sets starting with
A_{0}
such that
\bigcup_{i\in\mathbb{N}}A_{i}=\Omega
. Consider
\mathcal{B}:=\bigcup_{i\in\mathbb{N}}\mathcal{B}^{\prime}_{A_{i}}
with its elements partially ordered by inclusion (i.e., for
\phi\in\mathcal{B}^{\prime}_{A_{i}},\psi\in\mathcal{B}^{\prime}_{A_{j}}
with
i<j
, we say that
\phi<\psi
if
\psi_{\upharpoonright A_{i}}=\phi
). By König’s tree lemma, we can find an infinite chain in this partial order. We let
\hat{r}
be the map
\Omega\to\Omega
obtained by the union of this infinite chain. By construction,
\hat{r}\in S
. Moreover, again by construction,
\hat{r}
is distinct from
l
and belongs to the
\tau_{\mathrm{pw}}
-closure (and so the
\tau
-closure) of any
\tau
-neighbourhood of
r
, yielding that
\tau
is not
T_{1}
since
\{\hat{r}\}
is a point which is not closed in
\tau
.

The proof for
G
is essentially identical: in this case, since we can compose with inverses, we can work with neighbourhoods of the identity (i.e.,
l=1
), meaning that
\mathcal{V}=S_{A_{0}}
, and
\mathcal{V}_{A}=S_{A}
. So, in our constructions the sets
\mathcal{B}_{A}
are sets of permutations of
A
. This, in particular, shows that the map
\hat{l}
constructed above is a permutation in
\mathrm{Sym}(\Omega)
. Since
G
is closed in
\mathrm{Sym}(\Omega)
, it follows that
\hat{l}\in G
, completing the proof.

∎

The fact below follows from a union of chains argument which is standard in model theory:

Fact 5.12
.

Let
\mathcal{M}
be a countable saturated structure and
\{\phi_{j}\}_{j\in J}
a countable collection of elementary isomorphisms between countable substructures
\mathcal{M}
for some countable set
J
. Then, there is a countable saturated elementary extension
\mathcal{M}^{\prime}
of
\mathcal{M}
such that each
\phi_{j}
extends to an automorphism of
\mathcal{M}^{\prime}
.

Proof.

This is a standard union of chains argument (see
[
60
, Chapters 2, 4, and 5]
for several arguments of this kind). For each
j\in J
let
A_{j}
and
B_{j}
be, respectively, the domain and codomain of
\phi_{j}
. Let
\mathcal{M}_{0}:=\mathcal{M}
. By compactness (and the Löwenheim-Skolem theorem) can build a countable elementary extension
\mathcal{M}_{1}\succeq\mathcal{M}_{0}
such that
\mathcal{M}_{1}
contains tuples of countable length
(\overline{b}^{1}_{j})_{j\in J}
such that for each
j\in J
,

\mathrm{tp}(\overline{b}^{1}_{j}/B_{j})=\mathrm{tp}(M_{0}/A_{j})\;,

for fixed enumerations of
M_{0},(A_{j})_{j\in J},(B_{j})_{j\in J}
. Note in particular that this correspondence of types yields elementary isomorphisms
(\phi^{1}_{j})_{j\in J}
where for each
j\in J
,
\phi^{1}_{j}:M_{0}\to\overline{b}^{1}_{j}
extends
\phi_{j}:A_{j}\to B_{j}
. Then, we can build a countable elementary extension
\mathcal{M}_{2}\succeq\mathcal{M}_{1}
containing tuples (again of countable length)
(\overline{a}^{2}_{j})_{j\in J}
such that for each
j\in J
,

\mathrm{tp}(\overline{a}^{2}_{j}/M_{0})=\mathrm{tp}(M_{1}/\overline{b}^{1}_{j})\;.

Again, for each
j\in J
, we get an elementary isomorphism
\phi^{2}_{j}:\overline{a}^{2}_{j}\to M_{1}
. We proceed inductively by back-and-forth building a sequence of countable elementary extensions

\mathcal{M}_{0}\preceq\mathcal{M}_{1}\preceq\cdots\mathcal{M}_{2i}\preceq\mathcal{M}_{2i+1}\preceq\cdots\;,

sequences
(\overline{a}_{j}^{2i})_{i\in\mathbb{N},j\in J}
and
(\overline{b}_{j}^{2i+1})_{i\in\mathbb{N},j\in J}
, and elementary isomorphisms
(\phi^{i}_{j})_{i\in\mathbb{N},j\in J}
, where for each
j\in J
,
i>1
,
\phi_{j}^{2i+1}:M_{2i}\to\overline{b}^{2i+1}_{j}
and
\phi_{j}^{2i}:\overline{a}^{2i}_{j}\to M_{2i-1}
, where for each
n\in\mathbb{N}
,
\phi_{j}^{n+1}
extends
\phi_{j}^{n}
. Take the union of this chain of elementary extensions
\mathcal{M}^{\prime}:=\bigcup_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\mathcal{M}_{n}
, and for each
j\in J
let
\phi_{j}^{\infty}:=\bigcup_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\phi_{j}^{n}
. Since
\mathcal{M}^{\prime}\succeq\mathcal{M}
is a countable union of countable models, it is still countable. Moreover, by construction,
\phi_{j}^{\infty}
is an automorphism of
\mathcal{M}^{\prime}
extending
\phi_{j}
for each
j\in J
, as desired.
∎

Lemma 5.13
.

Let
S\in\{G,\overline{G}\}
and
\tau
be a semigroup topology on
S
coarser than
\tau_{\mathrm{pw}}
. Fix
s_{0}\in S
. Assume either
s_{0}\in G
or hypothesis

(
♣
)

‣
3.2

holds. Let
a^{\prime}\in\Omega
. Suppose that
s_{0}
is not
\tau
-rigid on
a^{\prime}
. Then, there exists some
s_{1}\in S
which is
\tau
-dispersive on some
a\in(G\cdot a^{\prime})\cap\im(s_{1})
.
Moreover, if
s_{0}\in G
, we can take
s_{1}=s_{0}
and
a=s_{0}(a^{\prime})
.

Proof.

We prove the lemma by contrapositive. Consider first the case of
s_{0}\in G
,
s_{1}=s_{0}
and
a=s_{0}(a^{\prime})
. Suppose
s_{0}
is not
\tau
-dispersive on
a
.
Then, there are
B\in[\Omega]^{<\omega}
and
\mathcal{V}\in\mathcal{N}_{\tau}(s_{0})
such that
a\in s(B)
for all
s\in\mathcal{V}
.
Clearly, we may assume
a=s_{0}(a^{\prime})
for some
a^{\prime}\in B
.
By Neumann’s lemma there is
h\in G_{a}
with
h(B)\cap B=\mathrm{acl}(a^{\prime})
. Let

\mathcal{W}:=\mathcal{V}\cap\lambda^{-1}_{h}\rho^{-1}_{\tilde{h}^{-1}}(\mathcal{V})=\mathcal{V}\cap\{s\in S\,|\,\tilde{h}^{-1}sh\in\mathcal{V}\},

where
\tilde{h}=h^{s_{0}^{-1}}
, and for
t\in S
,
\rho_{t}
and
\lambda_{t}
are, respectively, the shift operations
u\mapsto tu
and
u\mapsto ut
. Note that
s_{0}\in\mathcal{W}
, since
\tilde{h}s_{0}h^{-1}=s_{0}
, so
\mathcal{W}
is in fact a neighbourhood of
s_{0}
in
\tau
.
It is also easy to check that
\tilde{h}\in G_{a}
.

For any
s\in\mathcal{W}
we must have

a\in s(B)
but also
a\in\tilde{h}^{-1}sh(B)
, since
\tilde{h}^{-1}sh\in\mathcal{V}
.
The latter inclusion implies
a=\tilde{h}(a)\in s(h(B))
.
We conclude that for all
s\in\mathcal{W}
we have

a\in s(B)\cap s(h(B))=s(B\cap h(B))=s(\mathrm{acl}(a^{\prime}))=\mathrm{acl}(s(a)).

(7)

The equality above implies that
\mathrm{acl}(a)=\mathrm{acl}_{G}(s(a))
, so that
s(a)\in\mathrm{acl}(a)
by
3.2
, so in fact
s_{0}
is
\tau
-rigid on
a
, as needed.

The case where we only assume

(
♣
)

‣
3.2
but possibly
s_{0}\notin G
is somewhat more involved. The previous argument does not work, since for
s_{0}\in S\setminus G
and
h\in G
an element
\bar{h}\in G
with
\bar{h}s_{0}h^{-1}=s_{0}
might not always exist; we cannot use conjugation to define such element, since
s_{0}
is not invertible. We solve this by recourse to
5.12
. Consider the collection
\mathcal{F}
of all pairs
(a^{\prime},B)
, where
B
is a finite subset of
\Omega
and
a^{\prime}\in\Omega
. For each
(a^{\prime},B)\in\mathcal{F}
, by Neumann’s Lemma we can find some
g_{a^{\prime},B}\in G
such that
g_{a^{\prime},B}(B)\cap B\subseteq\mathrm{acl}(a)
and let
\phi_{a^{\prime},B}
be the push-forward of
g_{a^{\prime},B}
by
s_{0}
, i.e., the elementary partial map given by the set of images under the action of
s_{0}
on pairs of the collection of pairs the map
g_{a^{\prime},B}
consists of. This collection is countable, so we can apply
5.12
. The uniqueness up to isomorphism of the saturated countable model allows us to interpret the conclusion of
5.12
as stating the existence of
t\in S
, as well as that of
\hat{g}_{a,B}\in G
for each
(a^{\prime},B)\in\mathcal{S}
with the property that
ts_{0}\cdot g_{a^{\prime},B}=\hat{g}_{a^{\prime},B}ts_{0}
for all
(a^{\prime},B)\in\mathcal{S}
.

Take
s_{1}=ts_{0}
and assume that
s_{1}
is not dispersive on
a^{*}=s_{1}(a^{\prime})=t(a)
, i.e., that there is
\mathcal{V}\in\mathcal{N}_{\tau}(s_{1})
and finite
B\subseteq\Omega
such that for all
s\in\mathcal{V}
we have
a\in s(B)
.
Along the lines of the previous case, we write

\mathcal{W}=\mathcal{V}\cap\hat{g}_{a^{\prime},B}\mathcal{V}g_{a^{\prime},B}^{-1},\quad\quad\mathcal{W}^{\prime}=\{s\in S\,|\,ts\in\mathcal{W}\}.

Note that
\hat{g}_{a^{\prime},B}^{-1}s_{1}g_{a^{\prime},B}=s_{1}\in\mathcal{V}
, so
\mathcal{W}\in\mathcal{N}_{\tau}(s_{1})
. Likewise, continuity of multiplication also yields
\mathcal{W}^{\prime}\in\mathcal{N}_{\tau}(s_{0})
. It can be checked, arguing as in the previous case, that
a\in s(g_{a^{\prime},B}(B)\cap B)\subseteq\mathrm{acl}(s(a))
for all
s\in\mathcal{W}^{\prime}
. Then, we can conclude that
s
is
\tau
-rigid on
a
as above.
∎

Lemma 5.14
.

Let
S\in\{G,\overline{G}\}
and
\tau
be a semigroup topology on
S
coarser than
\tau_{\mathrm{pw}}
.
Assume that either
s_{0}\in G
or hypothesis

(
♣
)

‣
3.2
holds. Let
s_{0}
be
\tau
-dispersive on
a=s_{0}(a^{\prime})
where
a^{\prime}\in\Omega
. Then there is
s_{1}\in S
which is
\tau
-dispersive on the whole
G\cdot a
. If
s_{0}\in G
, then we can conclude that any
g\in G
is
\tau
-dispersive on
G\cdot a
.

Proof.

Using
5.12
we can find
t\in S
and elements
g_{b},\hat{g}_{b}\in G
for

b\in\Omega

such that
g_{b}(a^{\prime})=b
and
\hat{g}_{b}ts_{0}g_{b}^{-1}=ts_{0}
for all
b\in G\cdot a
. Write
s_{1}=s_{0}t_{0}
. Since
s_{0}
is
\tau
-dispersive on
a
, it follows that
s_{1}
is
\tau
-dispersive on
t(a)
. We need to check that
\tau
is
\tau
-dispersive on every
c\in G\cdot t(a)=G\cdot a
. Choose any such
c
and let
b
be its preimage by
s_{1}
. Since,
s_{1}=\hat{g}_{b}s_{1}g_{b}^{-1}
, it follows from
5.9
that
\tau
is
\tau
-dispersive on the point

\hat{g}_{b}(t(a_{0}))=s_{1}(g_{b}(a^{\prime}))=s_{1}(b)=c.

Since
c
is arbitrary, we are done.

If
s_{0}\in G
then the property above is satisfied for
t=1
, so we can conclude that
s_{0}
is
\tau
-dispersive on
G\cdot a
.
Using
Item

3
in Observation
5.9
, one then easily concludes that any element of the group is
\tau
-dispersive on the entire
G\cdot a
.
∎

In the last two lemmas of this section, when working with
G
we mention generalised pointwise topologies. Note that the parts of the statements mentioning such topologies are only needed for
Theorem

6.8
and are not required for
Theorem

7.19
.

Lemma 5.15
.

Let
S
be either
G
or
\overline{G}
, where in the latter case

(
♣
)

‣
3.2
holds.
Let
\tau
be a semigroup topology on
S
coarser than
\tau_{\mathrm{pw}}
. Let
s\in S
be deeply
\tau
-dispersive on some
G
-invariant set
\Sigma\subseteq\Omega
.
Then, for every fixed
D\in\mathfrak{X}
and every
\mathcal{V}\in\mathcal{N}_{\tau}(s_{0})
there are
E\in\mathfrak{X}
and
h\in G
such that
S_{E}h\subseteq\mathcal{V}
and
E\cap D\cap\Sigma=\emptyset
.
Moreover, if the set
\Delta:=\Omega\setminus\Sigma
is algebraically closed,
s=1
and
\tau^{\Delta,K(\Delta)}\subseteq\tau
, then we may assume that
h\in G_{\{D\cap\Delta\}}
.

Proof.

Let
d_{1},\dots d_{m}
be an enumeration of
D\cap\Sigma
and write
s=s_{1}\cdots s_{2m}
, where each
s_{i}
is dispersive on
\Sigma
.
Using continuity of multiplication, choose
\mathcal{W}_{i}\in\mathcal{N}_{\tau}(s_{i})
such that
\mathcal{W}_{1}\cdots\mathcal{W}_{2m}\subseteq\mathcal{V}
.
Inductively, we find elements
h_{l}\in G\cap\mathcal{W}
and
E_{l}
,
1\leqslant l\leqslant m
, such that the following properties hold

1.

E_{l}\cap D\cap\Sigma\subseteq\{d_{l+1},\dots d_{m}\}
,

2.

S_{E_{l}}h_{l}\subseteq\mathcal{W}_{1}\cdots\mathcal{W}_{2l}
.

Taking
l=m
, and so
E:=E_{m}
, yields the desired conclusion.

Note that since
\tau
is assumed to be coarser than
\tau_{\mathrm{pw}}
, for every
1\leqslant l\leqslant m
there is
\alpha_{l}\in G
and
B_{l}\in\mathfrak{X}
such that
S_{B_{l}}\alpha_{l}\subseteq\mathcal{W}_{2l}
.

For the base case
l=1
we choose
h^{\prime}_{1}\in\mathcal{W}_{1}\cap G
with the property that
d_{1}\notin h_{1}^{\prime}(B_{1})
using the fact that
s_{1}
is
\tau
-dispersive on
\Sigma
. By
Lemma

5.1
we have

S_{h^{\prime}_{1}(B_{1})}h^{\prime}_{1}\alpha_{2}=h^{\prime}_{1}S_{B_{1}}\alpha_{1}\subseteq\mathcal{W}_{1}\mathcal{W}_{2}
, so that the property is satisfied by
E_{1}:=h_{1}(B_{2})
and
h_{1}:=h^{\prime}_{1}\alpha_{1}
.

For the induction step, assume that
h_{i},E_{i}
with the required properties are given for any
i
with
1\leqslant i\leqslant l
, where
1<l<m
. Using the fact that
s_{2l+1}
is
\tau
-dispersiveness of
s_{2l+1}
on
\Sigma
, one can find

g_{l+1}\in G\cap\mathcal{W}_{2l+1}
such that
h_{l}^{-1}(d_{l+1})\notin g_{l+1}(B_{l+1})
.
Applying
Lemma

5.1
once more, we get

S_{E_{l}}S_{h_{l}g_{l+1}(B)}h_{l}g_{l+1}\alpha_{l+1}=(S_{E_{l}}h_{l})g_{l+1}(S_{B_{l}}\alpha_{l+1})\subseteq(W_{1}\cdots\mathcal{W}_{2l})\cdot\mathcal{W}_{2l+1}\cdot\mathcal{W}_{2l+2}.

(8)

Lemma

5.6
applied with
h_{l}g_{l+1}
in place of
g
,
E_{l}
in place of
C
and
h_{l}g_{l+1}(B_{l+1})
in place of
D
yields
E_{l+1}\in\mathfrak{X}
and
h^{\prime}_{l+1}\in G
such that

•

S_{E_{l+1}}h^{\prime}_{l+1}\subseteq S_{E_{l}}S_{h_{l}g_{l+1}(B_{l+1})}
,

•

E_{l+1}\subseteq E_{l}\cap h_{l}g_{l+1}(B_{l+1})
, which implies

D\cap\Sigma\cap E_{l+1}\subseteq D\cap\Sigma\cap E_{l}\cap h_{l}g_{l+1}(B_{l+1})\subseteq\{d_{l+2},\dots d_{m}\},

where in the last inclusion we use
d_{l+1}\notin h_{l}g_{l+1}(B)
and the induction assumption
1
.

It follows from (
8
) that
S_{E_{l+1}}h^{\prime}_{l+1}h_{l}g_{l+1}\alpha_{l+1}\subseteq\mathcal{W}_{1}\cdots\mathcal{W}_{2l+2}
.
We can thus take
h_{l+1}:=h_{l}g_{l+1}\alpha_{l+1}
and the induction step is complete.

Finally, notice that if
s=1
and
\tau^{\Delta,K(\Delta)}\subseteq\tau
, then we could have chosen
\mathcal{V}
in such a way that that all the elements in
\mathcal{V}
stabilize
D\cap\Delta
setwise. Note that this inclusion only implies that preserving a class of the form
\mathrm{acl}(a)
,
a\in\Omega
that is an open condition, it is crucial for this step that
D\cap\Delta
is closed. The inclusion
S_{E}g
then implies that
h
must stabilize
D\cap\Delta
setwise (and also that
D\cap\Delta\subseteq E
).
∎

Lemma 5.16
.

Let
S
be either
G
or
\overline{G}
, where in the latter case

(
♣
)

‣
3.2
holds.
Let
\tau
be a semigroup topology on
S
coarser than
\tau_{\mathrm{pw}}
.
Let
\Delta\subseteq\Omega
be
G
-invariant and algebraically closed. Assume that
w
is deeply
\tau
-dispersive on
\Omega\setminus\Delta
.
Fix
k>0
and pick
\mathcal{U}\in\mathcal{N}_{\tau}(w^{2k+1})
. Then there are
h\in\mathcal{U}
and a chain of algebraically closed sets
\mathcal{E}=(\overline{e}_{i})_{i=0}^{k}
such that:

1.

\overline{e}_{i}\cap\overline{e}_{i+1}\subseteq\Delta
for all
0\leqslant i\leqslant k-1
,

2.

S_{\mathcal{E}}h\subseteq\mathcal{U}
.

If, moreover,
w=1
and
\tau^{\Delta,K(\Delta)}\subseteq\tau
, then we may assume

\overline{e}_{i}\cap\overline{e}_{i+1}=A
for some fixed
A\in\mathfrak{X}_{\Delta}
and for all
0\leqslant i\leqslant k-1
.

Proof.

Pick
\mathcal{V}\in\mathcal{N}_{\tau}(w)
such that
\mathcal{V}^{2k+1}\subseteq\mathcal{U}
.
Since
\tau\subseteq\tau_{\mathrm{pw}}
there are
D\in\mathfrak{X}
and
h_{0}\in\mathcal{V}
such that
S_{D}h_{0}\subseteq\mathcal{V}
.
Clearly, we may assume that
D\cap\Delta\in\mathfrak{X}
.

If
w=1
we may also assume
h_{0}=1
. If, furthermore
\tau^{\Delta,K(\Delta)}\subseteq\tau
, then we may choose some smaller
\mathcal{V}^{\prime}\subseteq\mathcal{V}
such that
s(B)=B
(as sets) for every
s\in\mathcal{V}
and every algebraically closed
B\subseteq D\cap\Delta
.
Otherwise, let
\mathcal{V}^{\prime}=\mathcal{V}
.

By
Lemma

5.15
there are
E\in\mathfrak{X}
and
h_{1}\in G
such that

•

S_{E}h_{1}\subseteq\mathcal{V}^{\prime}
for some
h_{1}\in\mathcal{V}^{\prime}
;

•

E\cap h_{0}^{-1}(D)\subseteq\Delta
.

By a repeating application of
Lemma

5.1
we obtain the equality below:

S_{\mathcal{C}}(h_{0}h_{1})^{k}h_{0}=S_{D}h_{0}(S_{E}h_{1}S_{D}h_{0})^{k}\subseteq\mathcal{V}^{2k+1},

where
\mathcal{C}=(\overline{c}_{i})_{i=0}^{2k}
is the chain given by

\overline{c}_{2i}=(h_{0}h_{1})^{i}(D),\,\,0\leqslant i\leqslant k;\quad\quad\overline{c}_{2i+1}=(h_{0}h_{1})^{i}h_{0}(E)\,\,0\leqslant i<k.

Notice that the inclusion
h_{0}^{-1}(D)\cap E\subseteq\Delta
implies

D\cap h_{0}(E)\subseteq\Delta
and thus
\overline{c}_{2i}\cap\overline{c}_{2i+1}\subseteq\Delta
for all
0\leqslant i\leqslant k
.

Claim:
Under the assumption that
w=1
and
\tau^{\Delta,K(\Delta)}\subseteq\tau
the intersection
\overline{c}_{2_{i}}\cap\overline{c}_{2i+1}\cap\overline{c}_{2i+2}
is a fixed subset of
D\cap\Delta
for all
0\leqslant i\leqslant k-1
.

Proof of Claim.

Here we take
h_{0}=1
so

\overline{c}_{2i}\cap\overline{c}_{2i+1}\cap\overline{c}_{2i+2}=h_{1}^{i}(D\cap E\cap h_{1}(D))=D\cap E\cap h_{1}(D),

where in the last equality we use the fact that
D\cap h_{1}(E)\cap h_{1}(D)\subseteq D\cap\Delta
and thus this set is setwise preserved by
h_{1}
.
∎

The conclusion of the lemma now follows with
h=(h_{0}h_{1})^{k}h_{0}
from an application of
Lemma

5.7
.
∎

6
Minimal semigroup topologies on automorphism groups of simple structures

In this section, we prove
Theorem

6.8
, generalising previous work of
[
34
]
. The reader interested in
Theorem

7.19
may skip this section with no loss of understanding, though this section may provide a “warm-up” for the latter result.

6.1
More on independence relations

Definition 6.1
.

Given
k\geqslant 1
, by an
independent chain
of length
k
we mean a chain of algebraically closed sets
\mathcal{C}=(\overline{c}_{0},\dots,\overline{c}_{k})
such that for all
1\leqslant i\leqslant k-1
we have

\overline{c}_{0}\dots\overline{c}_{i-1}\mathop{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\textstyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\scriptstyle\kern 2.80048pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 1.89871pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 2.80048pt}{\scriptscriptstyle\kern 1.42882pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 0.96873pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 1.42882pt}}_{\overline{c}_{i}}\overline{c}_{i+1}\dots\overline{c}_{k}.

We say that an independent
chain is
over
\bm{A}
, where
A\in\mathfrak{X}
, if
\overline{c}_{i}\cap\overline{c}_{i+1}=A
for all
0\leqslant i\leqslant k-1
. Note that we impose no specific conditions on how
A
sits inside the tuples.

The following is a standard observation.

Observation 6.2
.

For
(\overline{c}_{0},\overline{c}_{1},\dots,\overline{c}_{k})
where
k\geqslant 2
to be an independent chain it suffices to assume that for all
1\leqslant i\leqslant k-1
we have
\overline{c}_{0}\dots\overline{c}_{i-1}\mathop{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\textstyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\scriptstyle\kern 2.80048pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 1.89871pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 2.80048pt}{\scriptscriptstyle\kern 1.42882pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 0.96873pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 1.42882pt}}_{\overline{c}_{i}}\overline{c}_{i+1}
, or for all
0\leqslant i\leqslant k-2
we have
\overline{c}_{i}\mathop{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\textstyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\scriptstyle\kern 2.80048pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 1.89871pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 2.80048pt}{\scriptscriptstyle\kern 1.42882pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 0.96873pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 1.42882pt}}_{\overline{c}_{i+1}}\overline{c}_{i+2}\dots\overline{c}_{k}
.

It follows that for every chain
(\overline{c}_{i})_{i=0}^{k}
(over
A
) there is some independent chain
(\overline{c}^{\prime}_{i})_{i=0}^{k}
with
\overline{c}_{i}\overline{c}_{i+1}\equiv\overline{c}^{\prime}_{i}\overline{c}^{\prime}_{i+1}
(
\overline{c}_{i}\overline{c}_{i+1}\equiv_{A}\overline{c}^{\prime}_{i}\overline{c}^{\prime}_{i+1}
) for all
0\leqslant i\leqslant k-1
and
\overline{c}_{k}=\overline{c}^{\prime}_{k}
(alternatively, we can require
\overline{c}_{0}=\overline{c}^{\prime}_{0}
instead).

Definition 6.3
.

Let
\mathop{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\textstyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\scriptstyle\kern 2.80048pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 1.89871pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 2.80048pt}{\scriptscriptstyle\kern 1.42882pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 0.96873pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 1.42882pt}}
be an independence relation on
\Omega
. Let
k\geqslant 1
. We say that
\mathop{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\textstyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\scriptstyle\kern 2.80048pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 1.89871pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 2.80048pt}{\scriptscriptstyle\kern 1.42882pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 0.96873pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 1.42882pt}}
is
\bm{k}
-narrow
if it satisfies the following property:

•

(
k
-narrowness) for every
A\in\mathfrak{X}
and every independent chain,
\mathcal{C}=(\overline{c}_{0},\dots,\overline{c}_{k})
over
A
we have
\overline{c}_{0}\mathop{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\textstyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\scriptstyle\kern 2.80048pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 1.89871pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 2.80048pt}{\scriptscriptstyle\kern 1.42882pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 0.96873pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 1.42882pt}}_{A}\overline{c}_{k}
.

Note that
1
-narrowness is equivalent to strong one-basedness. Simple
\omega
-categorical not one-based Hrushovski constructions give examples of structures with a
3
-narrow notion of independence satisfying independent
3
-amalgamation
[
34
, Section 3E]
. The definition of
k
-narrowness is inspired by the definition of the failure of
k
-ampleness in the sense of
[
49
]
.

The usefulness of independence relations in our context stems from the following result.

Lemma 6.4
.

Let
G\curvearrowright\Omega
have locally finite algebraic closure and
\mathop{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\textstyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\scriptstyle\kern 2.80048pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 1.89871pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 2.80048pt}{\scriptscriptstyle\kern 1.42882pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 0.96873pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 1.42882pt}}
be an independence relation.
Assume furthermore that we are given some chain of algebraically closed sets
\mathcal{C}=(\overline{c}_{i})_{i=0}^{L}
for some
L\geqslant 2
such that for some
A\in\mathfrak{X}
we have

•

A\subseteq\overline{c}_{i}
for all
0\leqslant i\leqslant L
, and

•

\overline{c}_{i}\mathop{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\textstyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\scriptstyle\kern 2.80048pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 1.89871pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 2.80048pt}{\scriptscriptstyle\kern 1.42882pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 0.96873pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 1.42882pt}}_{A}\overline{c}_{i+1}
for all
0\leqslant i<L
.

Assume furthermore that one of the following possibilities holds:

(A)

We have
L=2
and
\mathop{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\textstyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\scriptstyle\kern 2.80048pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 1.89871pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 2.80048pt}{\scriptscriptstyle\kern 1.42882pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 0.96873pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 1.42882pt}}
satisfies stationarity.

(B)

We have
L=3
and
\mathop{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\textstyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\scriptstyle\kern 2.80048pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 1.89871pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 2.80048pt}{\scriptscriptstyle\kern 1.42882pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 0.96873pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 1.42882pt}}
satisfies independent
3
-amalgamation.

Then
G_{\mathcal{C}}=G_{A}
.

Proof.

We wish to apply
5.2
, according to which
G_{\mathcal{C}}
equals the collection of all elements
s
of
G
for which there is a chain of algebraically closed sets
(\overline{c}_{i}^{\prime})_{i=0}^{L}
where
\overline{c}^{\prime}_{0}=\overline{c}_{0}
,
\overline{c}^{\prime}_{L}=s(\overline{c}_{L})
, and

\overline{c}_{i}\overline{c}_{i+1}\equiv_{A}\overline{c}^{\prime}_{i}\overline{c}^{\prime}_{i+1}
for all
0\leqslant i<L
.

Fix some
s\in G_{A}
. To begin with, notice that by full existence we can find some
\overline{c}^{\prime}_{1}\in\mathfrak{X}
such that
\overline{c}^{\prime}_{1}\equiv_{\overline{c}_{0}}\overline{c}_{1}
and

s(\overline{c}_{L})\mathop{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\textstyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\scriptstyle\kern 2.80048pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 1.89871pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 2.80048pt}{\scriptscriptstyle\kern 1.42882pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 0.96873pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 1.42882pt}}_{\overline{c}_{0}}\overline{c}^{\prime}_{1}
. Since by assumption
\overline{c}_{0}\mathop{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\textstyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\scriptstyle\kern 2.80048pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 1.89871pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 2.80048pt}{\scriptscriptstyle\kern 1.42882pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 0.96873pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 1.42882pt}}_{A}\overline{c}_{1}
, by invariance and the assumption that
s\in G_{A}
we also have
\overline{c}_{0}\mathop{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\textstyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\scriptstyle\kern 2.80048pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 1.89871pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 2.80048pt}{\scriptscriptstyle\kern 1.42882pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 0.96873pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 1.42882pt}}_{A}\overline{c}^{\prime}_{1}
. An application of transitivity yields
\overline{c}_{0}s(\overline{c}_{L})\mathop{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\textstyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\scriptstyle\kern 2.80048pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 1.89871pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 2.80048pt}{\scriptscriptstyle\kern 1.42882pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 0.96873pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 1.42882pt}}_{A}\overline{c}_{1}^{\prime}
, from which one can derive, using monotonicity and symmetry, the following relation

\overline{c}_{1}^{\prime}\mathop{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\textstyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\scriptstyle\kern 2.80048pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 1.89871pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 2.80048pt}{\scriptscriptstyle\kern 1.42882pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 0.96873pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 1.42882pt}}_{A}s(\overline{c}_{L}).

(9)

Case
(A)
.
In this case, we claim that
(\overline{c}_{0},\overline{c}^{\prime}_{1},s(\overline{c}_{2}))
is a suitable chain between
\overline{c}_{0}
and
s(\overline{c}_{2})
. Indeed, by assumption

\overline{c}_{1}\mathop{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\textstyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\scriptstyle\kern 2.80048pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 1.89871pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 2.80048pt}{\scriptscriptstyle\kern 1.42882pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 0.96873pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 1.42882pt}}_{A}\overline{c}_{2},\quad\quad\overline{c}_{1}^{\prime}\equiv_{A}\overline{c}_{1},\quad\quad s(\overline{c}_{2})\equiv_{A}\overline{c}_{2},

which together with (
9
) allows to apply stationarity to conclude
\overline{c}^{\prime}_{1}s(\overline{c}_{2})\equiv_{A}\overline{c}_{1}\overline{c}_{2}
.

Case
(B)
.
Here we take
L=3
. We claim we can find
\overline{c}^{\prime}_{2}
with
\overline{c}_{1}^{\prime}\overline{c}_{2}^{\prime}\equiv_{A}\overline{c}_{1}\overline{c}_{2}
and
\overline{c}_{2}^{\prime}\overline{c}^{\prime}_{3}\equiv_{A}\overline{c}_{2}\overline{c}_{3}
, as a result of an application of independent
3
-amalgamation, which means
(\overline{c}_{0},\overline{c}^{\prime}_{1},\overline{c}^{\prime}_{2},s(\overline{c}_{3}))
is the needed chain.
The corresponding assumptions are listed below:

•

\overline{c}_{1}^{\prime}\equiv_{A}\overline{c}_{1}
by construction, since
a\subseteq\overline{c}_{0}
;

•

s(\overline{c}_{3})\equiv_{A}\overline{c}_{3}
, since
s\in G_{A}
;

•

\overline{c}_{2}\mathop{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\textstyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\scriptstyle\kern 2.80048pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 1.89871pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 2.80048pt}{\scriptscriptstyle\kern 1.42882pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 0.96873pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 1.42882pt}}_{A}\overline{c}_{1}
, by assumption and symmetry;

•

\overline{c}_{2}\mathop{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\textstyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\scriptstyle\kern 2.80048pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 1.89871pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 2.80048pt}{\scriptscriptstyle\kern 1.42882pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 0.96873pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 1.42882pt}}_{A}\overline{c}_{3}
, by assumption;

•

\overline{c}^{\prime}_{1}\mathop{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\textstyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\scriptstyle\kern 2.80048pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 1.89871pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 2.80048pt}{\scriptscriptstyle\kern 1.42882pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 0.96873pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 1.42882pt}}_{A}s(\overline{c}_{3})
, which is just (
9
) with
L=3
.

∎

Corollary 6.5
.

In the setting of
Lemma

6.4
(with the same
L\in\{2,3\}
), assume furthermore that
\mathop{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\textstyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\scriptstyle\kern 2.80048pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 1.89871pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 2.80048pt}{\scriptscriptstyle\kern 1.42882pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 0.96873pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 1.42882pt}}
satisfies
k
-narrowness for some
k\geqslant 1
. Then for every chain
\mathcal{C}=(\overline{c}_{i})_{i=0}^{kL}
for which
\overline{c}_{i}\cap\overline{c}_{i+1}=A
for some fixed
A\in\mathfrak{X}
and all
0\leqslant i\leqslant kL-1
we have
G_{A}\subseteq G_{\mathcal{C}}
.

Proof.

By
6.2
there is an independent chain
\mathcal{C}^{\prime}=(\overline{c}^{\prime}_{i})_{i=0}^{k}
with
\overline{c}^{\prime}_{i}\overline{c}^{\prime}_{i+1}\equiv_{A}\overline{c}_{i}\overline{c}_{i+1}
for all
0\leqslant i<k
and
\overline{c}^{\prime}_{k}=\overline{c}_{k}
. By
Corollary

5.4
we have that
G_{\mathcal{C}^{\prime}}=gG_{\mathcal{C}}
for some
g\in G_{A}
. It thus suffices to prove that
G_{A}\subseteq G_{\mathcal{C}^{\prime}}
.

Since
\mathop{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\textstyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\scriptstyle\kern 2.80048pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 1.89871pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 2.80048pt}{\scriptscriptstyle\kern 1.42882pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 0.96873pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 1.42882pt}}
is
k
-narrow, we have that
\overline{c}^{\prime}_{ki}\mathop{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\textstyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\scriptstyle\kern 2.80048pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 1.89871pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 2.80048pt}{\scriptscriptstyle\kern 1.42882pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 0.96873pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 1.42882pt}}_{A}\overline{c}^{\prime}_{k(i+1)}
for all
0\leqslant i\leqslant L-1
. The conclusion can now be obtained by a direct application of
Lemma

6.4
to the chain
(\overline{c}^{\prime}_{ik})_{i=0}^{L}
, since
G_{(\overline{c}^{\prime}_{ik})_{i=0}^{L}}\subseteq G_{\mathcal{C}}
.
∎

6.2
Semigroup topologies on automorphism groups of simple structures

Suppose that
G
is a closed group of permutations of an infinite set
\Omega
with locally finite algebraic closure. The following is immediate from the definition of a generalized topology.

Observation 6.6
.

Let
\Delta
be a
G
-invariant subset of
\Omega
. Then
\tau_{\mathrm{pw}}^{\Delta,K(\Delta)}
is the coarsest semigroup topology
\tau
such that every
g\in G
is
\tau
-rigid on
\Delta
. Given any semigroup topology
\tau
on
G
coarser than
\tau_{\mathrm{pw}}
, consider the set
\Delta_{\tau}
consisting of all the
a\in\Omega
on which every
g\in G
is
\tau
-rigid. Then:

(i)

\Delta_{\tau}
is the largest
\Delta\subseteq\Omega

such that
\tau_{\mathrm{pw}}^{\Delta,K(\Delta)}\subseteq\tau
;

(ii)

\Delta_{\tau}
agrees with the set of elements
a\in\Omega
such that some
g\in G
is
\tau
-rigid on
a
;

(iii)

\Delta_{\tau}
is
G
-invariant.

Proof.

Since
G
is a group, for the first statement it is sufficient to prove that
\tau_{\mathrm{pw}}^{\Delta,K(\Delta)}
is minimal amongst semigroup topologies such that
1
is
\tau
-rigid on
\Delta
. Pick
A\in\mathfrak{X}_{\Delta}
and consider the basic neighbourhood
G_{A}^{K(\Delta)}
of the identity in
\tau_{\mathrm{pw}}^{\Delta,K(\Delta)}
. Then, we have that

G_{A}^{K(\Delta)}=\{g\in G\ |\ \text{ for all }a\in A,\ g(a)\in[a]\}\;,

where the left-to-right containment is obvious and the right-to-left containment follows from the definitions of
K(\Delta)
and
G(\Delta)
. Moreover, from the above characterisation we can see that for any semigroup topology
\tau
such that the identity
1
is
\tau
-rigid on
\Delta
,
\tau_{\mathrm{pw}}^{\Delta,K(\Delta)}\subseteq\tau
.

Let us move to the second statement.
Claim
(i)
on
\Delta_{\tau}
follows immediately from the above discussion.
For claims
(ii)
and
(iii)
we reason as follows. Suppose that we are given
g\in G
and
a\in\Omega
such that
g
is
\tau
-rigid on
a
. We use
5.9
, parts
1
and
2
to first conclude that
1
is
\tau
-rigid on some point
a^{\prime}\in G\cdot a
. Since
1=1^{h}
for all
h\in G
, another application of those statements allows us to conclude that
1
is
\tau
-rigid on the entire orbit
G\cdot a
.
One last application of either part
1
or
2
of the same Observation allows us to conclude that every
h\in G
is
\tau
-rigid on the entire
G\cdot a
. This establishes
(i)
and
(ii)
.

∎

Lemma 6.7
.

Let
G\curvearrowright\Omega
be a closed permutation group with locally finite algebraic closure. Let
\tau
be a semigroup topology on
G
coarser than
\tau_{\mathrm{pw}}
, and
\Delta_{\tau}
the
G
-invariant set given by
6.6
.
Let
K_{\tau}
be the collection of elements
\alpha\in K(\Delta_{\tau})
such that for every
\mathcal{V}\in\mathcal{N}_{\tau}(1)
and every
A\in\mathfrak{X}_{\Delta_{\tau}}
, there exists
h\in\mathcal{V}
with
h_{\restriction A}=\alpha_{\restriction A}
.
Then
K_{\tau}
is closed, and it is the smallest
K
such that
K\unlhd G(\Delta_{\tau})
,
K\leqslant K(\Delta_{\tau})
, and
\tau_{\mathrm{pw}}^{\Delta_{\tau},K}\subseteq\tau
.

Proof.

For any
A\in\mathfrak{X}_{\Delta_{\tau}}
write
G[A]
for the image of
G_{\{A\}}
on the permutation group
\mathrm{Sym}(A)

by the restriction map. Given any
K\leqslant K(\Delta_{\tau})
we define

K_{\restriction A}:=\{\phi\in G[A]\,|\,\exists g\in K,\;g_{\restriction A}=\phi\}.

We also write:

K_{\tau}(A):=\{\phi\in G[A]\,|\,\,\forall\mathcal{V}\in\mathcal{N}_{1}(\tau)\,\,\exists\,s\in\mathcal{V}\,\,s_{\restriction A}=\phi\}.

Suppose
K
is such that
\tau_{\mathrm{pw}}^{\Delta_{\tau},K}
is a well-defined generalized pointwise semigroup topology coarser than
\tau
.
It is not hard to see that
K_{\tau}(A)\leqslant K_{\upharpoonright A}

for all
A\in\mathfrak{X}_{\Delta_{\tau}}
. Since
K
is closed for the pointwise convergence topology and
K_{\tau}
is defined as the subset consisting of those
g\in K(\Delta_{\tau})
(or, equivalently
g\in G(\Delta_{\tau})
) with
g_{\restriction A}\in K_{\tau}(A)
, one easily concludes that
K_{\tau}\leqslant K
.
Therefore, all is left to show is that
K_{\tau}
satisfies all the conditions needed for
(\Delta_{\tau},K_{\tau})
to define a generalized pointwise topology.

To begin with, it is immediate from the construction that
K_{\tau}
is closed, and
K_{\tau}\subseteq K(\Delta_{\tau})
, by the definition of
\Delta_{\tau}
. It remains to show that
K_{\tau}
is a subgroup of
K(\Delta_{\tau})
and normal in
G(\Delta_{\tau})
.

For the first, note that continuity of multiplication at the identity easily implies that
K_{\tau}(A)
is a subsemigroup of
G[A]
. Indeed, given
\phi,\psi\in K_{\tau}(A)
and
\mathcal{V}\in\mathcal{N}_{1}(\tau)
we can choose
\mathcal{W}\in\mathcal{N}_{1}(\tau)
with
\mathcal{W}^{2}\subseteq\mathcal{V}
and
s,t\in\mathcal{W}
with
s_{\restriction A}=\phi
,
s_{\restriction A}=\psi
. Then
st_{\restriction A}=\phi\psi
. Since any subsemigroup of a finite group is necessarily a subgroup, as the inverse of any element in a finite group can be written as a positive power of that element, necessarily
K_{\tau}(A)
is a subgroup of
G[A]
for all
A\in\mathfrak{X}_{\Delta_{\tau}}
, and hence
K_{\tau}\leqslant K(\Delta_{\tau})
.
Using continuity of conjugation by a single fixed element at the identity in
\tau
, i.e., the invariance of the family
\mathcal{N}_{\tau}(1)
under conjugation by any fixed element of
G
, one can easily show that
gK_{\tau}(A)g^{-1}=K_{\tau}(g(A))
. We conclude from this that
K_{\tau}
is normal in
G(\Delta_{\tau})
, finishing the proof of the lemma.

∎

Theorem 6.8
.

Let
G\curvearrowright\Omega
be a closed permutation group with locally finite algebraic closure.
Let
