\mathop{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\textstyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\scriptstyle\kern 2.80048pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 1.89871pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 2.80048pt}{\scriptscriptstyle\kern 1.42882pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 0.96873pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 1.42882pt}}
be an independence relation satisfying the following properties:

(I)

k
-narrowness for some
k\geqslant 1
; and

(II)

either stationarity or independent
3
-amalgamation from Definition
3.18
holds.

Then every semigroup topology on
G
that is coarser that
\tau_{\mathrm{pw}}
is of the form
\tau_{\mathrm{pw}}^{\Delta_{\tau},K_{\tau}}
for some closed invariant
\Delta_{\tau}\subseteq\Omega
and some
K_{\tau}\unlhd G(\Delta_{0})
,
K_{0}\leqslant K(\Delta_{0})
.

Proof.

Let
\tau
be a semigroup topology on
G
coarser than
\tau_{\mathrm{pw}}=\tau_{\mathrm{pw}}^{\Omega,\{1\}}
. Let
\Delta_{\tau}
and
K_{\tau}
be the
G
-invariant subset of
\Omega
and the subgroup
K_{\tau}\leqslant K(\Delta_{\tau})
provided by
6.6
and
Lemma

6.7
, respectively.

We need to prove the following two facts, which conclude the proof of the theorem:

•

\Delta_{\tau}
is algebraically closed,

•

\tau_{\mathrm{pw}}^{K_{\tau},\Delta_{\tau}}=\tau
.

We write
\Sigma
for the set
\Omega\setminus\Delta_{\tau}
. Note that by part
(ii)
of
6.6
no element of
G
is
\tau
-rigid on some
a\in\Sigma
.
It follows from
Lemma

5.13
and
Lemma

5.14
that every element of
G
is
\tau
-dispersive on the entire
\Sigma
. By claim
6
in
5.9
no element
g\in G
can be
\tau
-dispersive on any

a\in\mathrm{acl}(\Delta_{\tau})
. Therefore,
\Delta_{\tau}
is algebraically closed.

It remains to show the inclusion
\tau\subseteq{\tau_{\mathrm{pw}}^{\Delta_{\tau},K_{\tau}}}
, which is equivalent to the assertion that every
\mathcal{U}\in\mathcal{N}_{\tau}(1)
contains some element of
\mathcal{N}_{{\tau_{\mathrm{pw}}^{\Delta_{\tau},K_{\tau}}}}(1)
.

Claim:
Every
\mathcal{U}\in\mathcal{N}_{\tau}(1)
contains some element of
\mathcal{N}_{{\tau_{\mathrm{pw}}^{\Delta_{\tau},\{1\}}}}(1)
.

Proof of Claim.

Since all elements of
g
are all
\tau
-dispersive on
\Sigma
, clearly they are all deeply
\tau
-dispersive on
\Sigma
. We can therefore apply
Lemma

5.15
.

Let
L\in\{2,3\}
be the constant from
Lemma

6.4
, and let
\mathcal{U}\in\mathcal{N}_{\tau}(1)
be arbitrary. Applying
Lemma

5.16
with
w=1
, we obtain a closed set
A\subset\Delta_{\tau}
, an element

h\in G_{A}
, and a chain of algebraically closed sets
\mathcal{E}=(\overline{e}_{j})_{j=0}^{kL}
such that

•

G_{\mathcal{E}}h\subseteq\mathcal{U}
,

•

\overline{e}_{i}\cap\overline{e}_{i+1}=A
for all
0\leqslant i\leqslant kL-1
.

It now follows from
Corollary

6.5
(used in the first inequality) that

G_{A}=G_{A}h\subseteq G_{\mathcal{E}}h\subseteq\mathcal{U},

as required.
∎

To conclude, take an arbitrary
\mathcal{U}\in\mathcal{N}_{\tau}(1)
and choose
\mathcal{V}\in\mathcal{N}_{\tau}(1)
such that
\mathcal{V}^{2}\subseteq\mathcal{U}
. By the claim above,
G_{A}\subseteq\mathcal{V}
for some
A\in\mathfrak{X}_{\Delta_{\tau}}
.
It remains to show that for every
h\in K_{\tau}
there exists
s\in\mathcal{V}

such that
s_{\restriction A}=h_{\restriction A}
, since this clearly implies
G^{K_{\tau}}_{A}\subseteq\mathcal{V}^{2}\subseteq\mathcal{U}
, where
G^{K_{\tau}}_{A}
is as defined in
Definition

3.26
. This follows from the final clause in
Lemma

6.7
.
∎

Proof of
C
.

One only needs to recall the statement from the preliminaries that if
G\curvearrowright\Omega
is the automorphism group of a countable saturated structure with locally finite algebraic closure, weak elimination of imaginaries, and whose theory is simple, then non-forking independence is strongly one-based (and so
1
-narrow) and satisfies independent
3
-amalgamation. Note that if
G
is transitive, then
\Delta=\Omega
in
Theorem

6.8
, and in particular every generalized pointwise topology of the form occurring in
Theorem

6.8
is such that every
s\in G
is
\tau
-rigid on
\Omega
. By
Lemma

5.11
,
\tau_{\mathrm{pw}}
is minimal amongst
T_{1}
semigroup topologies on
G
.
∎

7
Minimality of
\tau_{\mathrm{pw}}
for monoids of elementary embeddings

7.1
Sinks

Definition 7.1
.

Let
G
be a group of permutations of an infinite set
\Omega
with locally finite closure, and let
S=\overline{G}
.
We say that
\Omega^{\prime}\subset\Omega
, is a
k
-sink
for
S
for
k\geqslant 1
if the following conditions are satisfied for every
G
-invariant and algebraically closed
\Delta\subseteq\Omega
:

1.

there are distinct
u,u^{\prime}\in S
such that
\Omega^{\prime}=\{a\in\Omega\,|\,u(a)=u^{\prime}(a)\}
(in particular,
\Omega^{\prime}\subsetneq\Omega
);

2.

for every chain of algebraically closed sets
\mathcal{C}=(\overline{c}_{i})_{i=0}^{k}
such that
\overline{c}_{i}\cap\overline{c}_{i+1}\subseteq\Delta
, for
0\leqslant i\leqslant k-1
, there is
u\in S_{\mathcal{C}}
such that
\im(u)\subseteq\Omega^{\prime}
;

3.

there is
v\in S
such that
\im(v)\cap\Omega^{\prime}=v(\Delta)
.

The following is a very useful observation from
[
52
]
, which follows from the fact that in a Hausdorff space
X
the diagonal
\Delta\subseteq X^{2}
is closed.

Observation 7.2
.

In the situation of
Definition

7.1
, if
\tau
is a Hausdorff semigroup topology on
S
and
\Omega^{\prime}\subseteq\Omega
satisfies property
1
, then for for distinct
u,u^{\prime}\in S
the set

\{s\in S\,|\,\im(s)\nsubseteq\Omega^{\prime}\,\}=\{s\in S\,|\,us\neq u^{\prime}s\}

is open in
\tau
.

Remark 7.3
.

Note that if
\Omega^{\prime}\subset\Omega
is a
k
-sink, then it is an
n
-sink for all
n\geqslant k
. To see this, we only need to verify that condition
2
in Definition
7.1
is still satisfied for
n\geqslant k
. Suppose that we have a chain of algebraically closed sets
\mathcal{C}=(\overline{c}_{i})_{i=0}^{n}
. Then, we can consider the subchain
\mathcal{D}:=(\overline{c}_{i})_{i=0}^{k}
. Since
\Omega^{\prime}
is a
k
-sink, there is
u\in S_{\mathcal{D}}=S_{\overline{c}_{0}}\cdots S_{\overline{c}_{k}}
such that
\im(u)\subseteq\Omega^{\prime}
. Clearly,
u\in S_{\mathcal{C}}
as well since the identity belongs to each of the
S_{\overline{c}_{l}}
for
l\geqslant k
. Hence, condition
2
is satisfied.

The following is a standard union of chains argument (cf.
[
46
, Lemma 4.5]
):

Lemma 7.4
.

Let
\mathcal{M}
be a countable saturated structure and let
S
be its monoid of elementary embeddings.
Let
(s_{l})_{l\in\mathbb{N}}
be a countable sequence of elements from
S
. Then, there is
w\in S
such that for each
m\geqslant 1
there is
t_{m}\in S
such that
w=t_{m}s_{m-1}s_{m-2}\dots s_{0}
.

Proof.

We construct an elementary chain of countable models
\mathcal{M}:=\mathcal{M}_{0}\preceq\mathcal{M}_{1}\dots
, as well as isomorphisms
\alpha_{l}:\mathcal{M}_{l}\to\mathcal{M}
with the property that
\alpha_{l+1}\iota_{l}=s_{l}\alpha_{l}

for all
l\in\mathbb{N}
, where
\iota_{l}
is the inclusion of
\mathcal{M}_{l}
into
\mathcal{M}_{l+1}
. First take
\mathcal{M}_{0}=\mathcal{M}
and let
\alpha_{0}
be an arbitrary isomorphism of
\mathcal{M}_{0}
. For the induction step, assume that we have built
(\alpha_{i})_{i\leqslant l}
and
(\mathcal{M}_{i})_{i\leqslant l}
satisfying the desired properties. Now let
\mathcal{M}_{l+1}
be an elementary extension of
\mathcal{M}_{l}
so that the inclusion
\iota_{l}
, is isomorphic over
\alpha_{l}
to the embedding
s_{l}
, i.e.
there is an isomorphism
\alpha_{l+1}:\mathcal{M}_{l+1}\to\mathcal{M}
such that
\alpha_{l+1}\iota_{l}=\alpha_{l}\iota_{l}
.

Finally, consider the union
\mathcal{M}^{\prime}:=\bigcup_{l\in\mathbb{N}}\mathcal{M}_{l}
. This is countable, elementary equivalent to
\mathcal{M}
and also saturated, since each of the
\mathcal{M}_{k}
is. Uniqueness of countable saturated models guarantees the existence of an isomorphism
\alpha:\mathcal{M}^{\prime}\to\mathcal{M}
.

Let
\iota_{k,\infty}
is the inclusion of
\mathcal{M}_{k}
into
\mathcal{M}^{\prime}
. We check that
w:=\alpha\iota_{0,\infty}\alpha_{0}^{-1}
satisfies the desired property. Indeed, fix
m\geqslant 2
and consider the map
t_{k}=\alpha\iota_{k,\infty}\alpha_{k}^{-1}
. Being a composition of elementary embeddings,

t_{k}
is an elementary embedding. Moreover:

t_{k}s_{m-1}s_{m-2}\cdots s_{0}=\alpha\iota_{k,\infty}\alpha_{k}^{-1}(\alpha_{k}\iota_{k-1}\alpha_{k-1}^{-1})(\alpha_{k-1}\iota_{k-2}\alpha_{k-1})\cdots(\alpha_{1}\iota_{0}\alpha_{0}^{-1})=\alpha\iota_{0,\infty}\alpha_{0}^{-1}=w.

∎

Our reason for caring about
k
-sinks is that their existence implies minimality of
\tau_{\mathrm{pw}}
:

Proposition 7.5
.

Let
G
be the automorphism group of a saturated countable structure
\mathcal{M}
with locally finite algebraic closure and let
S=\overline{G}
. If
\mathcal{M}
admits a
k
-sink for some
k\geqslant 1
, then
\tau_{\mathrm{pw}}
is a minimal Hausdorff semigroup topology on
S
.

Proof.

Let
\tau
be a Hausdorff semigroup topology strictly coarser than
\tau_{\mathrm{pw}}
. Let

\Delta:=\{a\in\Omega\ |\text{ every }s\in S\text{ is }\tau\text{-rigid on }a\}\;.

Lemma

5.11
implies that
\Delta\subsetneq\Omega
. Write
\Sigma=\Omega\setminus\Delta
and let
\{\Sigma_{j}\}_{j\in J}
for the (necessarily countable) partition of
\Sigma
into orbits under the action of
G
.

Claim:
There is
w^{\prime}\in S
which is deeply
\tau
-dispersive on the entire
\Sigma
.

Proof of claim.

Fix first some
j\in J
. Since
\Sigma_{j}\subseteq\Omega\setminus\Delta
, there is some
t_{j}\in S
which is not
\tau
-rigid on some point
a_{j}\in\Sigma_{j}
.
An application of
Lemma

5.13
implies the existence of
t^{\prime}_{j}\in S
which is
\tau
-dispersive on some point
a^{\prime}_{j}\in\Sigma_{j}
. Finally
Lemma

5.14
yields the existence of some
s_{j}\in S
which is
\tau
-dispersive on every
a\in\Sigma_{j}
.

Using
Lemma

7.4
we can construct an element
s_{J}\in S
with the property that for every
j\in J
there are
u_{j},v_{j}\in S
yielding a decomposition of the form
s_{J}=u_{j}s_{j}v_{j}
(here the elements
v_{j}
are just sequences of the form
s_{j_{m}}s_{j_{m-1}}\dots s_{j_{0}}
under some ordering of
J
). By virtue of
5.9
, we conclude that
w^{\prime}
is
\tau
dispersive on
\Sigma
. To conclude, we can use
Lemma

7.4
with respect to the constant sequence of countably many copies of
s_{J}
to find
w^{\prime}\in S
with the property that
w^{\prime}
admits a decomposition of the form
w^{\prime}=t_{m}s_{J}^{m}
for all
m\geqslant 1
. By applying
5.9
to the term
t_{m}s_{J}
it is clear that
w^{\prime}
is deeply
\tau
-dispersive on
\Sigma
.
∎

It follows immediately from this that
\Delta
is algebraically closed (see item
6
in
5.9
). Let now
\Omega^{\prime}
be a
k
-sink for
S
, for some
k\geqslant 2
. By property
3

of the definition of being a sink, there is
v\in S
such that
\im(v)\cap\Omega^{\prime}=v(\Delta)
.
Since
\Delta
is
G
-invariant, it follows that this property also holds for any element of the form
vv^{\prime}
instead of
v
, where
v^{\prime}\in S
.
3
3

3

Indeed,
vv^{\prime}(\Delta)\subseteq v(\Delta)
, and similarly
v(v^{\prime}(\Omega\setminus\Delta))\supseteq v(\Omega\setminus\Delta)\subseteq\Omega\setminus\Omega^{\prime}
.

In particular this holds for any power of
w=vw^{\prime}
. This implies that for all
m
we have

\mathcal{U}=\{s\in S\,|\,\im(s)\nsubseteq\Omega^{\prime}\}\in\mathcal{N}_{\tau}(w^{m}),

a set which we know to be open, by
7.2
. Take
\mathcal{U}^{\prime}\in\mathcal{N}_{\tau}(w)
and
\mathcal{U}^{\prime\prime}\in\mathcal{N}_{\tau}(w^{2k+1})
such that
\mathcal{U}^{\prime}\mathcal{U}^{\prime\prime}\subseteq\mathcal{U}
.

Take
\mathcal{V}\in\mathcal{N}_{\tau}(w)
such that
w\mathcal{V}^{2k+1}\subseteq\mathcal{U}
. The fact that
w
is deeply
\tau
-dispersive on
\Sigma
, together with
Lemma

5.16
yields the existence of some
h\in G
and some chain of algebraically closed sets
\mathcal{E}^{\prime}=(\overline{e}^{\prime}_{j})_{j=0}^{k}
such that

•

\overline{e}^{\prime}_{j}\cap\overline{e}^{\prime}_{j+1}\subseteq\Delta
for all
0\leqslant j\leqslant k-1
,

•

S_{\mathcal{E}^{\prime}}h\subseteq\mathcal{U}^{\prime\prime}
.

Now, since
\tau\subseteq\tau_{\mathrm{pw}}
,
we can find
h^{\prime}\in\mathcal{U}^{\prime}
such that
\overline{e}_{j}=h^{\prime}(\overline{e}_{j}^{\prime})\subseteq\Omega^{\prime}
for all
0\leqslant j\leqslant k
.
Write
\mathcal{E}=(\overline{e}_{j})_{j=0}^{k}
. Using
Lemma

5.1
repeatedly we obtain:

S_{\mathcal{E}}h^{\prime}h=h^{\prime}S_{\mathcal{E}^{\prime}}h\subseteq\mathcal{U}^{\prime}\mathcal{U}^{\prime\prime}\subseteq\mathcal{U}.

We can now apply
Item

2
of the definition of a
k
-sink to conclude the existence of
u\in S_{\mathcal{E}}
with
\im(u)\subseteq\Omega^{\prime}
. In particular,
\im(uh^{\prime}h)\subseteq\Omega^{\prime}
too. But
uh^{\prime}h\in\mathcal{U}
by the above equation. This contradicts the definition of
\mathcal{U}
, thus concluding the proof.
∎

7.2
The method of universally embedded models

In this section we define a natural way to obtain sinks through the existence of a sufficiently “generic” copy of
\mathcal{M}
within itself, which we shall call a universally embedded model. In
Section

7.2.1
we define and give examples of universally embedded models. We show that in most cases of interest to us these are just what is commonly known in model theory as a lovely pair
[
8
]
. Most of our discussion is to give the reader context and intuitions for the definition. We prove such universally embedded models exist whenever
\mathop{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\textstyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\scriptstyle\kern 2.80048pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 1.89871pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 2.80048pt}{\scriptscriptstyle\kern 1.42882pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 0.96873pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 1.42882pt}}^{a}
satisfies base monotonicity in
Lemma

7.14
, and this is the only proof in the section needed for
Theorem

7.19
. Then, in
Section

7.2.2
, we show that universally embedded models do indeed yield sinks. This concludes the proof of
Theorem

7.19
.

7.2.1
On the existence of universally embedded models

Definition 7.6
.

Let
\Omega^{\prime}\subseteq\Omega
be a (possibly infinite) algebraically closed set. An
absorbing configuration

(\overline{a}_{1};\overline{a}_{2};\overline{b})
for
\Omega^{\prime}
consists of algebraically closed finite tuples
\overline{a}_{1},\overline{a}_{2}
, and
\overline{b}
from
\Omega
such that

(a)

\overline{a}_{1}\subseteq\overline{a}_{2}
;

(b)

\overline{b}\mathop{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\textstyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\scriptstyle\kern 2.80048pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 1.89871pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 2.80048pt}{\scriptscriptstyle\kern 1.42882pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 0.96873pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 1.42882pt}}^{a}_{\overline{b}\cap\Omega^{\prime}}\overline{a}_{1}
; and

(c)

\overline{b}\cap\Omega^{\prime}\mathop{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\textstyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\scriptstyle\kern 2.80048pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 1.89871pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 2.80048pt}{\scriptscriptstyle\kern 1.42882pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 0.96873pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 1.42882pt}}^{a}_{\overline{a}_{1}}\overline{a}_{2}
.

Definition 7.7
.

Let
G\curvearrowright\Omega
be the automorphism group of a countable saturated structure
\mathcal{M}
with locally finite algebraic closure.
We say that
\Omega^{\prime}\subsetneq\Omega
is
universally embedded
in
\Omega
if the following conditions are satisfied:

•

The set
\Omega^{\prime}
is algebraically closed.

•

Whenever
(\overline{a}_{1};\overline{a}_{2};\overline{b})
forms an absorbing configuration for
\Omega^{\prime}
, there are
\overline{a}_{1}^{\prime}\overline{a}_{2}^{\prime}\equiv_{\overline{b}}\overline{a}_{1}\overline{a}_{2}
such that
\Omega^{\prime}\cap\overline{a}_{2}^{\prime}=\overline{a}_{1}^{\prime}
.

We will see in
7.9
below that if
\Omega^{\prime}\subseteq\Omega
is universally embedded, then it is the universe of an elementary submodel of
\mathcal{M}
. Hence, we will call
\Omega^{\prime}
an
universally embedded model
. We will say that
G\curvearrowright\Omega

admits universally embedded models
if some universally embedded set
\Omega^{\prime}\subseteq\Omega
exists.
Fig.

1
illustrates the behaviour of a universally embedded model.

Before going into deeper structural results, the reader may verify for themselves that the following are examples of universally embedded models when
G=\Aut(M)
:

•

a dense codense subset of
M:=(\mathbb{Q};<)
;

•

a subspace of infinite dimension and infinite codimension, where
M
is a countable vector space over a finite field.

Figure 1:
An illustration of the definition of a universally embedded model. In the figure, we have that
(\overline{a}_{1};\overline{a}_{2};\overline{b})
is an absorbing configuration for
\Omega^{\prime}
. In particular,
\overline{b}\mathop{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\textstyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\scriptstyle\kern 2.80048pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 1.89871pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 2.80048pt}{\scriptscriptstyle\kern 1.42882pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 0.96873pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 1.42882pt}}^{a}_{\overline{b}\cap\Omega^{\prime}}\overline{a}_{1}
and
\overline{b}\cap\Omega^{\prime}\mathop{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\textstyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\scriptstyle\kern 2.80048pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 1.89871pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 2.80048pt}{\scriptscriptstyle\kern 1.42882pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 0.96873pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 1.42882pt}}^{a}_{\overline{a}_{1}}\overline{a}_{2}
. This allows us to “absorb”
\overline{a}_{1}
into
\Omega^{\prime}
: that is, to find
\overline{a}_{1}^{\prime}\overline{a}_{2}^{\prime}\equiv_{\overline{b}}\overline{a}_{1}\overline{a}_{2}
such that
\Omega^{\prime}\cap\overline{a}_{2}^{\prime}=\overline{a}_{1}^{\prime}
.

Definition 7.8
.

Let

(
♣
)

‣
3.2
hold. We say that the algebraically closed set
\Omega^{\prime}\subseteq\Omega
has the
coheir property

if for any absorbing configuration
(\overline{a};\overline{a};\bar{b})
for
\Omega^{\prime}
, there is
\overline{a}^{\prime}\equiv_{\overline{b}}\overline{a}
such that
\overline{a}^{\prime}\subseteq\Omega^{\prime}
. Equivalently,
\Omega^{\prime}
has the coheir property if it satisfies the following condition:

•

(coheir) for all finite and algebraically closed tuples
\overline{a},\overline{b}
from
\Omega
such that
\overline{b}\mathop{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\textstyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\scriptstyle\kern 2.80048pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 1.89871pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 2.80048pt}{\scriptscriptstyle\kern 1.42882pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 0.96873pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 1.42882pt}}^{a}_{\overline{b}\cap\Omega^{\prime}}\overline{a}
, there is
\overline{a}^{\prime}\equiv_{\overline{b}}\overline{a}
such that
\overline{a}^{\prime}\subseteq\Omega^{\prime}
.

By definition, universally embedded models have the coheir property.

Observation 7.9
.

Let

(
♣
)

‣
3.2
hold. Let
\Omega^{\prime}\subseteq\Omega
be universally embedded. Then,
\Omega^{\prime}
is the universe of
some elementary submodel
\mathcal{M}^{\prime}\preceq\mathcal{M}
. Moreover,
\mathcal{M}^{\prime}
is also saturated.

Proof.

This is a simple application of the Tarski-Vaught test
[
60
, Theorem 2.1.2]
, using the coheir property for
\Omega^{\prime}
. By the same reasoning, since
\mathcal{M}
is saturated,
\mathcal{M}^{\prime}
is also saturated.
∎

Definition 7.10
.

Let

(
♣
)

‣
3.2
hold. We say that
(\Omega^{\prime};\Omega)
forms a
lovely pair
if
\Omega^{\prime}\subseteq\Omega
is an algebraically closed subset of
\Omega
satisfying the coheir property and the following extension property:

•

(extension) for any finite and algebraically closed tuples
\overline{a},\overline{b}
from
\Omega
, there is
\overline{a}^{\prime}\equiv_{\overline{b}}\overline{a}
such that

\acl(\overline{a}^{\prime}\overline{b})\cap\Omega^{\prime}=\overline{b}\;.

(Note: if we extended the notion of algebraic independence to infinite algebraically closed sets, the conclusion could be phrased as
\overline{a}^{\prime}\mathop{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\textstyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\scriptstyle\kern 2.80048pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 1.89871pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 2.80048pt}{\scriptscriptstyle\kern 1.42882pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 0.96873pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 1.42882pt}}^{a}_{\overline{b}}\Omega^{\prime}
.)

Our definition of lovely pair agrees (in the context of

(
♣
)

‣
3.2
) with the standard definition of a lovely pair for a geometric theory
[
9
, Definition 2.3]
. We clarify this in
Lemma

7.12
. A first-order theory is
geometric
if it eliminates the quantifier
\exists^{\infty}
(in the sense of
[
60
, Definition 5.5.6]
) and (model-theoretic) algebraic closure
[
60
, Definition 5.6.1]
forms a pregeometry.
4
4

4

Note that if model-theoretic algebraic closure forms a pregeometry in a model of a theory, then it is a pregeometry in all models. Moreover, any
\omega
-categorical theory eliminates the quantifier
\exists^{\infty}
.

Lovely pairs of models are heavily studied in simple theories
[
8
]
(where the definition is in terms of non-forking independence rather than algebraic independence), and more specifically in stable theories, where they are known as
belles paires
from work of Poizat
[
54
]
. They are also studied in the context of o-minimal theories
[
64
]
as
dense pairs
.

Lemma 7.11
.

Let

(
♣
)

‣
3.2
hold and
(\Omega^{\prime};\Omega)
be a lovely pair. Suppose that algebraic closure is modular. Then,
\Omega^{\prime}
is a universally embedded model.

Proof.

Let
(\overline{a}_{1};\overline{a}_{2};\overline{b})
be an absorbing configuration for
\Omega^{\prime}
. By the coheir property, there is some
\overline{a}_{1}^{\prime}\equiv_{\overline{b}}\overline{a}_{1}
such that
\overline{a}_{1}^{\prime}\subseteq\Omega^{\prime}
. Taking
\overline{a}_{2}^{\prime}
such that
\overline{a}_{1}^{\prime}\overline{a}_{2}^{\prime}\equiv_{\overline{b}}\overline{a}_{1}\overline{a}_{2}
. By invariance, we have that
\overline{a}_{2}^{\prime}\mathop{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\textstyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\scriptstyle\kern 2.80048pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 1.89871pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 2.80048pt}{\scriptscriptstyle\kern 1.42882pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 0.96873pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 1.42882pt}}^{a}_{\overline{a}_{1}^{\prime}}\overline{b}\cap\Omega^{\prime}
. Let
C=\acl(\overline{a}_{1}^{\prime}\overline{b})
. Then, by the extension property, there is
\overline{a}_{2}^{\prime\prime}\equiv_{C}\overline{a}_{2}^{\prime}
such that

\acl(\overline{a}_{2}^{\prime\prime}C)\cap\acl(\Omega^{\prime}C)=C\;.

(10)

Suppose that
d\in\overline{a}_{2}^{\prime\prime}\cap\Omega^{\prime}
. Then, by (
10
),
d\in C
, and in particular,
d\in C\cap\Omega^{\prime}
. By the modular law, we have that

\acl(C\cap\Omega^{\prime})=\acl(\overline{a}_{1}^{\prime}\cup(\overline{b}\cap\Omega^{\prime}))\;.

By invariance, we know that
\overline{a}_{2}^{\prime\prime}\mathop{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\textstyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\scriptstyle\kern 2.80048pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 1.89871pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 2.80048pt}{\scriptscriptstyle\kern 1.42882pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 0.96873pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 1.42882pt}}^{a}_{\overline{a}_{1}^{\prime}}\overline{b}\cap\Omega^{\prime}
, and by monotonicity,
d\mathop{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\textstyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\scriptstyle\kern 2.80048pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 1.89871pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 2.80048pt}{\scriptscriptstyle\kern 1.42882pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 0.96873pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 1.42882pt}}^{a}_{\overline{a}_{1}^{\prime}}\overline{b}\cap\Omega^{\prime}
. But then, since
d\in\acl(\overline{a}_{1}^{\prime}\cup(\overline{b}\cap\Omega^{\prime}))
, by definition of algebraic independence and since
\overline{a}_{1}^{\prime}
is algebraically closed, we must have that
d\in\overline{a}_{1}^{\prime}
.
This yields the desired conclusion that
\overline{a}_{1}^{\prime}\overline{a}_{2}^{\prime\prime}\equiv_{\overline{b}}\overline{a}_{1}\overline{a}_{2}
and
\overline{a}_{2}^{\prime\prime}\cap\Omega^{\prime}=\overline{a}_{1}^{\prime}
.
∎

Since the context of our definition is slightly different from that of
[
9
]
, in the lemma below we reassure the reader that countable saturated models of geometric theories with locally finite algebraic closure do indeed have lovely pairs.

Lemma 7.12
.

Let
\mathcal{M}
be a countable saturated model of a geometric theory. Suppose further that
\mathcal{M}
has locally finite algebraic closure. Then, there is some
\mathcal{M}^{\prime}\preceq\mathcal{M}
such that
(\mathcal{M}^{\prime};\mathcal{M})
forms a lovely pair (with respect to the action
\Aut(\mathcal{M})\curvearrowright\Omega
).

Proof.

Let
T
denote the theory of
\mathcal{M}
. The theory
T
can be chosen to have countable language
\mathcal{L}
(by the existence of a countable saturated model). From
[
9
]
, we know that
T
has lovely pairs (in the sense of
[
9
, Definition 2.3]
). The theory
T_{P}
of a lovely pairs of models of
T
(in the language
\mathcal{L}_{P}
obtained by adding to
\mathcal{L}
a predicate
P
for the smaller model) is given an axiomatisation in
[
9
, Theorem 2.10]
. Inspecting the proof of the second claim of
[
9
, Theorem 2.10]
, one can see that in our context (where algebraic closure is locally finite) any countable saturated model of
T_{P}
is still a lovely pair. So we need to prove that
T_{P}
still has countable saturated models. In particular, a countable theory having a countable saturated model is equivalent to it only having countably many types of
n
-tuples for each finite
n

[
60
, Lemma 4.3.9]
. For this, we move to the expansion
T^{\prime}_{P}
(with language
\mathcal{L}_{P}^{\prime}
) of
T_{P}
which is obtained by adding, for each formula
\phi(\overline{x};\overline{y})
a relation symbol
R_{\phi(\overline{x};\overline{y})}
together with the axiom:

\forall\overline{y}\left(R_{\phi(\overline{x};\overline{y})}(\overline{y})\leftrightarrow\exists\overline{x}P(\overline{x})\wedge\phi(\overline{x};\overline{y})\right)\;.

We know that
T^{\prime}_{P}
has quantifier elimination
[
9
, Corollary 3.2]
. Given a finite algebraically closed tuple
\overline{a}
in a (sufficiently saturated) model
(\mathcal{N}^{\prime};\mathcal{N})
of
T_{P}^{\prime}
(where
\mathcal{N}^{\prime}
is the smaller model named by the predicate
P
), it is easy to see that the type of
\overline{a}
in
T_{P}^{\prime}
is entirely determined by which elements of
\overline{a}
belong to
P
. Since
\overline{a}
is finite (by our local finiteness assumption), we can deduce that for any
\mathcal{L}
-type of an
n
-tuple
p
in
T
, there are only finitely many
\mathcal{L}_{P}^{\prime}
-types of an
n
-tuple in
T_{P}^{\prime}
which restrict to
p
, implying that
T_{P}^{\prime}
(and so
T_{P}
) only has countably many types of
n
-tuples for each
n\in\mathbb{N}
. This completes the proof.
∎

Non-example 7.13
.

We give an example of a class of
\omega
-categorical structures with no universally embedded models. Consider Droste’s
[
25
]

2
-homogeneous semilinear orders of positive type
[
45
, Example 6.1.2.3]
. These are the countable partially ordered structures
(T,\leqslant)
satisfying the following conditions:

•

(lower semilinear order)
for any
a\in T
, the set
\{b\in T|b\leqslant a\}
forms a linear order;

•

(density) every maximal chain is isomorphic to
(\mathbb{Q},\leqslant)
;

•

(positive type)
T
contains its ramification points (i.e., the infimum in the Dedekind-MacNeille completion of any two incomparable elements);

•

(fixed branching) every element
a\in T
has the same branching number
s\in\{n\in\mathbb{N}\ |\ n>1\}\cup\{\aleph_{0}\}
, where this is the number of equivalence classes of the equivalence relation
E_{a}
on
C_{a}:=\{b\in T\ |\ a\leqslant b\}
where for
b,c\in C_{a}
,
E_{a}(b,c)
holds if and only if there is
d\in T
such that
a<d\leqslant b,c
.

These structures are
\omega
-categorical (indeed, they can be made homogeneous by adding finitely many relations to the language) and with primitive automorphism group. They also have non-trivial algebraic closure: any two incomparable points contain their ramification point in their algebraic closure. It is easy to prove that if
(T,\leqslant)
is a
2
-homogeneous semilinear order of positive type, given
\Aut(T)\curvearrowright T
, any algebraically closed set
\Omega^{\prime}\subseteq T
with the coheir property is
T
itself. In fact, suppose that
\Omega^{\prime}
is universally embedded in
T
. Take any
c\in T
. By transitivity of
\Aut(T)
, there are
a,b\in T
such that
c
is their meet. By the coheir property (and the fact that single elements are algebraically closed),
\Omega^{\prime}
contains
a^{\prime}\equiv_{c}a
and
b^{\prime}\equiv_{ca^{\prime}}b
. But then, since
\Omega^{\prime}
is algebraically closed,
c\in\Omega^{\prime}
. This implies that
\Omega^{\prime}=T
. In particular,
T
does not admit any universally embedded model (or lovely pair).

Lemma 7.14
.

Let

(
♣
)

‣
3.2
hold and assume further that
\mathop{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\textstyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\scriptstyle\kern 2.80048pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 1.89871pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 2.80048pt}{\scriptscriptstyle\kern 1.42882pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 0.96873pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 1.42882pt}}^{a}
satisfies base monotonicity. Then,
G\curvearrowright\Omega
admits universally embedded models. In fact, the family of all universally embedded models is comeager in the space of all algebraically closed subsets of
\Omega
.

Proof.

Let
\mathfrak{C}\subseteq 2^{\Omega}

be the space of all (possibly infinite) algebraically closed subsets of
\Omega
. It is not hard to see that
\mathfrak{C}
is closed in
2^{\Omega}
with the Tychonoff topology (where
\Omega
is endowed with the discrete topology). Recall that a basic neighbourhood in this topology is of the form

\mathcal{U}_{E_{0},E}=\{\,F\subseteq\Omega\,|\,F\cap E=E_{0}\}

for finite sets
E_{0}\subset E\subset\Omega
.
It is not hard to see such a set has a non-trivial intersection with
\mathfrak{C}
if and only if
\mathrm{acl}(E_{0})\cap E=E_{0}
.

Note that since the countable intersection of comeagre sets is still comeagre, in order to deduce that the family of universally embedded models is comeagre in
\mathfrak{C}
, it is sufficient to prove that for each triplet
(\overline{a}_{1};\overline{a}_{2};\overline{b})
of finite algebraically closed subsets of
\Omega
, there is a comeagre set
\mathcal{V}\in\mathfrak{C}
such that for each
\Omega^{\prime}\in\mathcal{V}
either one of the following two alternatives holds:

•

(\overline{a}_{1};\overline{a}_{2};\overline{b})
is not an absorbing configuration for
\Omega^{\prime}
;

•

there are
\overline{a}_{1}^{\prime}\overline{a}_{2}^{\prime}\equiv_{\overline{b}}\overline{a}_{1}\overline{a}_{2}
such that
\overline{a}_{2}^{\prime}\cap\Omega^{\prime}=\overline{a}_{1}^{\prime}
.

For this, it suffices to show that any (non-empty) basic open set
\mathfrak{C}\cap\mathcal{U}_{E_{0},E}
contains some open subset
\mathcal{U}^{\prime}
such that every
\Omega^{\prime}\in\mathcal{U}^{\prime}
verifies one of the two alternatives above.
So take
E_{0}\subseteq E
with
\mathrm{acl}(E_{0})\cap E=E_{0}
. By possibly taking larger
E_{0}
and
E
(and so some open subset of the original
\mathfrak{C}\cap\mathcal{U}_{E_{0},E}
, we may assume that
\overline{b}\subseteq E
and that
E_{0}
and
E
are algebraically closed. Write
\overline{b}_{0}=\overline{b}\cap E_{0}
. Note that since
\overline{b}\subseteq E
, for every
\Omega^{\prime}\in\mathfrak{C}\cap\mathcal{U}_{E_{0},E}
,
\Omega^{\prime}\cap\overline{b}=\overline{b}_{0}
. In particular, a triplet
(\overline{a}_{1};\overline{a}_{2};\overline{b})
is an absorbing configuration for
\overline{b}_{0}
if and only if for every it is an absorbing configuration for
\Omega^{\prime}
for every
\Omega^{\prime}\in\mathfrak{C}\cap\mathcal{U}_{E_{0},E}
. Hence, if
(\overline{a}_{1};\overline{a}_{2};\overline{b})
is not an absorbing configuration for
\overline{b}_{0}
, it is not an absorbing configuration for any
\Omega^{\prime}\in\mathfrak{C}\cap\mathcal{U}_{E_{0},E}
, and we are done. Thus, we may assume that
(\overline{a}_{1};\overline{a}_{2};\overline{b})
is an absorbing configuration for
\overline{b}_{0}
.
We will now construct finite algebraically closed sets
E^{\prime}_{0}\subseteq E^{\prime}
such that
E\subseteq E^{\prime}
, and:

(i)

E^{\prime}_{0}\cap E=E_{0}
;

(ii)

there is
\overline{a}_{2}^{\prime}\overline{a}_{1}^{\prime}\equiv_{\overline{b}}\overline{a}_{2}\overline{a}_{1}
such that
\overline{a}_{2}^{\prime}\subseteq E^{\prime}
and
\overline{a}_{2}^{\prime}\cap E_{0}^{\prime}=\overline{a}_{1}^{\prime}
.

From the above conditions, it follows that the open (and non-empty) set
\mathcal{U}^{\prime}=\mathfrak{C}\cap\mathcal{U}_{E^{\prime}_{0},E^{\prime}}\subseteq\mathfrak{C}\cap\mathcal{U}_{E_{0},E}
satisfies the required properties. In particular, note that
\mathcal{U}^{\prime}
is non-empty since
E_{0}^{\prime}\cap E=E_{0}^{\prime}
, and that the containment
\mathcal{U}^{\prime}\subseteq\mathfrak{C}\cap\mathcal{U}_{E_{0},E}
follows from
(i)
.

For our construction, take a realization
\overline{a}_{2}^{\prime}\overline{a}_{1}^{\prime}\equiv_{\overline{b}}\overline{a}_{2}\overline{a}_{1}
with
E\mathop{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\textstyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\scriptstyle\kern 2.80048pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 1.89871pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 2.80048pt}{\scriptscriptstyle\kern 1.42882pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 0.96873pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 1.42882pt}}^{a}_{\overline{b}}\overline{a}_{2}^{\prime}\overline{a}_{1}^{\prime}
by full existence. Write
E^{\prime}=\mathrm{acl}(E\overline{a}_{2}^{\prime}\overline{a}_{1}^{\prime})
,
E^{\prime}_{0}=\mathrm{acl}(E_{0}\overline{a}_{1}^{\prime})
. The following two conditions are now equivalent to items
(i)
and
(ii)
respectively:

(i
⋆
)

\overline{a}_{1}^{\prime}\mathop{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\textstyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\scriptstyle\kern 2.80048pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 1.89871pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 2.80048pt}{\scriptscriptstyle\kern 1.42882pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 0.96873pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 1.42882pt}}^{a}_{E_{0}}E
;

(ii
⋆
)

E_{0}^{\prime}\mathop{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\textstyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\scriptstyle\kern 2.80048pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 1.89871pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 2.80048pt}{\scriptscriptstyle\kern 1.42882pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 0.96873pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 1.42882pt}}^{a}_{\overline{a}_{1}^{\prime}}\overline{a}_{2}^{\prime}
.

In order to do this, let us look back first at our assumption that
(\overline{a}_{1};\overline{a}_{2};\overline{b})
is an absorbing configuration for
\overline{b}_{0}
. Since
\overline{a}_{2}^{\prime}\overline{a}_{1}^{\prime}\equiv_{\overline{b}}\overline{a}_{2}\overline{a}_{1}
, by invariance,
(\overline{a}_{1}^{\prime};\overline{a}_{2}^{\prime};\overline{b})
is also an absorbing configuration for
\overline{b}_{0}
.
Conditions
(b)
and
(c)
in
Definition

7.7
are then equivalent to:

\overline{b}\mathop{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\textstyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\scriptstyle\kern 2.80048pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 1.89871pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 2.80048pt}{\scriptscriptstyle\kern 1.42882pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 0.96873pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 1.42882pt}}^{a}_{\overline{b}_{0}}\overline{a}_{1}^{\prime},

(11)

\overline{b}_{0}\mathop{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\textstyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\scriptstyle\kern 2.80048pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 1.89871pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 2.80048pt}{\scriptscriptstyle\kern 1.42882pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 0.96873pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 1.42882pt}}^{a}_{\overline{a}_{1}^{\prime}}\overline{a}_{2}^{\prime}.

(12)

We use the assumption that
\mathop{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\textstyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\scriptstyle\kern 2.80048pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 1.89871pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 2.80048pt}{\scriptscriptstyle\kern 1.42882pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 0.96873pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 1.42882pt}}^{a}
is a symmetric independence relation (the nontrivial assumption is that it satisfies base monotonicity) in a crucial way. To establish
(i⋆)
we use monotonicity, transitivity together with
Eq.

11
, and finally base monotonicity:

\displaystyle E\mathop{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\textstyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\scriptstyle\kern 2.80048pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 1.89871pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 2.80048pt}{\scriptscriptstyle\kern 1.42882pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 0.96873pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 1.42882pt}}^{a}_{\overline{b}}\overline{a}_{2}^{\prime}\quad

\displaystyle\Rightarrow\quad E\mathop{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\textstyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\scriptstyle\kern 2.80048pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 1.89871pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 2.80048pt}{\scriptscriptstyle\kern 1.42882pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 0.96873pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 1.42882pt}}^{a}_{\overline{b}}\overline{a}_{1}^{\prime}

by monotonicity,

\displaystyle\Rightarrow\quad E\mathop{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\textstyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\scriptstyle\kern 2.80048pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 1.89871pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 2.80048pt}{\scriptscriptstyle\kern 1.42882pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 0.96873pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 1.42882pt}}^{a}_{\overline{b}_{0}}\overline{a}_{1}^{\prime}

by transitivity and (
11
),

\displaystyle\Rightarrow\quad E\mathop{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\textstyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\scriptstyle\kern 2.80048pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 1.89871pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 2.80048pt}{\scriptscriptstyle\kern 1.42882pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 0.96873pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 1.42882pt}}^{a}_{E_{0}}\overline{a}_{1}^{\prime}

by base monotonicity.

Thus, we conclude
(i⋆)
by symmetry. Finally, for
(ii⋆)
,

\displaystyle E\mathop{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\textstyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\scriptstyle\kern 2.80048pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 1.89871pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 2.80048pt}{\scriptscriptstyle\kern 1.42882pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 0.96873pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 1.42882pt}}^{a}_{\overline{b}}\overline{a}_{2}^{\prime}\quad

\displaystyle\Rightarrow\quad E_{0}\mathop{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\textstyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\scriptstyle\kern 2.80048pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 1.89871pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 2.80048pt}{\scriptscriptstyle\kern 1.42882pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 0.96873pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 1.42882pt}}^{a}_{\overline{b}}\overline{a}_{2}^{\prime}

by monotonicity,

\displaystyle\Rightarrow\quad E_{0}\mathop{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\textstyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\scriptstyle\kern 2.80048pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 1.89871pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 2.80048pt}{\scriptscriptstyle\kern 1.42882pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 0.96873pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 1.42882pt}}^{a}_{\overline{b}_{0}\overline{a}_{1}^{\prime}}\overline{a}_{2}^{\prime}

\displaystyle\text{ by base monotonicity},

\displaystyle\Rightarrow\quad E_{0}\mathop{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\textstyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\scriptstyle\kern 2.80048pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 1.89871pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 2.80048pt}{\scriptscriptstyle\kern 1.42882pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 0.96873pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 1.42882pt}}^{a}_{\overline{a}_{1}^{\prime}}\overline{a}_{2}^{\prime}

\displaystyle\text{ by transitivity, \eqref{cons2}, and }\overline{b}_{0}\subseteq E_{0}.

Since we verified
(i)
and
(ii)
, this completes our proof.
∎

Example 7.15
.

An important feature of universally embedded models being the main ingredient to our proof of
Theorem

7.19
is that, unlike
Theorem

6.8
, we are not relying on stationarity or amalgamation properties that would constrain us to some particular area of the model-theoretic universe (such as that of simple theories). For example, let
\mathcal{M}
be a homogeneous structure with no algebraicity in the language
\mathcal{L}
,
\mathcal{N}
be a countable vector space over the finite field
\mathbb{F}_{q}
(in the language of vector spaces
\mathcal{L}_{V}
. Consider the free superposition
\mathcal{M}^{\prime}
of
\mathcal{M}
and
\mathcal{N}
. This is just the Fraïssé limit of finite-dimensional vector spaces over
\mathbb{F}_{q}
with an underlying
\mathcal{L}
-structure in the age of
\mathcal{M}
.
Then, algebraic closure satisfies exchange on
\mathcal{M}^{\prime}
and is modular (since it agrees with algebraic closure in
\mathcal{N}
). Hence,
\mathop{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\textstyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\scriptstyle\kern 2.80048pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 1.89871pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 2.80048pt}{\scriptscriptstyle\kern 1.42882pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 0.96873pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 1.42882pt}}^{a}
satisfies base monotonicity on
\mathcal{M}^{\prime}
and
Lemma

7.14
and
Theorem

7.19
apply to
\mathcal{M}^{\prime}
.

7.2.2
Universally embedded models as sinks

Below we verify that universally embedded models satisfy condition
3
of
Definition

7.1
.

Lemma 7.16
.

Let

(
♣
)

‣
3.2
hold. Let
\Omega^{\prime}\subseteq\Omega
be a universally embedded model. Then, for every
\Delta\subseteq\Omega
which is
G
-invariant and algebraically closed there is
v\in\overline{G}
such that
\im(v)\cap\Omega^{\prime}=v(\Delta)
.

Proof.

Fix a
G
-invariant and algebraically closed set
\Delta\subseteq\Omega
. Let
(A_{(i,2)})_{i\in\mathbb{N}}
be a nested sequence of finite algebraically closed subsets of
\Omega
with
(A_{(i,2)}):=\acl(\emptyset)
and such that
\bigcup_{i\in\mathbb{N}}A_{(i,2)}=\Omega
. For
i\in\mathbb{N}
, let
A_{(i,1)}:=A_{(i,2)}\cap\Delta
. Note that we can prove the lemma by finding a sequence
(g_{i})_{i\in\mathbb{N}}
of elements of
G
such that for each
i\in\mathbb{N}
,
g_{i+1}\upharpoonright_{A_{(i,2)}}=g_{i}\upharpoonright_{A_{(i,2)}}
and

g_{i}(A_{(i,1)})=g_{i}(A_{(i,2)})\cap\Omega^{\prime}\;.

(13)

In fact, it is easy to verify that taking
v
to be the limit of the sequence
(g_{i})_{i\in\mathbb{N}}
in the pointwise convergence topology will have the desired property that
\im(v)\cap\Omega^{\prime}=v(\Delta)
.

We now build the sequences
(A_{(i,2)})_{i\in\mathbb{N}}
and
(g_{i})_{i\in\mathbb{N}}
inductively. Below, we shall also adopt the notational convention that for each
i\in\mathbb{N}
and
j\in\{1,2\}
we have

A_{(i,j)}^{\prime}:=g_{i}(A_{(i,j)})
. The base case is trivial since
\acl(\emptyset)\subseteq\Delta
and every
g\in G
fixes
\acl(\emptyset)
setwise (so we may choose any
g\in G
for
g_{0}
). For the inductive step, suppose that we have built
(g_{i})_{i\leqslant n}
. Firstly, take
A_{(n+1,2)}^{\prime\prime}:=g_{n}(A_{(n+1,2)})
and
A_{(n+1,1)}^{\prime\prime}:=g_{n}(A_{(n+1,1)})
. Below, we write
\overline{a}^{k}_{(j,i)}
for an enumeration of
A^{k}_{(j,i)}
, where
j\in\mathbb{N},i\in\{1,2\}
, and
k\in\{^{\prime},^{\prime\prime}\}
.

Claim:

(\overline{a}^{\prime\prime}_{(n+1,1)};\overline{a}^{\prime\prime}_{(n+1,2)};\overline{a}^{\prime}_{(n,2)})
forms an absorbing configuration for
\Omega^{\prime}
.

Proof of Claim.

Firstly,
(a)
is true by construction (and the fact that both
A_{n+1}
and
\Delta
are algebraically closed). For
(b)
, note that

A_{(n,2)}\cap A_{(n+1,1)}=A_{(n,2)}\cap A_{(n+1,1)}\cap\Delta=A_{(n,1)}\;.

From this and the definition of algebraic independence we get that

A_{(n,2)}\mathop{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\textstyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\scriptstyle\kern 2.80048pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 1.89871pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 2.80048pt}{\scriptscriptstyle\kern 1.42882pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 0.96873pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 1.42882pt}}^{a}_{A_{(n,1)}}A_{(n+1,1)}

Finally, by invariance (multiplying all sides by
g_{i}
),

A^{\prime}_{(n,2)}\mathop{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\textstyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\scriptstyle\kern 2.80048pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 1.89871pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 2.80048pt}{\scriptscriptstyle\kern 1.42882pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 0.96873pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 1.42882pt}}^{a}_{A^{\prime}_{(n,1)}}A^{\prime\prime}_{(n+1,1)}\;.

This is precisely condition
(b)
for
(\overline{a}^{\prime\prime}_{(n+1,1)};\overline{a}^{\prime\prime}_{(n+1,2)};\overline{a}^{\prime}_{(n,2)})
.

For condition
(c)
, note that by triviality and monotonicity

A_{(n,1)}\mathop{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\textstyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\scriptstyle\kern 2.80048pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 1.89871pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 2.80048pt}{\scriptscriptstyle\kern 1.42882pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 0.96873pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 1.42882pt}}^{a}_{A_{(n+1,1)}}A_{(n+1,2)}\;.

Again, by invariance,

A^{\prime}_{(n,1)}\mathop{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\textstyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\scriptstyle\kern 2.80048pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 1.89871pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 2.80048pt}{\scriptscriptstyle\kern 1.42882pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 0.96873pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 1.42882pt}}^{a}_{A^{\prime\prime}_{(n+1,1)}}A^{\prime\prime}_{(n+1,2)}\;,

which is precisely
(c)
.
∎

Since
(\overline{a}^{\prime\prime}_{(n+1,1)};\overline{a}^{\prime\prime}_{(n+1,1)};\overline{a}^{\prime}_{(n,2)})
is an absorbing configuration and
\Omega^{\prime}
is a universally embedded model, there are
A_{(n+1,2)}^{\prime}
and
A_{(n,1)}^{\prime}
such that

\overline{a}_{(n+1,2)}^{\prime\prime}\overline{a}^{\prime\prime}_{(n+1,1)}\equiv_{A^{\prime}_{(n,2)}}\overline{a}_{(n+1,2)}^{\prime}\overline{a}_{(n,1)}^{\prime}

(14)

and
A_{(n+1,2)}^{\prime}\cap\Omega^{\prime}=A^{\prime}_{(n+1,1)}
. In particular, letting
h\in G
witness (
14
), we have that
g_{n+1}:=hg_{n}
yields a group element which agrees with
g_{n}
on
A_{(n,2)}
and such that (
13
) is satisfied (for
i=n+1
). This concludes our induction, and thus the proof.
∎

Before proving that universally embedded models yield sinks, we will need the following variant of
Lemma

3.23
:

Lemma 7.17
.

Let
G\curvearrowright\Omega
be the automorphism group of a countable saturated structure and
X\subseteq\Omega
be an algebraically closed set. Then, there are
\alpha,\beta\in\overline{G}
such that
\alpha\upharpoonright_{X}=\beta\upharpoonright_{X}
and
\im(\alpha)\cap\im(\beta)=\alpha(X)
.

Proof.

The argument is identical to that of Lemma
3.23
.
Let
Y:=\Omega\setminus X
, and consider
\mathcal{M}
as before. Let
\overline{b}:=(b_{0},b_{1},b_{2},\dots)
be a tuple of length
\omega
enumerating
\mathcal{M}
with
a=b_{0}
. Consider the infinitary type
p(\overline{x}):=\mathrm{tp}(\overline{b})
. For the tuples of variables of length
\omega
,
\overline{x}
and
\overline{y}
, consider the partial type
\Gamma(\overline{x},\overline{y})
given by

p(\overline{x})\cup p(\overline{y})\cup\{x_{i}\neq y_{j}|b_{i},b_{j}\in Y\}\cup\{x_{i}=y_{i}|\ b_{i}\in X\}.

We just need to prove that
\Gamma(\overline{x},\overline{y})
is (finitely) satisfiable (and then conclude like in
Lemma

3.23
).
To see this, for some finite indexing set
J\subseteq\omega
, consider the restriction of
\Gamma
to variables
x_{i}
and
y_{i}
from
J
, which we call
\Gamma_{J}(\overline{x}_{J},\overline{y}_{J})
. Since
\acl((b_{i}|i\in J)\cap X)\cap Y=\emptyset
, by Neumann’s Lemma, there is
g\in G
such that
g\upharpoonright_{(b_{i}|i\in J)\cap X}=\mathrm{Id}_{(b_{i}|i\in J)\cap X}
and
g((b_{i}|i\in J)\cap Y)\cap((b_{i}|i\in J)\cap Y)=\emptyset
, meaning that
\Gamma_{J}(\overline{x}_{J},\overline{y}_{J})
is satisfied by the tuple
((b_{i}|i\in J),(g(b_{i})|i\in J))
. Thus,
\Gamma(\overline{x},\overline{y})
is finitely satisfiable and so satisfiable.
∎

Universally embedded models are one way of finding sinks. Note that the second part of
Lemma

7.18
is not needed for
Theorem

7.19
. However, it shows how universally embedded models may allow us to find sinks even in the absence of base monotonicity for
\mathop{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\textstyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\scriptstyle\kern 2.80048pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 1.89871pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 2.80048pt}{\scriptscriptstyle\kern 1.42882pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 0.96873pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 1.42882pt}}^{a}
.

Lemma 7.18
.

Let
G\curvearrowright\Omega
satisfy

(
♣
)

‣
3.2
. Let
\Omega^{\prime}\subsetneq\Omega
be a universally embedded model and let
S:=\overline{G}
. Then:

1.

If
\mathop{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\textstyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\scriptstyle\kern 2.80048pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 1.89871pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 2.80048pt}{\scriptscriptstyle\kern 1.42882pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 0.96873pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 1.42882pt}}^{a}
satisfies base monotonicity, then
\Omega^{\prime}
is a
1
-sink for
S
.

2.

If the action of
G
outside of
\mathrm{acl}(\emptyset)
is transitive, then
\Omega^{\prime}
is a
2
-sink for
S
.

Proof.

Property
1
of
Definition

7.1
follows from
Lemma

7.17
and the fact that
\Omega^{\prime}
is algebraically closed and a proper subset of

\Omega
, while property
3
was established in
Lemma

7.16
. It remains to show property
2
with the appropriate
k
in each case.

Recall from
7.9
that
\Omega^{\prime}
is the universe of a saturated elementary submodel
\mathcal{M}^{\prime}\preceq\mathcal{M}
. Let
\mathcal{C}=(\overline{c}_{i})_{i=0}^{k}
be a chain of algebraically closed sets. From
5.2
, in order to show that
S_{\mathcal{C}}
contains an element whose image is contained
\Omega^{\prime}
, it suffices to show the existence of some chain of algebraically closed sets
\mathcal{C}^{\prime}=(\overline{c}_{i}^{\prime})_{i=0}^{k}
where
\overline{c}_{0}=\overline{c}^{\prime}_{0}
,
\overline{c}_{k}^{\prime}
is a tuple from
\Omega^{\prime}
, and
\overline{c}^{\prime}_{i}\overline{c}^{\prime}_{i+1}\equiv\overline{c}_{i}\overline{c}_{i+1}
for all
0\leqslant i\leqslant k-1
. In fact, given
\mathcal{C}^{\prime}
, since
\Omega^{\prime}
is the universe of a saturated elementary submodel of
\mathcal{M}
, a back and forth argument yields that the partial map
\phi:\overline{c}_{k}\to\overline{c}_{k}^{\prime}
extends to an elementary map
u:\mathcal{M}\to\mathcal{M}^{\prime}
. From this,
5.2
yields that
u\in S_{\mathcal{C}}
. By construction
\im(u)\subseteq\Omega^{\prime}
as desired.

Consider first
Item

1
. Here we are given
\Delta\subseteq\Omega
algebraically closed and
G
-invariant and our chain of algebraically closed sets consists only of
\overline{c}_{1}
and
\overline{c}_{0}
with
\overline{c}_{1}\cap\overline{c}_{0}\subseteq\Delta
.
We claim that there is
\overline{c}_{1}^{\prime}\equiv_{\overline{c}_{0}}\overline{c}_{1}
such that
\overline{c}_{1}^{\prime}\subseteq\Omega^{\prime}
. For this, since
\Omega^{\prime}
is an universally embedded model, it suffices to show that
(\overline{c}_{1};\overline{c}_{1};\overline{c}_{0})
is an absorbing configuration for
\Omega^{\prime}
. Write
\overline{d}_{0}:=\overline{c}_{0}\cap\Omega^{\prime}
. Firstly,
(a)
holds trivially. Moreover,

(b)
and
(c)
are equivalent, respectively, to:

\overline{c}_{0}\mathop{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\textstyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\scriptstyle\kern 2.80048pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 1.89871pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 2.80048pt}{\scriptscriptstyle\kern 1.42882pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 0.96873pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 1.42882pt}}^{a}_{\overline{d}_{0}}\overline{c}_{1},\quad\text{ and }\quad\overline{d}_{0}\mathop{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\textstyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\scriptstyle\kern 2.80048pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 1.89871pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 2.80048pt}{\scriptscriptstyle\kern 1.42882pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 0.96873pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 1.42882pt}}^{a}_{\overline{c}_{1}}\overline{c}_{1}\;.

The first of the two conditions above follows from symmetry, base monotonicity, and the fact that
\overline{c}_{0}\mathop{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\textstyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\scriptstyle\kern 2.80048pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 1.89871pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 2.80048pt}{\scriptscriptstyle\kern 1.42882pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 0.96873pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 1.42882pt}}^{a}_{\overline{c}_{1}\cap\overline{c}_{0}}\overline{c}_{1}
, since
\overline{c}_{1}\cap\overline{c}_{0}\subseteq\Delta\cap\overline{c}_{0}\subseteq\overline{d}_{0}
. The second condition is just an instantiation of existence.

Consider now case
2
. Note that our assumption on
G
being transitive outside of
\mathrm{acl}(\emptyset)
implies that the only
G
-invariant algebraically closed sets are
\mathrm{acl}(\emptyset)
and
\Omega
. Since property
2
of being a sink is trivially satisfied by
\Omega
, we only need to verify the condition for
\mathrm{acl}(\emptyset)
.
Let
(\overline{c}_{i})_{i=2}^{0}
be a chain of algebraically closed sets with
\overline{c}_{i}\cap\overline{c}_{i+1}\subseteq\mathrm{acl}(\emptyset)
for
i\in\{1,2\}
.
First, we claim that we can find
\overline{c}^{\prime}_{1}
with
\overline{c}^{\prime}_{1}\equiv_{\overline{c}_{0}}\overline{c}_{1}
and
\overline{c}^{\prime}_{1}\cap\Omega^{\prime}=\mathrm{acl}(\emptyset)
. The existence of such
\overline{c}^{\prime}_{1}
would be implied by
(\mathrm{acl}(\emptyset);\overline{c}_{1};\overline{c}_{0})
being an absorbing configuration for
\Omega^{\prime}
and the fact that
\Omega^{\prime}
is a universally embedded model. Conditions
(b)
and
(c)
of being an absorbing configuration (
Definition

7.6
) are equivalent, respectively, to the following conditions:

\overline{c}_{0}\mathop{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\textstyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\scriptstyle\kern 2.80048pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 1.89871pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 2.80048pt}{\scriptscriptstyle\kern 1.42882pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 0.96873pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 1.42882pt}}^{a}_{\overline{c}_{0}\cap\Omega^{\prime}}\mathrm{acl}(\emptyset),\quad\text{ and }\quad(\overline{c}_{0}\cap\Omega^{\prime})\mathop{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\textstyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\scriptstyle\kern 2.80048pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 1.89871pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 2.80048pt}{\scriptscriptstyle\kern 1.42882pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 0.96873pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 1.42882pt}}^{a}_{\mathrm{acl}(\emptyset)}\overline{c}_{1}.

The first condition follows from existence and monotonicity. The second condition follows from the fact that
\overline{c}_{0}\cap\overline{c}_{1}=\mathrm{acl}(\emptyset)
(that is,
\overline{c}_{0}\mathop{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\textstyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\scriptstyle\kern 2.80048pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 1.89871pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 2.80048pt}{\scriptscriptstyle\kern 1.42882pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 0.96873pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 1.42882pt}}^{a}_{\mathrm{acl}(\emptyset)}\overline{c}_{1}
) and monotonicity.
To conclude, we want to choose
\overline{c}^{\prime}_{2}
with
\overline{c}^{\prime}_{2}\overline{c}^{\prime}_{1}\equiv\overline{c}_{2}\overline{c}_{1}
and
\overline{c}^{\prime}_{2}\subseteq\Omega^{\prime}
. Firstly, since
\overline{c}_{1}^{\prime}\equiv\overline{c}_{1}
, there is
\overline{c}_{2}^{\prime\prime}
such that
\overline{c}^{\prime\prime}_{2}\overline{c}^{\prime}_{1}\equiv\overline{c}_{2}\overline{c}_{1}
. Our desired conclusion would follow from
(\overline{c}_{2}^{\prime\prime};\overline{c}_{2}^{\prime\prime};\overline{c}_{1}^{\prime})
being an absorbing configuration for
\Omega^{\prime}
. That is, we require that

\overline{c}_{1}^{\prime}\mathop{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\textstyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\scriptstyle\kern 2.80048pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 1.89871pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 2.80048pt}{\scriptscriptstyle\kern 1.42882pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 0.96873pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 1.42882pt}}^{a}_{\overline{c}_{1}^{\prime}\cap\Omega^{\prime}}\overline{c}_{2}^{\prime\prime},\quad\text{ and }\quad(\overline{c}_{1}^{\prime}\cap\Omega^{\prime})\mathop{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\textstyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\scriptstyle\kern 2.80048pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 1.89871pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 2.80048pt}{\scriptscriptstyle\kern 1.42882pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 0.96873pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 1.42882pt}}^{a}_{\overline{c}_{2}^{\prime\prime}}\overline{c}_{2}^{\prime\prime}.

The second relation is an instance of existence. For the first relation, note first that
\overline{c}_{1}^{\prime}\cap\Omega^{\prime}=\mathrm{acl}(\emptyset)
. Hence, by invariance the first relation is equivalent to

\overline{c}_{1}\mathop{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\textstyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\scriptstyle\kern 2.80048pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 1.89871pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 2.80048pt}{\scriptscriptstyle\kern 1.42882pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 0.96873pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 1.42882pt}}^{a}_{\mathrm{acl}(\emptyset)}\overline{c}_{2}\;.

But the latter is just the original assumption that
\overline{c}_{2}\cap\overline{c}_{1}\subseteq\mathrm{acl}(\emptyset)
. This concludes the proof that
(\overline{c}_{2}^{\prime\prime};\overline{c}_{2}^{\prime\prime};\overline{c}_{1}^{\prime})
is an absorbing configuration.
∎

Combining
7.5
with
Lemma

7.14
and
Lemma

7.18
we obtain the following.

Theorem 7.19
.

Let
G
be the automorphism group of a countable saturated structure
\mathcal{M}
with locally finite algebraic closure.
Assume,
that
\mathop{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\textstyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\scriptstyle\kern 2.80048pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 1.89871pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 2.80048pt}{\scriptscriptstyle\kern 1.42882pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 0.96873pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 1.42882pt}}^{a}
satisfies base monotonicity.
Then
\tau_{\mathrm{pw}}
is a minimal Hausdorff semigroup topology on
\overline{G}
.

Remark 7.20
.

Theorem

7.19
implies that all simple one-based
\omega
-categorical structures with very weak elimination of imaginaries are such that
\tau_{\mathrm{pw}}
is a minimal Hausdorff semigroup topology on their monoid of elementary embeddings. This is just because one-basedness implies that non-forking independence agrees with imaginary algebraic independence; very weak elimination of imaginaries tells us that there is an action of
G
on an expansion
\mathcal{M}^{\star}
of
\mathcal{M}
by finitely many imaginary sorts on which imaginary algebraic independence agrees with algebraic independence; moreover, this action gives rise to the same topology
\tau_{\mathrm{pw}}
on
G
and
\overline{G}
; and finally, since non-forking independence satisfies base monotonicity in simple theories, in the action of
G
on
\mathcal{M}^{\star}
algebraic independence satisfies base monotonicity.

8
Minimal semigroup topologies in Urysohn spaces

So far, we obtained results showing that
\tau_{\mathrm{pw}}
is minimal amongst Hausdorff semigroup topologies in several monoids of elementary embeddings. Indeed, various sources of failures of minimality of
\tau_{\mathrm{pw}}
for groups do not work in the context of monoids. In this section, we study semigroup topologies on the monoids of isometric embeddings of Urysohn spaces. These are homogeneous metric spaces which will yield examples of semigroup topologies for which
\tau_{\mathrm{pw}}
is not minimal (amongst Hausdorff semigroup topologies). Indeed, the minimal Hausdorff semigroup topology for these structures naturally arises from the presence of a metric and is known as the
metric topology

\tau_{\mathrm{mp}}
. We will show minimality of
\tau_{\mathrm{mp}}
for the spaces of isometric embeddings of various Urysohn spaces by showing these coincide with the Zariski topology
\tau_{\mathrm{Z}}
. Our techiques can be seen as adapting the ideas of
[
52
]
to a metric context.

The Urysohn universal metric space
\mathcal{U}_{\mathbb R}
, also known as the
real Urysohn space
, constructed by Urysohn in 1925, is a complete separable metric space uniquely characterized by
universality
(every separable metric space embeds isometrically into
\mathcal{U}_{\mathbb R}
) and
homogeneity
(every partial isometry between finite subspaces extends to a global isometry). It was originally constructed by taking the completion of what we now call the rational Urysohn space
\mathcal{U}_{\mathbb{Q}}
. This countable rational metric space, although it can be identified today as the Fraïssé limit of the class of finite metric spaces with rational distances, was obtained by Urysohn through a direct constructive method. The real Urysohn space
\mathcal{U}_{\mathbb{R}}
then arises as its metric completion. There is also a bounded version of the Urysohn space, known as the
real Urysohn sphere

\mathcal{U}_{[0,1]}
, which is universal and homogeneous with respect to the class of finite metric spaces of diameter at most
1
. Again, this can be obtained as the completion of the
rational Urysohn sphere

\mathcal{U}_{\mathcal{Q}_{1}}
, which is the Fraïssé limit of finite metric spaces with rational distances bounded by
1
. It is easy to see that the spaces of isometries
\Aut(\mathcal{U})
and isometric embeddings
I(\mathcal{U})=\overline{\Aut(\mathcal{U})}
of a Urysohn space
\mathcal{U}
are naturally endowed with a metric topology induced by the product topology on
\mathcal{U}^{\mathcal{U}}
, where
\mathcal{U}
is endowed with the topology arising from its metric.

The isometry groups
\mathrm{I}(\mathcal{U}_{\mathbb R})
and
\mathrm{I}(\mathcal{U}_{[0,1]})
, equipped with
\tau_{\mathrm{mp}}
, are Polish groups whose algebraic and dynamical properties have been extensively studied. Notably, every Polish group embeds as a closed subgroup of
\mathrm{I}(\mathcal{U}_{\mathbb R})

[
62
]
, and
\mathrm{I}(\mathcal{U}_{\mathbb R})
is extremely amenable
[
50
]
. The spaces of isometric embeddings have also been studied in a topological context, with, for example,
[
42
]
showing that every transformation monoid on a countable set embeds in the space of isometric embeddings of
\mathcal{U}_{\mathbb{Q}}
.

The model-theoretic construction of
\mathcal{U}_{\mathbb R}
and
\mathcal{U}_{[0,1]}
extends to a broader framework based on so called distance monoids: for any such monoid, the associated class of finite metric structures admits a Fraïssé limit, yielding a universal and homogeneous structure. Details of this setting are provided below and may also be found in
[
21
]
.

The minimality of the
\tau_{\mathrm{mp}}
of
I(\mathcal{U}_{[0,1]})
was established in
[
63
]
by proving the fact that the isometry group of the Urysohn sphere is Roelcke precompact. In
[
33
]
the minimality of
\tau_{\mathrm{mp}}
of
\mathrm{Isom}(\mathcal{U}_{\mathbb R})
and for other generalized Urysohn spaces was obtained through a different approach. In what follows, we study the monoids of isometric embeddings of generalized Urysohn spaces. These are also the monoids of elementary embeddings of these structures. We prove that
\tau_{\mathrm{mp}}=\tau_{\mathrm{Z}}\subsetneq\tau_{\mathrm{pw}}
for the monoids of isometric embeddings of
\mathcal{U}_{\mathbb Q}
,
\mathcal{U}_{\mathbb R}
,
\mathcal{U}_{[0,1]}
, and
\mathcal{U}_{\mathcal{Q}_{1}}
. We will give most proofs in the broader context of generalised Urysohn spaces
[
21
]
.

8.1
Generalized Urysohn spaces

We work within the framework of generalized metric spaces introduced by
[
21
]
. This setting encompasses both the rational numbers and bounded rationals, which are used in the construction of
\mathcal{U}_{\mathbb R}
and
\mathcal{U}_{[0,1]}
.

A
distance monoid

\mathcal{R}=(R,\leqslant,\oplus,0)
is a commutative monoid
(R,\oplus,0)
endowed with a total order
\leqslant
which is compatible with the monoid structure by satisfying the following axioms:

•

(positivity) for all
s,r\in R,\ r\leqslant r\oplus s
;

•

(order) for all
s,s^{\prime},t\in R
, if
s\leqslant s^{\prime}
, then,
s\oplus t\leqslant s^{\prime}\oplus t
.

Definition 8.1
.

A distance monoid
\mathcal{R}
is
metrically complete
if for any
s\leqslant r\in R
, the set
\{t\in R\,|\,r\leqslant t\oplus s\}
is of the form
\{t\,|\,t_{0}\leqslant t\}
for some
t_{0}\in R
, which we denote by
r\ominus s
.

Fact 8.2
.

Suppose that
\mathcal{R}
is a metrically complete distance monoid. For all
r,s,t,u\in R
with
s\leqslant r
, we have

1.

r\ominus s\leqslant t
if and only if
r\leqslant s\oplus t
;

2.

Suppose that
s\leqslant t\oplus u
. Then,
r\leqslant t\oplus u\oplus(r\ominus s)
.

Proof.

The first item is just the definition. For (2), note that by the first item,
r\leqslant s\oplus(r\ominus s)
. Hence, we have

r\leqslant s\oplus(r\ominus s)\leqslant t\oplus u\oplus(r\ominus s)\;.

∎

Definition 8.3
.

Given a distance monoid
\mathcal{R}:=(R,\leqslant,\oplus,0)
. An
\bm{\mathcal{R}}
-metric space

(\mathcal{X},d)
consists of a set
X
together with a map
d:X^{2}\to R
such that for all
x,y,z\in X
:

1.

d(x,y)=0
if and only if
x=y
;

2.

d(x,y)=d(y,x)
;

3.

d(x,z)\leqslant d(x,y)\oplus d(y,z)
.

Definition 8.4
.

Let
(\mathcal{X},d)
and
(\mathcal{Y},d)
be
\mathcal{R}
-metric spaces. An
isometric embedding

is a map
\phi:X\to Y
such that for all
a,b\in X

d(\phi(a),\phi(b))=d(a,b)\;.

If an isometric embedding is a bijection we call it an
isometry
.
We denote by
\mathrm{IE}(\mathcal{X})
be the space of
isometric embeddings
of
\mathcal{X}
into itself and by
\mathrm{I}(\mathcal{X})
its space of isometries (into itself).

It is easy to see that the space of isometric embeddings
\mathrm{IE}(\mathcal{X})
of an
\mathcal{R}
-metric space
\mathcal{X}
equals the space of elementary embeddings of
\mathcal{X}
whenever the first-order theory of
\mathcal{X}
has quantifier elimination in the language
\mathcal{L}_{\mathcal{R}}:=\{D_{r}(x,y)\ |r\in\mathcal{R}\}
where each binary relation
D_{r}(x,y)
is interpreted as the set
\{(a,b)\in X^{2}\ |d(a,b)\leqslant r\}
. Clearly, every elementary embedding in the above language is an isometric embedding. And quantifier elimination ensures that every isometric embedding is indeed elementary. We recall that a first-order theory
\mathcal{T}
has quantifier elimination whenever every first-order formula is equivalent modulo
\mathcal{T}
to a quantifier-free formula.

Definition 8.5
.

Suppose
\mathcal{R}
is a distance monoid. An
\mathcal{R}
-
Urysohn space
is an
\mathcal{R}
-metric space satisfying the following two properties:

•

(universality) any finite
\mathcal{R}
-metric space embeds in
\mathcal{U}
