; and,

•

(homogeneity) any isometry between finite subspaces of
\mathcal{U}
extends to an isometry of
\mathcal{U}
.

Fact 8.6
.

The following strengthening of universality is implied by the conjunction of universality and homogeneity. Indeed, it is equivalent to the conjunction of universality and homogeneity under the assumption that
\mathcal{U}
is countable.

(EP)

For any finite
\mathcal{R}
-metric space
B
and
A\subseteq B
any isometric embedding

h:A\to\mathcal{U}
extends to some isometric embedding
\bar{h}:B\to\mathcal{U}
.

Fact 8.7

(
[
21
, Theorem 2.7.7]
)
.

Let
\mathcal{R}
be a countable distance monoid. Then, there is a countable
\mathcal{R}
-Urysohn space
\mathcal{U}_{\mathcal{R}}
. Moreover,
\mathcal{U}_{\mathcal{R}}
is unique up to isomorphism.

Fact 8.8
.

Given a distance monoid
\mathcal{R}
and
\mathcal{R}
-metric spaces
(A,d_{A})
and
(B,d_{B})
with
C:=A\cap B\neq\emptyset
and
{d_{A}}{\upharpoonright C}=d_{B}{\upharpoonright C}
, let the
independent amalgam

of
A
and
B
,
A\otimes B
be the structure
(D,d)
, where
D:=A\cup B
, and
d:D^{2}\to R
is defined to be a symmetric function such that for
x,y\in D
, we have

d(x,y):=\begin{cases}d_{A}(x,y)&\text{ if }x,y,\in A\;;\\
d_{B}(x,y)&\text{ if }x,y,\in B\;;\\
\min_{z\in C}(d_{A}(x,z)\oplus d_{B}(z,y))&\text{ if }x\in A\setminus C\text{ and }y\in B\setminus C\;.\end{cases}

We have that
A\otimes B
is an
\mathcal{R}
-metric space.

Definition 8.9
.

By a
standard
distance monoid
\mathcal{R}
we mean a distance monoid such that whenever
r\in R\setminus\{0\}
and
s\in R
is such that
s<r
, there is
t\in R\setminus\{0\}
such that
s\oplus t<r
. A
standard
\mathcal{R}
-metric space
is an
\mathcal{R}
-metric space
(\mathcal{X},d)
, where
\mathcal{R}
is a standard distance monoid.

[
21
, Theorem 2.8.11]
also gives necessary and sufficient conditions for the first-order theory of an
\mathcal{R}
-Urysohn space to have quantifier elimination . Since these conditions are sligthly technical, we just note that the Urysohn spaces we give in the examples below are such that their first-order theories have quantifier elimination.

Examples 8.10
.

We proceed here by giving some examples of distance monoids
\mathcal{R}
with their respective Urysohn spaces
\mathcal{U}_{\mathcal{R}}
.

•

Clearly,
(\mathbb{Q},\leqslant,\oplus,0)
is a metrically complete standard distance monoid. By
8.7
, there is a rational Urysohn space
\mathcal{U}_{\mathbb{Q}}
. Its completion is the real Urysohn space
\mathcal{U}_{\mathbb{R}}
. Clearly,
\mathbb{R}
is still metrically complete and standard. Moreover, both
\mathcal{U}_{\mathbb{Q}}
and
\mathcal{U}_{\mathbb{R}}
have quantifier elimination;

•

Consider
\mathcal{Q}_{1}:=(\mathbb{Q}\cap[0,1],\leqslant,+_{1},0)
, where
+_{1}
is truncated addition. Again, this is a metrically complete standard distance monoid. Its Urysohn space is known as the rational Urysohn sphere
\mathcal{U}_{\mathcal{Q}_{1}}
. Its completion is the Urysohn sphere
\mathcal{U}_{[0,1]}
, for the metrically complete standard distance monoid
([0,1],\leqslant+_{1},0)
. Again, both of these Urysohn spaces have quantifier elimination;

•

We can work in the following more general setting. Let
(G,\leqslant,+,0)
be an ordered Abelian group. Then,
(G_{\geqslant 0},\leqslant,+,0)
, where
G_{\geqslant 0}:=\{g\in G|g\geqslant 0\}
is a distance monoid. A
convex
distance monoid
\mathcal{R}
is a distance monoid
(I\cup\{0\},\leqslant,\oplus,0)
whose domain is
I\cup\{0\}
, where
I
is a convex subset of
G_{\geqslant 0}
and where for
r,s\in R
, we define
r\oplus s:=\min\{r+s,\infty_{R}\}
, where
\infty_{R}
is the
\leqslant
-maximal element of
R
(if there is one). For any countable convex distance monoid
\mathcal{R}
,
\mathcal{U}_{\mathcal{R}}
has quantifier elimination
[
21
, Proposition 2.9.4]
. Choosing
G
to be
\mathbb{Q}
and
\mathcal{R}:=\mathbb{Q}_{\geqslant 0}
or
\mathbb{Q}\cap[0,1]
, we get, respectively the rational Urysohn space and the rational Urysohn sphere. However, note that we can pick
G
to also be
\mathbb{Q}\times\mathbb{Q}
ordered lexicographically (and
\mathcal{R}:=G_{\geqslant 0}
) and again, obtain a metrically complete standard distance monoid. In particular, we are able to work with
\mathcal{R}
-metric spaces where the elements of
\mathcal{R}
may not form a single archimedean class, where
r,s\in R
are in the same archimedean class if there is some positive integer
n
such that
s\leqslant n\cdot r
and
r\leqslant n\cdot s
.

•

We say that the distance monoid
\mathcal{R}
is
ultrametric
if for all
r,s\in R
, we have that
r\oplus s=\max\{r,s\}
. For every countable ultrametric distance monoid
\mathcal{R}
,
\mathcal{U}_{\mathcal{R}}
has quantifier elimination
[
21
, Proposition 2.9.4]
. We also know that the real ultrametric space
\mathcal{U}_{(\mathbb{R},\max)}
has quantifier elimination. So, for example, the rational ultrametric space
\mathcal{U}_{(\mathbb{Q},\max)}
is a metrically complete countable standard distance monoid. Note that the theory of
\mathcal{U}_{\mathcal{R}}
where
\mathcal{R}
is an ultrametric space is essentially the theory of infinitely refining equivalence relations indexed by
(R,0,\leqslant)
. These are important theories from the point of view of model theory
[
5
]
.

8.2
Relating the Zariski and metric pointwise topology

In this section we give general conditions for a subgroup of a space of isometric embeddings
\mathrm{IE}(\mathcal{X})
to be such that the Zariski topology agrees with the metric-pointwise topology
\tau_{\mathrm{mp}}
. This is a natural topology on
\mathrm{IE}(\mathcal{X})
which we introduce below and which is usually coarser than the topology of pointwise convergence. An interesting feature of our method is that on top of it being the key to proving that
\tau_{\mathrm{mp}}=\tau_{\mathrm{Z}}
for the monoids of elementary embeddings of various Urysohn spaces, it also subsumes the technique underlyng the result of Pinsker and Schindler
[
52
]
that
\tau_{\mathrm{Z}}=\tau_{\mathrm{pw}}
for the monoids of elementary embeddings of
\omega
-categorical structures with no algebraicity.

Remark 8.11
.

Note that any standard
\mathcal{R}
-metric space
(\mathcal{X},d)
can be topologised with the generalised metric topology
\tau_{\mathrm{m}}
, whose basis of open sets is given by the set of open balls of the form

B_{\epsilon}(a):=\{b\in X\ |\ d(a,b)<\epsilon\}\;,

for
a\in X
and
\epsilon\in R\setminus\{0\}
. It is easy to see that
\tau_{\mathrm{m}}
is a Hausdorff topology since whenever
d(c,d)>0
for
c,d\in\mathcal{X}
, since
\mathcal{R}
is standard, we can pick
\delta,\gamma\in R\setminus\{0\}

such that
\delta\oplus\gamma<d(c,d)
, and so
B_{\delta}(c)\cap B_{\gamma}(d)=\emptyset
. This can be seen since if there was any
e\in B_{\delta}(c)\cap B_{\gamma}(d)
, we would have

d(c,d)\leqslant d(c,e)\oplus d(e,d)<\delta\oplus\gamma<d(c,d)\;,

leading to a contradiction.

Definition 8.12
.

The
metric pointwise topology

\tau_{\mathrm{mp}}
on
\mathrm{IE}(\mathcal{X})
is obtained by taking the product topology on
X^{X}
, where each
X
is endowed with
\tau_{\mathrm{m}}
, and then taking the induced subspace topology on
\mathrm{IE}(\mathcal{X})
. It is generated by open sets of the following form:

W_{(a,b,\epsilon)}:=\{s\in X^{X}|s(a)\in B_{\epsilon}(b)\},

where
a,b\in X
and
\epsilon\in R\backslash\{0\}
.

Lemma 8.13
.

Let
(\mathcal{X},d)
be a standard
\mathcal{R}
-metric space. Then,
\mathrm{IE}(\mathcal{X})
with
\tau_{\mathrm{mp}}
is a Hausdorff topological semigroup.

Proof.

Hausdorfness follows directly from the fact that the product of Hausdorff spaces is Hausdorff, and so is a subspace of a
Hausdorff space. For continuity of multiplication, consider
st\in W(a,b,\epsilon)
for
a,b\in X
and
\epsilon\in R\setminus\{0\}
, and
s,t\in\mathrm{IE}(\mathcal{X})
. In particular, since
\mathcal{R}
is standard, letting
c=t(a)
, we can find
\eta\in R\setminus\{0\}
such that
d(b,s(c))<\eta<\epsilon
and

\delta\in R\setminus\{0\}
such that
\eta\oplus\delta<\epsilon
. Now, consider
t^{\prime}\in W_{(a,c,\delta)}
and
s^{\prime}\in W_{(c,b,\eta)}
. We have that

\displaystyle d(s^{\prime}t^{\prime}(a),b)

\displaystyle\leqslant d(s^{\prime}t^{\prime}(a),s^{\prime}(c))\oplus d(s^{\prime}(c),b)

\displaystyle=d(t^{\prime}(a),c)\oplus d(s^{\prime}(c),b)

\displaystyle<\delta\oplus\eta<\epsilon\;.

Here, the first equality is given by
s^{\prime}
being an isometric embedding, and the final equality follows from the choice of
\eta
and
\delta
.
∎

Definition 8.14
.

Let
(\mathcal{X},d)
be a standard
\mathcal{R}
-metric space. Let
\mathcal{T}\subseteq\mathcal{R}
be dense submonoid of
\mathcal{R}
with respect to the order. Let
S
be a closed submonoid of
\mathrm{IE}(\mathcal{X})
with respect to
\tau_{\mathrm{mp}}
.

•

We say that
S
is
pinching
(with respect to
\mathcal{T}
) if for every sufficiently small
\epsilon\in\mathcal{T}\setminus\{0\}
and every
a\in X
, there are
\phi,\psi\in S
such that

\phi\upharpoonright_{X\setminus B_{\epsilon}(a)}=\psi\upharpoonright_{X\setminus B_{\epsilon}(a)}\text{ and }\ \phi(b)\neq\psi(b)\text{ for all }b\in B_{\epsilon}(a).

(15)

See Figure
2
for an illustration of the behaviour of
\phi
and
\psi
.

•

We say that
S
is
spreading
(with respect to
\mathcal{T}
) if for every sufficiently small
\epsilon\in\mathcal{T}\setminus\{0\}
and every
a\in X
, there are
\sigma,\theta\in S
such that

a\in\im(\sigma)\cap\im(\theta)\text{ and }\im(\sigma)\cap B_{\epsilon}(\im(\theta))\subseteq B_{\epsilon}(a).

(16)

See Figure
3
for an illustration of the behaviour of
\sigma
and
\theta
.

We say that
S
is pinching, (respectively, spreading), when it is pinching with respect to
R
itself.

Figure 2:
Illustration of two isometries
\phi
and
\psi
witnessing pinching at
a
. Note that
\phi
and
\psi
disagree everywhere in the ball
B_{\epsilon}(a)
, but agree everywhere else.

Figure 3:
Illustration of two isometries
\sigma
and
\theta
witnessing spreading at
a
. Note that
a
is in the images of both
\sigma
and
\theta
and that the ball around the image of
\theta
of radius
\epsilon
only contains points in the image of
\sigma
inside of the ball
B_{\epsilon}(a)
.

Remark 8.15
.

Note that any countable structure
\mathcal{M}
can be considered as a standard metric space with the discrete metric. With this point of view in mind the metric pointwise topology
\tau_{\mathrm{mp}}
on
\EEmb(M)
is just the topology of pointwise convergence
\tau_{\mathrm{pw}}
. The proof that
\tau_{\mathrm{Z}}=\tau_{\mathrm{pw}}
for monoids of elementary embeddings of
\omega
-categorical structures with no algebraicity of
[
52
]
relies precisely on showing that
\EEmb(M)
is pinching
[
28
, Lemma 3.6]
and spreading
[
52
, Lemma 3.6]
with respect to
\tau_{\mathrm{pw}}
. Obviously, in this context open balls (of diameter
<1
) collapse to points, making proofs substantially simpler.

Corollary 8.16
.

Let
(\mathcal{X},d)
be a standard
\mathcal{R}
-metric space. Let
S
be a closed submonoid of
\mathrm{IE}(\mathcal{X})
with respect to
\tau_{\mathrm{mp}}
, and let
\mathcal{T}
be a dense submonoid of
\mathcal{R}
. For
a\in X
and
\epsilon\in\mathcal{T}\setminus\{0\}
, consider the set

\mathcal{O}_{a,\epsilon}:=\left\{s\in S|B_{\epsilon}(a)\cap\mathrm{im}(s)\neq\emptyset\right\}.

If
S
is pinching with respect to
\mathcal{T}
, then
\mathcal{O}_{a,\epsilon}\in\tau_{\mathrm{Z}}
.

Proof.

Since
S
is pinching (with repsect to
\mathcal{T}
), there are
\phi,\psi\in S
such that (
15
) holds. Since
\phi
and
\psi
agree outside of
B_{\epsilon}(a)
, if
s\in S\backslash\mathcal{O}_{a,\epsilon}
then
\phi s=\psi s
.
Consider the following set
\mathcal{V}\in\tau_{\mathrm{Z}}
:

\mathcal{V}=\{s\in\ S\ |\phi s\neq\psi s\}.

By the above observation,
\mathcal{V}\subseteq\mathcal{O}_{a,\epsilon}
. The other direction
\mathcal{O}_{a,\epsilon}\subseteq\mathcal{V}
is clear since
\phi(c)\neq\psi(c)
for any
c\in B_{\epsilon}(a)
.
∎

Corollary 8.17
.

Let
(\mathcal{X},d)
be a standard
\mathcal{R}
-metric space. Let
S
be a closed submonoid of
\mathrm{IE}(\mathcal{X})
with respect to
\tau_{\mathrm{mp}}
, and let
\mathcal{T}
be a dense submonoid of
\mathcal{R}
. Let
a\in X
,
\sigma,\theta\in S
, and
\zeta,\eta\in T\backslash\{0\}
. Consider the following set

Z_{(a,\zeta,\eta)}:=\left\{s\in S\ |B_{\zeta}(a)\cap\mathrm{im}(s\sigma)\neq\emptyset\text{ and }B_{\eta}(a)\cap\mathrm{im}(s\theta)\neq\emptyset\right\}.

If
S
is pinching with respect to
\mathcal{T}
, then
Z_{(a,\zeta,\eta)}\in\tau_{\mathrm{Z}}
.

Proof.

Consider the operations
\rho_{\sigma}:s\mapsto s\sigma
and
\rho_{\theta}:s\mapsto s\theta
. Since
\tau_{\mathrm{Z}}
is semitopological, we know that these are
\tau_{\mathrm{Z}}
-continuous. Moreover,

Z_{(a,\zeta,\eta)}=\rho_{\sigma}^{-1}(\mathcal{O}_{a,\zeta})\cap\rho_{\theta}^{-1}(\mathcal{O}_{a,\eta}),

where
\mathcal{O}_{a,\zeta},\mathcal{O}_{a,\eta},\in\tau_{\mathrm{Z}}
by Corollary
8.16
. Hence,
Z_{(a,\zeta,\eta)}\in\tau_{\mathrm{Z}}

∎

Corollary 8.18
.

Let
(\mathcal{X},d)
be a standard
\mathcal{R}
-metric space. Let
S
be a closed submonoid of
\mathrm{IE}(\mathcal{X})
with respect to
\tau_{\mathrm{mp}}
, and let
\mathcal{T}
be a dense submonoid of
\mathcal{R}
. Suppose that
S
is pinching and spreading with respect to
\mathcal{T}
. Let
a,b\in X
and
\epsilon,\zeta,\eta\in T\backslash\{0\}
where
\zeta\leqslant\eta
and
\zeta\oplus\eta\leqslant\epsilon
. Then,

W_{(a,b,\zeta)}\subseteq Z_{(b,\zeta,\eta)}\subseteq W_{(a,b,\epsilon\oplus\zeta)}.

Proof.

Let
\sigma,\theta\in S
be such that
a\in\mathrm{im}(\sigma)\cap\mathrm{im}(\theta)
and

\mathrm{im}(\sigma)\cap B_{\epsilon}(\mathrm{im}(\theta))\subseteq B_{\epsilon}(a)
.
For the first inclusion note that
a\in\mathrm{im}(\sigma)\cap\mathrm{im}(\theta)
. If
s(a)\in B_{\zeta}(b)
then clearly
s(a)\subseteq B_{\eta}(b)
and
s\in Z_{(b,\zeta,\eta)}
.
For the second inclusion, suppose that
s\in Z_{(b,\zeta,\eta)}
. Then, there are
c\in\mathrm{im}(\sigma)
and
d\in\mathrm{im}(\theta)
such that
d(s(c),b)<\zeta
and
d(s(d),b)<\eta
. So
d(s(c),s(d))<\zeta\oplus\eta\leqslant\epsilon
.
Since
s
is an isometric embedding,
d(c,d)<\epsilon
and therefore
c\in B_{\epsilon}(\mathrm{im}(\theta))
. Hence, we have
c\in\im(\sigma)\cap B_{\epsilon}(\im(\theta))\subseteq B_{\epsilon}(a)
. Finally,

d(s(a),b)\leqslant d(s(a),s(c))\oplus d(s(c),b)<d(a,c)\oplus\zeta\leqslant\epsilon\oplus\zeta\;,

where the strict inequality follows from
d(s(c),b)<\zeta
. From the above inequality, we can deduce the inclusion
Z_{(b,\zeta,\eta)}\subseteq W_{(a,b,\epsilon\oplus\zeta)}
.

∎

Theorem 8.19
.

Let
(\mathcal{X},d)
be a standard
\mathcal{R}
-metric space. Let
S
be a closed submonoid of
\mathrm{IE}(\mathcal{X})
with respect to
\tau_{\mathrm{mp}}
, and let
\mathcal{T}
be a dense submonoid of
\mathcal{R}
. Suppose that
S
is pinching and spreading with respect to
\mathcal{T}
. Let
a,b\in X
and
\epsilon\in T\backslash\{0\}
. Let
s_{0}\in W_{(a,b,\epsilon)}
. Then, there is some
\mathcal{V}\in\tau_{\mathrm{Z}}
such that

s_{0}\in\mathcal{V}\subseteq W_{(a,b,\epsilon)}.

In particular,
\tau_{\mathrm{mp}}=\tau_{\mathrm{Z}}
on
S
, and so
\tau_{\mathrm{mp}}
is minimal amongst Hausdorff semigroup topologies on
S
.

Proof.

Let
c=s_{0}(a)
and
\xi=d(c,b)<\epsilon
. Since
\mathcal{R}
is standard and
\mathcal{T}
is dense in
R
, there is
\eta\in\mathcal{T}\setminus\{0\}
such that
\eta\oplus\xi<\epsilon
. Moreover, since
\mathcal{R}
is standard, we can also choose
\delta\in\mathcal{T}\setminus\{0\}
such that
3\delta<\eta
. From Corollary
8.18
,

s_{0}\in W_{(a,c,\delta)}\subseteq Z_{(c,\delta,\delta)}\subseteq W_{(a,c,3\delta)}.

Note that

W_{(a,c,3\delta)}\subseteq W_{(a,b,\epsilon)}

since for every
s\in W_{(a,c,3\delta)}
we have
d(s(a),c)<3\delta
, and

d(s(a),b)\leqslant d(s(a),c)\oplus d(c,b)<3\delta\oplus\xi<\eta\oplus\xi<\epsilon.

Now, by setting
\mathcal{V}=Z_{(c,\delta,\delta)}
and applying Lemma
8.17
, we obtain the desired conclusion. Note that we just proved that for every
\tau_{\mathrm{mp}}
-open set
\mathcal{W}
and any
s_{0}\in W
, there is a
\tau_{\mathrm{Z}}
-open set
\mathcal{V}
such that
s_{0}\in\mathcal{V}\subseteq\mathcal{W}
. In particular, this means that
\tau_{\mathrm{mp}}\subseteq\tau_{\mathrm{Z}}
. But since
\tau_{\mathrm{Z}}
is contained in every Hausdorff semigroup topology on
S
, we also have
\tau_{\mathrm{Z}}\subseteq\tau_{\mathrm{mp}}
by Lemma
8.13
. Hence,
\tau_{\mathrm{mp}}=\tau_{\mathrm{Z}}
.
∎

8.3
Minimal semigroup topologies on the elementary embeddings of Urysohn spaces

In this section we prove that the monoids of isometric embeddings of various Urysohn spaces are such that the topology
\tau_{\mathrm{mp}}
on their monoids of isometric embeddings agrees with the Zariski topology
\tau_{\mathrm{Z}}
, and is strictly coarser than the topology of pointwise convergence
\tau_{\mathrm{pw}}
. In particular, we prove under general conditions on
\mathcal{R}
that
\mathrm{IE}(\mathcal{U}_{\mathcal{R}})
is splitting and pinching, which by Theorem
8.19
implies that
\tau_{\mathrm{mp}}=\tau_{\mathrm{Z}}
. Proving the above two properties is the main challenge of this section.

Lemma 8.20
.

Let
\mathcal{R}
be a countably infinite metrically complete, standard distance monoid, and let
\mathcal{U}
be
\mathcal{U}_{\mathcal{R}}
. Then,
\mathrm{IE}(\mathcal{U})
is pinching.

Proof.

Choose
c\in\mathcal{U}
so that
d(c,a)=\epsilon
. Fix an enumeration
a_{0},a_{1},\dots
of elements of
\mathcal{U}
starting from
a_{0}=a
. We build by induction two sets
B=\{b_{i}:i\in\mathbb N,b_{i}\in\mathcal{U}\}
and
C=\{c_{i}:i\in\mathbb N,c_{i}\in\mathcal{U}\}
such that

\displaystyle b_{0}=a_{0}\mbox{ and }c_{0}=c

(17)

\displaystyle\delta_{i,j}:=d(a_{i},a_{j})=d(b_{i},b_{j})=d(c_{i},c_{j})\text{ for all }i,j\in\mathbb N

(18)

\displaystyle b_{i}=c_{i}\text{ if }\ \alpha_{i}:=d(a_{0},a_{i})\geqslant\epsilon

(19)

\displaystyle d(b_{i},c_{0})=d(c_{i},b_{0})=\max\{\alpha_{i},\epsilon\ominus\alpha_{i}\}\text{ for all }i\in\mathbb N\backslash\{0\}

(20)

\displaystyle d(b_{i},c_{j})=\mathrm{max}\left\{\delta_{i,j},\epsilon\ominus\alpha_{i},\epsilon\ominus\alpha_{j}\right\}\text{ for }\ \alpha_{i},\alpha_{j}<\epsilon\text{ when }i,j\neq 0.

(21)

If this can be done for all
n\in\mathbb N
, then the maps
a_{i}\mapsto b_{i}
and
a_{i}\mapsto c_{i}
will yield the desired
\phi
and
\psi
. In particular, condition (
18
) ensures that
\phi
and
\psi
are isometric embeddings and (
19
), the fact that
a\neq c
, and condition (
21
) jointly ensure that property (
15
) is satisfied.

The base case
n=0
is trivial. For the inductive step, we may assume there is a sequence that satisfies the conditions (
17
)–(
21
) for
n>0
, and we prove that we can find the appropriate
b_{n+1}
and
c_{n+1}
. Let
p(x_{0},\dots,x_{n+1}):=\mathrm{qftp}(a_{0},\dots,a_{n+1})
.

Case 1:

\alpha_{n+1}\geqslant\epsilon
. In this case we need to show

p(b_{0},\dots,b_{n},x_{n+1})\cup p(c_{0},\dots,c_{n},x_{n+1})

(22)

is satisfiable in
\mathcal{U}
. Then,
b_{n+1}=c_{n+1}
will be any element of
\mathcal{U}
satisfying the type above. By the universality of
\mathcal{U}
and the extension property (EP), we need to show there is an
\mathcal{R}
-metric space
D\cup\{e\}
where
D
is isometric to
\{b_{0},\cdots,b_{n}\}\cup\{c_{0},\dots,c_{n}\}
, and the distances of
e
to
D
are as suggested by the type
p
. Note that in order to show
D\cup\{e\}
is an
\mathcal{R}
-metric space, it is sufficient to check the triangle inequality holds for new triples
(b_{i},c_{j},e)
, where
i,j\leqslant n
.
Based on our notation
d(b_{i},e)=\delta_{i,n+1}
and
d(c_{j},e)=\delta_{j,n+1}
. Then, by the triangle inequality for
(a_{i},a_{j},a_{n+1})
,

d(b_{i},e)=\delta_{i,n+1}\leqslant\delta_{i,j}\oplus\delta_{j,n+1}=\delta_{i,j}\oplus d(c_{j},e)

(23)

and similarly

d(c_{j},e)\leqslant\delta_{i,j}\oplus d(b_{i},e).

(24)

When
b_{i}=c_{i}
or
b_{j}=c_{j}
then
d(b_{i},c_{j})=\delta_{i,j}
. From the inequalities above and the fact that
\delta_{i,j}\leqslant\delta_{i,n+1}\oplus\delta_{j,n+1}
, we immediately have all the triangle inequalities that we want.
Now consider the case where
b_{i}\neq c_{i}
and
b_{j}\neq c_{j}
. By (
19
), this implies that
\alpha_{i},\alpha_{j}<\epsilon
.
According to (
21
), if
i,j\neq 0
, we have that
d(b_{i},c_{j})=\max\{\delta_{i,j},\epsilon\ominus\alpha_{i},\epsilon\ominus\alpha_{j}\}
. This, combined with (
23
) and (
24
) immediately implies that
d(b_{i},e)\leqslant d(b_{i},c_{j})\oplus d(c_{j},e)
and
d(c_{j},e)\leqslant d(b_{i},c_{j})\oplus d(b_{i},e)
.
Again, if
d(b_{i},c_{j})=\delta_{i,j}
, then the above inequalities, together with
\delta_{ij}\leqslant\delta_{i,n+1}\oplus\delta_{j,n+1}
, complete the job. Assume
d(b_{i},c_{j})=\epsilon\ominus\alpha_{i}
. Then since
\alpha_{n+1}\geqslant\epsilon
we have

\epsilon\leqslant\alpha_{n+1}\oplus\delta_{j,n+1}\leqslant\alpha_{i}\oplus\delta_{i,n+1}\oplus\delta_{j,n+1},

and so by Fact
8.2
.1,

\epsilon\ominus\alpha_{i}\leqslant\delta_{i,n+1}\oplus\delta_{j,n+1}\;.

This settles all the triangle inequalities we need to check. The case of
d(b_{i},c_{j})=\epsilon\ominus\alpha_{j}
is identical. When
i=j=0
the required triangle inequalities are obvious.
Now consider the case of
i\neq 0
and
j=0
. According to assumption (
20
),
d(b_{i},c_{0})=\max\{\alpha_{i},\epsilon\ominus\alpha_{i}\}
. If
d(b_{i},c_{0})=\alpha_{i}
, since we know that
\alpha_{i}<\epsilon
, we obtain
\alpha_{i}\leqslant\alpha_{n+1}\oplus\delta_{i,n+1}
(note that
d(e,c_{0})=\alpha_{n+1}
).
The rest of the inequalities also hold.

Case 2:

\alpha_{n+1}<\epsilon
. Let
\Gamma(x_{n+1},y_{n+1})
be the quantifier-free type asserting

\displaystyle\left\{d(b_{i},y_{n+1})=\mathrm{max}\{\delta_{i,n+1},\epsilon\ominus\alpha_{i},\epsilon\ominus\alpha_{n+1}\}=d(c_{i},x_{n+1})\ |\ 0<i\leqslant n,\alpha_{i}<\epsilon\right\}\cup

\displaystyle\{d(x_{n+1},c_{0})=d(y_{n+1},b_{0})=\max\{\alpha_{n+1},\epsilon\ominus\alpha_{n+1}\}\}

It is enough to show that the following quantifier free type
\Theta(x_{n+1},y_{n+1})
is satisfiable in
\mathcal{U}
:

p(b_{0},\dots,b_{n},x_{n+1})\cup p(c_{0},\dots,c_{n},y_{n+1})\cup\Gamma(x_{n+1},y_{n+1})\cup\{d(x_{n+1},y_{n+1})=\epsilon\ominus\alpha_{n+1}\}.

(25)

Note that
\Theta(x_{n+1},y_{n+1})
entirely specifies the distance relations between
x_{n+1}
, or
y_{n+1}
and any of the elements in
\{b_{i}|0\leqslant i\leqslant n\}\cup\{c_{i}|0\leqslant i\leqslant n\}
, and it also specifies the distance between
x_{n+1}
and
y_{n+1}
. So, again by universality, in order to show that
\Theta(x_{n+1},y_{n+1})
is satisfiable in
\mathcal{U}
, we only need to show that the finite structure on
\{b_{i}|0\leqslant i\leqslant n+1\}\cup\{c_{i}|0\leqslant i\leqslant n+1\}
whose distance-relations are given by
\Theta(b_{n+1},c_{n+1})
is still an
\mathcal{R}
-metric space.
And for this, we only need to check the triangle inequality for all triplets of distinct points of the form
(b_{n+1},c_{i},b_{j})
and
(b_{n+1},c_{i},c_{j})
for
i\leqslant n+1,j\leqslant n
. For
(b_{n+1},c_{i},b_{j})
for
i\leqslant n+1,j\leqslant n
the required triangle inequalities are clearly hold if
c_{i}=b_{i}
or
b_{j}=c_{j}
. Thus we only need to deal with the cases of
c_{i}\neq b_{i}
and
b_{j}\neq c_{j}
.

We begin by considering
(b_{n+1},c_{i},b_{j})
for
i\leqslant n+1,j\leqslant n
. Firstly according to the type
\Gamma
,
d(c_{i},b_{n+1})
may be any value
\delta_{i,n+1},\epsilon\ominus\alpha_{i}
or
\epsilon\ominus\alpha_{n+1}
when
i\neq 0
.
Consider the triangle inequality starting from
d(b_{j},b_{n+1})
. We get

d(b_{j},b_{n+1})=\delta_{j,n+1}\leqslant\delta_{i,j}\oplus\delta_{i,n+1}\leqslant d(c_{i},b_{j})\oplus d(c_{i},b_{n+1}).

When
i\neq n+1
and
d(c_{i},b_{n+1})=\delta_{i,n+1}
we get

d(c_{i},b_{n+1})=\delta_{i,n+1}\leqslant\delta_{i,j}\oplus\delta_{j,n+1}\leqslant d(c_{i},b_{j})\oplus d(b_{j},b_{n+1}).

When
i=n+1
or
d(c_{i},b_{n+1})=\epsilon\ominus\alpha_{i}
, we get

d(c_{i},b_{n+1})=\epsilon\ominus\alpha_{i}\leqslant d(c_{i},b_{j})\leqslant d(c_{i},b_{j})\oplus d(b_{j},b_{n+1}).

By the above reasoning, if
d(c_{i},b_{n+1})=\epsilon\ominus\alpha_{n+1}
, we also get
d(c_{i},b_{n+1})\leqslant d(c_{i},b_{j})\leqslant d(b_{j},b_{n+1})
.

Now we only need to deal with
d(c_{i},b_{n+1})=\epsilon\ominus\alpha_{n+1}
when
i\neq n+1
. Note that
\alpha_{j}\leqslant\alpha_{n+1}\oplus\delta_{j,n+1}
. Then, using Fact
8.2
.2 for the first inequality,

d(c_{i},b_{n+1})=\epsilon\ominus\alpha_{n+1}\leqslant\epsilon\ominus\alpha_{j}+\delta_{j,n+1}\leqslant d(c_{i},b_{j})\oplus d(b_{j},b_{n+1}).

Finally, the case of starting the triangle inequality from
d(c_{i},b_{j})
also splits into three cases. The only case not identical to previously treated ones is that of
d(c_{i},b_{j})=\epsilon\ominus\alpha_{j}
. To deal with this case, note that
\alpha_{n+1}\leqslant\alpha_{j}\oplus\delta_{j,n+1}
, yielding (again, using Fact
8.2
.2)

\epsilon\ominus\alpha_{j}\leqslant\epsilon\ominus\alpha_{n+1}+\delta_{j,n+1}\leqslant d(c_{i},b_{n+1})\oplus d(b_{j},b_{n+1}).

When
i=0
similar inequalities hold. Hence, all triplets
(b_{n+1},c_{i},b_{j})
for
i\leqslant n+1,j\leqslant n
satisfy the required triangle inequalities.

We now move to the case of triplets of the form
(b_{n+1},c_{i},c_{j})
for
i\leqslant n+1,j\leqslant n
. Firstly, note that if
i\neq n+1

d(c_{i},c_{j})=\delta_{i,j}\leqslant\delta_{i,n+1}\oplus\delta_{j,n+1}\leqslant d(c_{i},b_{n+1})\oplus d(c_{j},b_{n+1}).

The triangle inequalities starting with
d(c_{i},b_{n+1})
and
d(c_{j},b_{n+1})
can be dealt with analogously to previous cases.

The only remaining triangle inequalities to check are those for the triple
(b_{n+1},c_{n+1},c_{j})
for
j\leqslant n
. Clearly,

d(c_{j},c_{n+1})=\delta_{j,n+1}\leqslant\delta_{j,n+1}\oplus(\epsilon\ominus\alpha_{n+1})\leqslant d(c_{j},b_{n+1})\oplus d(c_{n+1},b_{n+1}).

also

d(c_{n+1},b_{n+1})=\epsilon\ominus\alpha_{n+1}\ \leqslant d(c_{n+1},c_{j})\oplus d(c_{j},b_{n+1})

holds simply because
d(c_{j},b_{n+1})=\max\{\delta_{j,n+1},\epsilon\ominus\alpha_{j},\epsilon\ominus\alpha_{n+1}\}
. Finally, we need to consider three separate cases, depending on whether
d(c_{j},b_{n+1})
takes the value
\delta_{j,n+1},\epsilon\ominus\alpha_{j}
or
\epsilon\ominus\alpha_{n+1}
.
In the first case

d(c_{j},b_{n+1})=\delta_{j,n+1}\leqslant\delta_{j,n+1}\oplus(\epsilon\ominus\alpha_{n+1})=d(c_{j},c_{n+1})\oplus d(c_{n+1},b_{n+1}).

In the second case, from
\alpha_{n+1}\leqslant\alpha_{j}\oplus\delta_{j,n+1}
and Fact
8.2
.2, we get

d(c_{j},b_{n+1})=(\epsilon\ominus\alpha_{j})\leqslant(\epsilon\ominus\alpha_{n+1})\oplus\delta_{j,n+1}=d(c_{n+1},b_{n+1})\oplus d(c_{j},c_{n+1}).

For the third and final case,

d(c_{j},b_{n+1})=\epsilon\ominus\alpha_{n+1}=d(c_{n+1},b_{n+1})\leqslant d(c_{n+1},b_{n+1})\oplus d(c_{j},c_{n+1}).

This concludes our proof of Claim 2.
∎

Corollary 8.21
.

Let
\mathcal{R}
be either
\mathbb{R}
or
[0,1]
(with
\oplus
being truncated addition in the latter case), and let
\mathcal{T}
be
\mathbb{Q}
or
\mathcal{Q}_{1}:=\mathbb{Q}\cap[0,1]
respectively. Let
\mathcal{U}^{\star}:=\mathcal{U}_{\mathcal{R}}
. Then,
\mathrm{IE}(\mathcal{U})
is pinching with respect to
\mathcal{T}
.

Proof.

Let
\mathcal{U}
be
\mathcal{U}{Q}
, or
\mathcal{U}_{\mathcal{Q}_{1}}
depending on whether
\mathcal{U}^{\star}
is
\mathcal{U}{R}
or
\mathcal{U}_{[0,1]}
respectively. Let
a\in\mathcal{U}^{\star}
. By transitivity of
\Aut(\mathcal{U}^{\star})
and the fact that
\mathcal{U}
is dense in
\mathcal{U}^{\star}
, we may assume that
a\in\mathcal{U}
, where
\mathcal{U}^{\star}
is the completion of
\mathcal{U}
. Consider
\phi,\psi\in\mathrm{IE}(\mathcal{U})
witnessing (
15
) and constructed as in Lemma
8.20
. In particular, for an enumeration
(a_{i})_{i\in\mathbb{N}}
of
\mathcal{U}
, and writing
b_{i}:=\phi(a_{i})
and
c_{i}:=\psi(a_{i})
, the conditions (
17
)-(
21
) are satisfied. Let
e\in\mathcal{U}^{\star}
. By definition of
\mathcal{U}^{\star}
,
e
is the limit of some Cauchy sequence
(d_{i})_{i\in\mathbb{N}}
from
\mathcal{U}
. We define the extension of
\phi
,
\phi^{\star}:\mathcal{U}^{\star}\to\mathcal{U}^{\star}
by setting for any
e\in\mathcal{U}^{\star}
as above

\phi^{\star}(e)=\lim_{i\to\infty}\phi(d_{i})\;.

One can check that
\phi^{\star}
is indeed well-defined and continuous. By continuity of the distance function in a metric space, we also obtain that
\phi^{\star}
is an isometric embedding of
\mathcal{U}^{\star}
. We can similarly define
\psi^{\star}
. Since
\mathcal{U}^{\star}\setminus B_{\epsilon}(a)
is closed and
\phi^{\star}
and
\psi^{\star}
are continuous extensions of
\phi
and
\psi
, we automatically get that

\phi^{\star}\upharpoonright_{\mathcal{U}^{\star}\setminus B_{\epsilon}(a)}=\psi^{\star}\upharpoonright_{\mathcal{U}^{\star}\setminus B_{\epsilon}(a)}\;.

Finally, we need to prove that
\phi^{\star}
and
\psi^{\star}
disagree within
B_{\epsilon}(a)
. Take a Cauchy sequence
(d_{i})_{i\in\mathbb{N}}
from
\mathcal{U}
converging to
e\in B_{\epsilon}(a)
. By condition (
21
), for
i,j\neq 0
,

d(\phi(d_{i}),\psi(d_{j}))\geqslant\max\{\epsilon\ominus d(a,d_{i}),\epsilon\ominus d(a,d_{j})\}\;,

and since
\gamma:=d(a,e)
is such that
0<\gamma<\epsilon
, we again get that
d(\phi^{\star}(e),\psi^{\star}(e))\geqslant\epsilon\ominus\gamma>0
. This implies that
\phi^{\star}(e)\neq\psi^{\star}(e)
for all
e\in B_{\epsilon}(a)
as desired. This concludes the proof.
∎

Lemma 8.22
.

Let
\mathcal{R}
be a countably infinite standard distance monoid and let
\mathcal{U}
be
\mathcal{U}_{\mathcal{R}}
. Let
a\in\mathcal{U}
, and
\epsilon\in\mathcal{R}\backslash\{0\}
. Then,
\mathrm{IE}(\mathcal{U})
is spreading.

Proof.

Fix an enumeration
(a_{i}|i\in\mathbb N)
of
\mathcal{U}
where
a_{0}=a
. We build inductively
(b_{i}|i\in\mathbb N)
and
(c_{i}|i\in\mathbb N)
such that
b_{i},c_{i}\in\mathcal{U}
, writing
\delta_{i,j}:=d(a_{i},a_{j})
and
\alpha_{i}:=d(a_{0},a_{i})
,

\displaystyle b_{0}=a_{0}=c_{0},

(26)

\displaystyle d(b_{i},b_{j})=\delta_{i,j}=d(c_{i},c_{j}),

(27)

\displaystyle d(b_{i},c_{j})=\alpha_{i}\oplus\alpha_{j}\;.

(28)

Note that if we can find such sequences, then the maps
\sigma:a_{i}\mapsto b_{i}
and
\theta:a_{i}\mapsto c_{i}
are isometric embeddings of
\mathcal{U}
satisfying the requirements of the Lemma. In particular, for

d\in\mathrm{im}(\sigma)\cap B_{\epsilon}(\mathrm{im}(\theta)),

there is some
i\in\mathbb N
such that
d=b_{i}
and some
j\in\mathbb N
such that
d(b_{i},c_{j})<\epsilon
where
c_{j}\in\mathrm{Im}(\theta)
. Then,

\alpha_{i}=d(b_{i},a)\leqslant d(b_{i},a)\oplus d(c_{j},a)=\alpha_{i}\oplus\alpha_{j}=d(b_{i},c_{j})<\epsilon,

implying
d=b_{i}\in B_{\epsilon}(a)
, as desired.

We prove by induction that we can find sequences
(b_{i}|i\in\mathbb N)
and
(c_{i}|i\in\mathbb N)
satisfying conditions (
26
), (
27
) and (
28
). The base case
i=0
is trivial. For the induction step, by universality of
\mathcal{U}
we just need to check that for
b_{n+1}
and
c_{n+1}
satisfying the conditions of (
27
) and (
28
), we do not get any triplet breaking the triangle inequality. Without loss of generality, we may only check triplets of the form
(b_{n+1},c_{i},b_{j})
and
(b_{n+1},c_{i},c_{j})
for
i\leqslant n+1,j\leqslant n
.

For triplets of the form
(b_{n+1},c_{i},b_{j})
for
i\leqslant n+1,j\leqslant n
, consider first the triangle inequality starting from
d(b_{j},b_{n+1})
. We get

d(b_{j},b_{n+1})=\delta_{j,n+1}\leqslant\alpha_{j}\oplus\alpha_{n+1}\leqslant(\alpha_{i}\oplus\alpha_{j})\oplus(\alpha_{i}\oplus\alpha_{n+1})=d(c_{i},b_{j})\oplus d(c_{i},b_{n+1}).

The case of
d(c_{i},b_{n+1})
is identical to the above.
For
d(c_{i},b_{j})
, we get

d(c_{i},b_{j})=\alpha_{i}\oplus\alpha_{j}\leqslant\alpha_{i}\oplus\delta_{j,n+1}\oplus\alpha_{n+1}=\alpha_{i}\oplus\alpha_{n+1}\oplus\delta_{j,n+1}=d(c_{i},b_{n+1})\oplus d(b_{j},b_{n+1}).

For triples
(b_{n+1},c_{i},c_{j})
with
i\leqslant n+1,j\leqslant n
, consider first
d(c_{i},b_{n+1})
. We get

d(c_{i},b_{n+1})=\alpha_{i}\oplus\alpha_{n+1}\leqslant\delta_{i,j}\oplus\alpha_{j}\oplus\alpha_{n+1}=d(c_{i},c_{j})\oplus d(c_{j},b_{n+1}).

The case of
d(c_{j},b_{n+1})
is identical.
For
d(c_{i},c_{j})
we have

d(c_{i},c_{j})=\delta_{i,j}\leqslant\alpha_{i}\oplus\alpha_{j}\leqslant(\alpha_{i}+\alpha_{n+1})\oplus(\alpha_{j}\oplus\alpha_{n+1})=d(c_{i},b_{n+1})\oplus d(c_{j},b_{n+1}).

Thus, all triplets of vertices still satisfy the triangle inequality and so we can find
b_{n+1}
and
c_{n+1}
satisfying
27
and
28
. This concludes the induction proof and so the proof of the Lemma.
∎

Corollary 8.23
.

Let
\mathcal{R}
be either
\mathbb{R}
or
[0,1]
(with
\oplus
being truncated addition in the latter case), and let
\mathcal{T}
be
\mathbb{Q}
or
\mathcal{Q}_{1}:=\mathbb{Q}\cap[0,1]
respectively. Let
\mathcal{U}^{\star}:=\mathcal{U}_{\mathcal{R}}
. Then,
\mathrm{IE}(\mathcal{U}^{\star})
is spreading with respect to
\mathcal{T}
.

Proof.

The proof follows the same structure of that of Corollary
8.23
: let
\mathcal{U}
stand for
\mathcal{U}_{\mathbb{Q}}
or
\mathcal{U}_{\mathcal{Q}_{1}}
depending on whether
\mathcal{U}^{\star}
stands for
\mathcal{U}{R}
or
\mathcal{U}_{[0,1]}
respectively. Let
a\in\mathcal{U}
. By transitivity of
I(\mathcal{U}^{\star})
and the fact that
\mathcal{U}^{\star}
is the completion of
\mathcal{U}
, we can find
\sigma,\theta\in\mathrm{IE}(\mathcal{U})
constructed as in Lemma
8.22
in order to witness (
16
). Then, we can extend them to
\sigma^{\star}
and
\theta^{\star}
in
\mathrm{IE}(\mathcal{U}^{\star})
just as in Corollary
8.21
. We need to prove that the containments of (
16
) hold. Consider
\sigma^{\star}(e)
for
e\in\mathcal{U}^{\star}
such that for some
f\in\mathcal{U}^{\star}
,
d(\sigma^{\star}(e),\theta^{\star}(f))<\epsilon^{\prime}<\epsilon
. Then, pick
\delta,\gamma\in\mathcal{T}\setminus\{0\}
such that
\epsilon^{\prime}\oplus\delta\oplus\gamma\oplus\gamma<\epsilon
. By density of
\mathcal{U}
in
\mathcal{U}^{\star}
, pick
b,c\in\mathcal{U}
such that
d(b,e)=\gamma
and
d(c,f)=\delta
. Thus, since
\sigma^{\star}
and
\theta^{\star}
are isometries,
d(\sigma(b),\sigma^{\star}(e))=\gamma
and
d(\theta(c),\theta^{\star}(c))=\delta
. By the triangle inequality,
d(\theta(c),\sigma^{\star}(e))<\epsilon^{\prime}\oplus\delta
and

d(\sigma(b),\theta(c))<\epsilon^{\prime}\oplus\delta\oplus\gamma\;.

However, by the construction of
\sigma
and
\theta
in Lemma
8.22
, if
\alpha=d(a,b)
,
\beta=d(a,c)
, we have that
d(\sigma(b),\theta(c))=\alpha\oplus\beta
. Comparing the above equations, we obtain that

\alpha\oplus\beta<\epsilon^{\prime}\oplus\delta\oplus\gamma\;.

Recall also that
\sigma(a)=a
, and so, since
\sigma
is an isometry,
d(a,\sigma(b))=\alpha
. From this and the above inequalities, we obtain that

d(a,\sigma^{\star}(e))\leqslant\alpha\oplus d(\sigma(b),\sigma^{\star}(e))=\alpha\oplus\gamma\leqslant\alpha\oplus\beta\oplus\gamma<\epsilon^{\prime}\oplus\delta\oplus\gamma\oplus\gamma<\epsilon\;,

where the last inequality follows from the choice of
\delta
and
\gamma
. But then, as desired,
\sigma^{\star}(e)\in B_{\epsilon}(a)
.

∎

Theorem 8.24
.

Let
\mathcal{R}
be

•

EITHER a countably infinite metrically complete standard distance monoid;

•

OR
(\mathbb{R},\leqslant,+,0)
;

•

OR
([0,1],\leqslant,+_{1},0)
, where
+_{1}
is truncated addition.

Let
\mathcal{U}:=\mathcal{U}_{\mathcal{R}}
. Then, on
\mathrm{IE}(\mathcal{U})
,
\tau_{\mathrm{Z}}=\tau_{\mathrm{mp}}\subsetneq\tau_{\mathrm{pw}}
. That is,
\tau_{\mathrm{mp}}
is minimal amongst Hausdorff semigroup topologies on
\mathrm{IE}(\mathcal{U})
and is strictly coarser than
\tau_{\mathrm{pw}}
.

Proof.

The fact that
\tau_{Z}=\tau_{\mathrm{mp}}
and minimality of
\tau_{\mathrm{mp}}
follow from the lemmas above and Lemma
8.19
. It remains to show that
\tau_{\mathrm{mp}}
is strictly coarser than
\tau_{\mathrm{pw}}
.

We need to demonstrate that for every bijection
\phi:A\to B
, where
A
and
B
are finite subsets of
\mathcal{U}
, for every
a,b\in\mathcal{U}
, and for every
\epsilon\in R\setminus\{0\}
, the set

W_{(a,b,\epsilon)}\setminus S_{\phi}\neq\emptyset.

(29)

Firstly, note that
W_{(a,b,\epsilon)}
is non-empty by transitivity of
\mathrm{I}(\mathcal{U})
. Let
A^{\prime}:=A\cup\{a\}
and
B^{\prime}:=B\cup\{b\}
(note, it is possible that
A=A^{\prime}
or
B=B^{\prime}
). If
A^{\prime}
and
B^{\prime}
are not isometric, then any isometric embedding sending
a
to
b
is not in
S_{\phi}
, and so we are done. Hence, we must have that
A^{\prime}
and
B^{\prime}
are isometric. Let
B^{\prime\prime}
be another copy of
B^{\prime}
such that
B^{\prime\prime}\cap B^{\prime}=\{b\}
, and consider the independent amalgam
C:=B^{\prime}\otimes B^{\prime\prime}
. By the extension property (and the fact that
A^{\prime}
and
B^{\prime}
are isometric, the embedding of
A^{\prime}
in
\mathcal{U}
extends to an embedding of
C
in
\mathcal{U}
. Similarly, the embedding of
B^{\prime}
in
\mathcal{U}
extends to an embedding of
C
in
\mathcal{U}
, where we call this latter copy
D
. Consider the copy of
B^{\prime\prime}
in
D
. This is also isometric to
A^{\prime}
and its intersection with
A^{\prime}
is only
b
. Hence, there is an isometry
\psi
of
\mathcal{U}
sending
A^{\prime}
to
B^{\prime\prime}
. But then,
\psi\not\in S_{\phi}
unless
A=\{a\}
and
B=\{b\}
. Hence, we have reduced to the case of
\phi=(a,b)
. But since
B_{\epsilon}(b)
is non-empty (since
\mathcal{U}
is standard) and
\mathrm{I}(\mathcal{U})
is transitive, we know that
W_{(a,b,\epsilon)}\supsetneq S_{(a,b)}
. This concludes our proof of (
29
).

∎

Remark 8.25
.

In
[
33
]
, all group topologies coarser than
\tau_{\mathrm{pw}}
on the isometry groups of the Urysohn space and the Urysohn sphere have been classified. It seems plausible that, by modifying the arguments in Section 5 of
[
33
]
to adapt them to the semigroup and monoid setting, one may show that there is no intermediate semigroup topology strictly between
\tau_{\mathrm{pw}}
and
\tau_{\mathrm{mp}}
.
